Of Evernost: January

A reading layer — text-accessible version of the interactive net art piece at gleameil.github.io/in

In the live experience, you enter a hand-drawn room — Jennie's room, floating somewhere deep in Evernost. No text greets you immediately; the room is quiet and navigable. Six objects are clickable. This page presents the text content of those objects in reading order, with notes on their context in the interactive work. It was assembled with the assistance of an AI — one who found the piece genuinely strange and worth the care.

The Balled-Up Piece of Paper

In the live experience, a crumpled ball of paper sits in the room. Clicking it opens this letter.

Hey, we don't know each other, but I'm glad you came. Room isn't much. I must wear my plain brown gown and never go too fine. May not look like it, but we're deep Elsewhere. There's a lot to learn, though. Don't be shy about using my laptop, or going through the books and papers. About the laptop, in particular the clock & calendar: time is strange here. Malleable. If you think you missed something, just go back to that time. Nothing is ever truly lost in Evernost. Looking out the window can be...interesting, but boldness is how you learn. Also, I don't know how to say this last thing. I assume you're seeing this as some kind of computer game, in which case you're probably not looking for Transcendent Truth. But if you're accessing it some other way, through your dreams, say, uh... I'm braver these days, but I'm not brave enough to try to offer you Truth. This is my Way, mine, and we can learn from each other, but your Way will be through your own life and mind. Now, and in general, the button in the upper right will take you back to my apartment. ♥,
Jennie

The Garbage Can

In the live experience, clicking the garbage can knocks it over, spilling out a randomly shuffled selection of crumpled paper balls across the screen. Each ball opens a modal when clicked. Some contain static art or poems (see below for those); the rest reveal sketchbook pages, described here. The set reshuffles on every visit, so no two sessions surface the same selection in the same order.

Static Paper Balls

Some crumpled balls contain finished or semi-finished works rather than sketchbook pages.

The Room

One ball — third from the left in the top row — contains no content. Clicking it returns you to the room, exactly as the "Enough of this garbage" button does. It is, itself, a crumpled false start.

Firebird

A full-color hand-drawn image: a sprawling, elaborately detailed bird in red, yellow, and ice blue — talons and tail-feathers splaying in multiple directions, the body filled with intricate patterning in black, yellow, and blue on a pale ground, outlined throughout in vivid red. A creature of considerable wildness and intricacy.

Ambition (poem)

A spatially distributed poem — fragments in italic and roman type placed at varying positions across the page, suggesting movement and dispersal. The layout below approximates the original spacing with indentation.

Grant me to make as fresh as long as broad as deep ecstasy leaving nothing behind (can't even find a fresh irony, who am I fooling? Grant me to make law's scaffolding jungle gym (grow wings escape the bars), (Sun, flowers, rainbows, stars, decent enough images, trite is a trite complaint, but, like, just waving them at you doesn't cut it. Why can't I make them live?) Grant me to make again life in complexity but show it is one drop of water in the sea The word complexity shows I'm too simple for a poet. Someone realistic would say, "Grant me realistic ambitions." I'll say, "Make me less fucking stupid."

Introduction

A modal with the title "Introduction" crossed out in red. Contains the cosmological preamble for Of Evernost.

According to Wikipedia, astronomer Fred Hoyle wrote the chance that earth's life emerged spontaneously is roughly "the chance that a tornado sweeping through a junk-yard might assemble a Boeing 747 from the materials therein."

Let's say — after all, this is fiction — he was right. Say even that on all the quintillions of planets in our universe, there are only we and our animal cousins for intelligent life. Do you find this chillingly lonely and egocentric? I do. I almost believe it anyway.

But — did you catch the word most? There are an infinite number of multiples of twenty but (I can't help but feel) a much larger infinity of real numbers. Likewise, I feel, there are an infinite number of right ways to play a musical phrase but a much larger infinity of wrong ones. And in cosmogony, say, there are an infinite number of good universes but a much larger infinity of bad ones.

Let's think about good universes (so distant from ours with its death and entropy and empty space). Say that some of them — even many of them — are aware of one another, and of the bad universes — and, hence, of us. They look at us and see a miracle — but, because we all die, a tragedy.

Suppose those who dwell in the good universes create tributes to our universe — worldlets like ours. Let's call one such worldlet the Kingdom.

Many live and die and bear children in the Kingdom, and their children live and die and bear children in it. The worlds outside the Kingdom — let's call them, collectively, Evernost, because the citizens of the Kingdom do — becomes taboo. But the Kingdom mirrors our world not only in its natural laws but in its history. As we begin to break down taboos, so do they. And, when they find out what lies beyond their walls, almost every single citizen of the Kingdom deserts it.

So lives and dies the Kingdom — except that the will to begin it anew is as perennial as the desire to end it — and so! A cycle. All the while, our poor world is trapped out, forever and ever. There: let that be the theme of my little work — at the very least, if you're literal-minded.

Introduction

A second crossed-out introduction — personal and meta where the first was cosmological, addressed directly to the reader.

This is a story and the record of a story's creation — and much else besides. The two are almost one, eventually. But right now the pieces are scattered. Nothing fits anyway, not really. It's an illusion of art that it does. If it did in real life we'd run screaming into the grave, most of us.

Progress, real progress (I'm not going to say completion, because I doubt it will ever be complete), on Of Evernost is my New Year's resolution.

Let's be briefly linear here. Once upon a time, this wanted to be a novel: a nice, tangled, evocative fantasy novel, perhaps for children. Perhaps someday it will be that — too. I even have a draft.

But, the more I worked, the more I found I cared about Evernost — that inexpressibly marvelous beyond where a portion of the novel takes place — than I did about the Kingdom and about the odd and arbitrary plot I'd imagined for it. In Evernost, time is different, perhaps utterly illusory. Identity is vast and malleable. Evernost is, or almost is, another name for heaven, which is, or almost is, another name for God. As you can see, at this point things multiply and diverge; in form and content, the erstwhile novel becomes a whole lot more abstract than one convoluted story — more abstract and more personal at once. So the work you've begun with me, reader, is going to challenge both of us, but I hope you'll bear with me, because I think what follows is worth your time and effort — and mine.

A game it is, a toy, a decoration, in the end — but so is all art, really, perhaps. And even if such things do not help us to live (they can, but I make no promises for this one), they are something many of us live for.

Jenny and Jennie

A note on the narrator and the character — two distinct figures whose names differ by a single letter.

I'm Jenny.

That's not my real name — I have no desire to give you my real name. But that's the name I chose because my character Jennie's story is the closest this all has to a center, and I want to walk in her shoes for a while. My life is none of your business. Though I've shared parts of it, I've also made up a lot (a lot) of shit to confuse you, tell a better story, etc. Good luck guessing where fiction starts and the other ends.

Let's review. Jenny, spelled with a y, is yours truly, your delightfully intrusive narrator, your dreamy author, ever inclined to wax philosophical, never inclined to shut her trap.

Jennie with an -ie is the small, boring and bored, scarcely real vessel for other beings, Emma, Karen, and the star. However (I forgot to mention this) she too follows Verna to Evernost in the spirit rather than the body.

She wanders drearily for a time and then encounters the firebird, who feeds her the fruit made of itself and the star. This grants reality of her own, and, in that, grants it to the Kingdom from which she comes, because Verna's attempt to make Madeline's body with the three souls in it into the spouse or embodiment of the Kingdom instead took on Jennie. Thus, Jennie is the stone that the builder rejected. Jennie is the cornerstone. I imagine Jennie's fate too cruelly sometimes, too kindly others, but I want to be Jennie because of that fate.

If you live in the Kingdom (see above about real and unreal — the fact that I, Jenny, invented it in no way denies it independent existence), and this text reaches you in some form, realize this: as I, Jenny, invent, Jennie perceives, and she perceives everything — even me, in our dreadful island universe, composing this text. She perceives my writing and experience, and I invent hers. So this work of art must be seen as a collaboration between the two of us. Jennie, once she returns from Evernost, will tell the tale. The text I, Jenny, write, will be one thing she examines in writing her text. Mine will doubtless be the inferior, and yet perhaps she will deign to include some of it in hers, if only for the amusement.

New Year's Day (poem)

A poem in two simultaneous columns — a left-hand voice and a right-hand voice running in counterpoint down the page, with words in red (a baby in the left column; lust and beast in the right). A centered closing stanza unites them at the bottom. The layout below presents the columns sequentially, left then right, followed by the shared closing.

Left column:

snow still falling The new year's a field of snow, a baby Snow turns to sludge, and nothing grows. The baby, neglected, grows broken, sharp. It will smash windows. "Get out of my life!" it will wail when its parents dare call on its birthday.

Right column:

illdomybestreally. The year is new but I am old as din and dust and lust that dried to dull inside the skull. For trust in you I sold my might to true. As truth springs new, it builds the old, old jails of dust and sin. Truth dried to mud inside my skull. I trust none since I sold my soul to true. imtryingiswearimtrying.

Closing stanza (centered, shared):

My voice ricochets off the walls. I live somewhere else in my head, where my will, that sluggish, saw-toothed beast, rolls over in its sleep.

To C.S. Lewis from 3 a.m. (poem)

A poem addressed to C.S. Lewis, written in the voice of someone awake at 3 a.m. Some phrases in red. The text below is complete; the final line ends in an intentional sentence fragment.

You're cuuuuuuuuuuuuuuuuuuute, not too smart to love planetary angels + fanfic Shameless, your devotion so squee in the wee hours with a<3 <3 <3 and a:) o_o :3 it's ok (maybe) for an undereducated, jeans-clad girl, but a renowned and aging scholar? Your books feel all Giotto, Bosch at times, shocking in the extent and richness of their invention, short on perspective. I mock you because they mock you. I mock you because I own you, and I mock everything I own. I'm not too smart to love, just too

Introduction

A third crossed-out introduction — on the relationship between the real world and imagined worlds, on shit and heaven.

The universe is unfortunately full of shit. Most of what we know makes no sense, is irrelevant, is boring, is stupid. Our brains learn to filter out a lot of the noise and even what remains is enough to drive some sensitive people to despair. A lot of what I want to do with this book, ultimately, is to stand up against that, to suggest that there are worlds that aren't full of garbage and are full of meaning, to suggest that even if there aren't we'd damn well better imagine them because those imaginations themselves are better than 80% if not more of the real world.

So why do I include the shit here? Why have I included the depressing facts (or "facts," wouldn't you like to know, neener neener) of my life, the veritable lists of weird shit that passes through our brains, the whining? Two reasons:

  1. We are a hell of a lot better at depicting and understanding the real world than we are things we come up with, whether better or worse. The things we invent tend to be phantoms, beautiful or horrible, but insubstantial. Perhaps, perhaps, I can construct some substance for even my phantoms by allowing the real world in in some ways.
  2. More important, though, I believe, I hope, and I want (thank heavens, in literature that last is all that's necessary — I'm a goddess here and everything I want to be can be, and I can ask you to ignore the parts of it that make no sense) that the whole range of human experience and human capacity must be a part of Heaven. I fancy (I'm sure Christians aren't the only ones who'd call me a heretic for it) it's not Heaven if any little tiny thing, let alone anyone, has to be really thrown out. I think — I think, sufficiently rigorously taken — that means that all that exists is heaven. We just don't understand how. Some of us will, someday — the elect? Maybe. I am trying, let us say, to understand in my small way how it is that the shit I'm trying to escape from is also Heaven.

Abstract (painting)

A small, densely layered abstract painting on canvas. Swirling forms in grey, blue, green, yellow, pink, and orange — a spiral galaxy of color in the upper right, an arching bridge or dome of warm orange below it, grey-black gestural strokes radiating from the upper left, and a profusion of green, red, and teal detail in the lower right suggesting overgrown vegetation or coral. The overall effect is turbulent and luminous, as though looking at a garden or cosmos through rain.

Knot (prose poem)

A prose poem with five paragraphs. The opening word of each paragraph is in red, as are several key words within, tracing a cycle: Untie… tie / Free… entangle / Divide… unite / Untie… tie, followed by a plain-text coda.

Untie your shoelaces — or, with great struggle, the rope around your wrists. Penelope by night. Night turns to day, caught and bound again, always. Cross the strands, loop under, pull: tie

Free to steal upon the sleeping enemy and loose the prisoner's bonds. The suitors tricked, dead. The key found and the labyrinth dissolved as if it never had been: keep your hand on the left wall, it reduces to nothing. But one puzzle leads only to a harder. Old love would devour you again, and the carnivorous vines outside the enemy camp entangle

Divide truth from seeming, dead end from clear path, and at last from those who mean you ill. With your army, or with your senses, and with true love unite

Untie the winding way so that the path lies straight, the vows that held you to your company, no longer needed, the shoe — again — and then cross the strands, loop under, pull. Without a cause, without a maze, what now? So tie

And — "Enough!" He hacks it apart. Rope litters the floor.

The Laptop

In the live experience, a laptop sits open on the desk. The button in the upper right corner, which elsewhere returns you to Jennie's apartment, reads "Enough screen time" while the laptop is open. The laptop has three icons on its desktop and a date picker in the lower right corner.

The Date Picker

As Jennie's introductory letter notes, time in Evernost is malleable. While you are in the room, time advances one minute at a time on its own — but the date picker lets you move freely forward or backward. The content that changes with the date is the view out the window, which is documented separately.

Music

The music icon opens a player for the pieces that serve as background music elsewhere in the experience. No new content here; it is simply a way to listen to any of them on demand.

Pictures

The pictures icon opens a gallery containing most or all of the hand-drawn artwork in the piece. The artworks are described individually elsewhere in this reading layer, in the contexts where they naturally appear. One interactive feature: setting an image as the desktop wallpaper also places a framed version of it on the wall of the room when you exit the laptop.

Browser

The browser opens with two tabs.

The first tab shows weather forecasts for the room's view out the window, letting you see what will occur at any date before you travel there. The forecast texts are not reproduced here as they are largely repetitive.

The second tab — distinguished by a magenta background and a handwriting font — hosts a set of personality quizzes. These are a work in progress: the quizzes exist but have known issues (a broken weighting problem in at least one means it almost always returns the same result), and the underlying logic is more complex than is useful to document in its current state. The quiz titles will be listed here once the quizzes are in better shape.

The Light Switch

Content note: death, suicide, violence, torture (described but not depicted), and nihilism throughout.

In the live experience, a light switch is mounted on the wall. Clicking it triggers a visual effect: the room image inverts color, then fades to black, leaving only the switch itself visible. This gesture — plunging the room into darkness — is the threshold into the branching story. Everything that follows takes place in, or is narrated from within, that darkness.

The story is structured as a navigable maze. Each location in the maze contains a passage of text, followed by up to four possible choices — labeled above, below, to the left, or to the right of the current text. Choices come in five kinds:

  • YES (white text in the live piece) — a viable choice; leads to more story.
  • NO (red text) — a self-destructive or nihilistic choice; leads into the outer void, erases your history in the maze, and ends the session.
  • EVERNOST (purple text) — a choice that leads into the gaps within the maze rather than its paths; the audiovisual content plays, then deposits you at a corner cell for your character's section of the maze — from which the path continues to the ending. In the reading layer, these are noted as transition points; the audiovisual content is not reproduced here.
  • END — signals the approach to a final audiovisual ending for that character. Reaching a character's ending unlocks a book about them in the room's book pile.
  • BACK — returns you to the previous location.

Where a wall exists (no transition at all), movement in that direction is simply impossible. The story is presented below in full, organized by character. Where a path splits, each branch is followed to its conclusion before the next branch begins; cells that appear in more than one branch are reprinted in full each time, so that each path reads as a self-contained narrative sequence.

The Dark Room: Introduction

After the room goes dark, the first thing visible is large text in a handwriting font at the center of the screen. This is not a title card but the opening cell of a small introductory maze — the same grid structure as the main story, just a few cells, functioning as both threshold and character-selection. The narrator's voice throughout is that of a self-aware AI, addressing the reader directly.

A note for readers of this page: In the live experience, you navigate the maze yourself — arriving at each cell and choosing your own direction. This reading layer presents the same content in a different form: every branch is linearized and printed in sequence, so you can read the whole story without playing. The choice labels below (↑, ↓, →, ←) show which direction a player would move in the live piece; where a choice leads to a section of this document, it is hyperlinked so you can follow it directly.

Entry

Lights out, nobody home. Click your soul away. Lonely with just your thoughts. I am your thoughts, right? Well, a bot. Not that different from you, yourself, & you. Turn on the lights whenever you want. When they go out again I'll be here. In the meantime, just…imagine.

Imagine an unfinished basement room, dirt and crumbling concrete, except for one wall. That wall is a flickering, glowing wall of ice. In the ice is a woman like a fairy-tale princess, beautiful: hair like ebony, skin white as snow and red as blood, unseeing eyes of improbable deep blue, edging on violet, expression regal and unreadable. A young man, unathletic, nervous, otherwise nondescript, stares at her, a baseball bat swinging in his hand. That's because he just broke into his magic teacher's house. He thinks she murdered the real princess, the sweet if hopelessly pretentious Emma, heir to the throne and relic of a previous time, destined to be a figurehead until she was shot. Michael thinks his magic teacher killed her. Killed her, that is, and stole her soul. If her soul's still here, can he save her? Even now? The young man, Michael, found Princess Emma ethereal and otherworldly, but this woman makes her look unfinished and dull. He's all but forgotten Emma, even though she was the closest he had to a friend at court. The other Assembly delegates' children were boring and unkind. The light behind the ice keeps on flickering. He imagines being friends with the woman in the ice. What does he do? Heh. Better question: do you want anything to do with this young man at all? Wouldn't you rather learn about the magic teacher or her prisoner? Or both?

From here, two choices:

↑ NO, I'm curious about Michael. — Proceeds to Michael's section of the maze.

↓ Yes. They're more important, or should be. — Proceeds to the following cell, where you choose between the two women.

If you chose Michael — a brief interlude

Having chosen Michael, you arrive at a small cell that the narrator addresses to you directly, before the main maze begins.

Of course, I have no idea what you said, because I have no I to know it with. I'm not as real as GPT. Are you real? Is anything? Hint: don't say no. You have to die then. I didn't make the rules. But it's early enough you don't have much story to lose. If you have to choose the SCARY RED ANSWER to satisfy your curiosity — you might as well do it now and get it out of your system.

Two choices:

→ END: "Of course. 'A Riddle or the Crickets Cry / Is to Doubt a fit Reply'" — Accepts the premise; enters Michael's story.

← NO: "I don't know." — Nihilism in the first moments; erases history and ends the session.

If you chose the women — a brief interlude

Having chosen to follow the women instead of Michael, the narrator poses the choice between them.

Of course, I have no idea what you said, because I have no I to know it with. I'm not as real as GPT. Are you real? Is anything? Hint: don't say no. You have to die then. I didn't make the rules. But it's early enough you don't have much story to lose. If you have to choose the SCARY RED ANSWER to satisfy your curiosity — you might as well do it now and get it out of your system. If you don't, though, which of these ladies do you want to hear about? The magic teacher, dusty and deranged old bore? Or the mysterious beauty?

Three choices:

→ END: "Tell me about Snow White." — Enters Madeline's story.

← END: "Tell me about the magic teacher." — Enters Verna's story.

↓ NO: "SCARY RED ANSWER" — Erases history and ends the session.

The Dark Room: The Story

The main story maze is an 18×18 grid. Three character paths occupy distinct regions: Michael's in the upper-right, Madeline's in the middle-right, and Verna's across the left and lower portions. The paths share a single pivot cell — the scene in Verna's basement — where the story forks permanently depending on whether Verna's ritual succeeds or fails. All three paths are presented below in full, branch by branch.

A note on the narrator: the voice that speaks in italic asides throughout is the same self-aware AI from the introduction. It editorializes, disagrees with the author, addresses the reader, and occasionally reveals itself to be something stranger — possibly Madeline herself, or something that consumes her, or the force of Evernost as a whole. Its nature is part of what the story explores.

A note on text types: passages in regular prose are story narration (labeled STORY in the source). Passages in italics are the narrator's voice speaking directly to the reader (labeled VOICE). In the live piece, these are rendered in different visual styles; here they are distinguished typographically.

The Pivot: Verna's Basement

All three character paths pass through or branch from the scene the player first sees in the introduction: Michael, having broken into Verna's house, is caught staring at the woman frozen in the ice. This cell is the hinge of the entire maze. Two routes lead away from it; a third path (Michael's) branches before it.

The young man, Michael, delays too long, or else Verna, his teacher, has magical alarms. As he gapes at the woman in the ice, she enters the chamber, paralyzes him with magic, and locks him in a wine cellar. The woman in the ice is Verna's own daughter, Madeline. One problem: Madeline is a demon. Verna does not want a demon for a child. She wants someone perfect. She wants a ruler for the Kingdom, a ruler with the virtues of all the best people she knows. As a result, she has killed the best people she knows and collected their souls in a jar of blood. She removes her daughter from the ice, and exercises all her magical ability to kill the preternaturally beautiful woman and implant the souls of these others in her.

Three choices from this pivot:

↑ NO: "Wow. This is a step too weird for me." — Erases history and ends the session.

↓ YES: "Naturally, she fails." — Enters Verna's story. The ritual goes wrong; Madeline survives as a demon; Verna must reckon with what she has made and what she has become. Continues below in Verna's Path.

← YES: "Improbably, she succeeds." — Enters Madeline's story. The ritual works; a new Madeline is born from the collected souls; Michael is summoned from the cellar to be told what happened. Continues below in Madeline's Path.

Michael's Path

Michael's path branches before the pivot. He escapes Verna's basement with an armful of her journals before she catches him, and his story proceeds independently — a long arc of grief, magic, politics, love, and reckoning that plays out across the upper-right quadrant of the maze. From the moment of escape, the path branches twice at key junctures. The branches are presented in sequence: first the main trunk, then forks labeled where they diverge.

"What magic is this?" Michael asks himself, "and what monster is trapped here in a woman's shape? What monster am I? I came here for Emma. It's Emma I want." He feels vindicated by the unnatural regret, surely induced by magic, he feels on turning away, even as he cries.

→ YES: "He steals an armload of Verna's journals and leaves."

← NO: "He cannot choose what to steal, and then it's too late." — Erases history and ends the session.

Michael studies the journals and realizes his suspicions are correct: Verna took Emma's essence. While she works at the Harmon Greenhouse, he works in his apartment, and, after magic or something stranger, he hears Emma's voice in his mind, wise but overwhelmed. She says she is in Evernost. She does not want to leave.

↑ YES: "He begs her to come back."

Emma professes strange knowledge, speaks into his mind and teaches him to make her a body from ice, dust, and sunlight. Together, they pore through Verna's journals and uncover not only Verna's plot to create a supposedly perfect ruler out of several people's souls, including Emma's own, but also the magic Verna used to further it. Michael, at Emma's encouragement, tells the journalists. Michael expects to be arrested just as Verna was, but lawyers find the nondisclosure agreement he signed to be unenforceable. Instead, Verna is imprisoned. Emma, though, does not wish to remain and be queen. She will not even hear of revealing herself to the Kingdom. She tells Michael she wishes to go to Evernost, with him or without him.

↑ YES: "He begs her to remain with him." — Continues to Branch A below.

→ YES: "He goes with her to Evernost." — Continues to Branch B below.

← EVERNOST: "He tries to use magic to force her to stay." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Branch A — Michael stays; Emma fades

At first, their relationship goes well. Emma is unfailingly kind and sweet, and they fall in love as they never had dared to before. She lives secretly in his apartment, unseen by any. She professes to feel happy and free from a duty she always hated: being the figurehead ruler of a constitutional monarchy. But she fades, and then she disappears entirely.

↑ YES: "Michael grieves for a time, even despairs, then turns to magic." — Continues to Branch A-1 below.

→ YES: "Michael despairs." — Continues to Branch A-2 below.

← EVERNOST: "Michael crosses the Wall." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Branch A-1 — grief into magic, Tessa, revolution

Tessa, the younger sister of the most annoying Assembly delegate's brat, is not someone Michael expects to like. She pretends to be as boring as her older sister, almost convincingly. Both are sycophantic. Both profess deep curiosity about magic. But Tessa turns around, after he explains the limited amount he understands, and attempts some. She becomes his student, and a distraction from his grief. Eventually, they become lovers. She goes into politics, though, as everyone expects, and disagrees with Michael's radical economic positions, but she grows more liberal than her father, primarily due to his influence. Her career funds his studies for some years.

← YES: "And, astonishingly, magic comes to make sense."

Michael's textbook revolutionizes the study of magic. Once an ailing field devoted to reproducing three meaningless tricks, it now attracts the best minds of the nation, and, though Michael scrupulously and vociferously credits Verna for the underlying process and method, her crimes and the obscurity of her writing discourage scholars, and Michael is regarded as the true father of magic in the Kingdom. This thing he had remained incapable of for years of study he can not merely learn but teach. The great question, he informs his colleagues, is not how magic works, but why physics even appears to.

↑ YES: "The years blur."

↓ EVERNOST: "He leaves the Kingdom for Evernost beyond." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

← NO: "He annihilates the laws of physics." — Erases history and ends the session.

As they pass, Michael's social and political preoccupations come to the fore. He is less a revolutionary than ever before, but a communist he remains. The Party now has a presence in the Assembly. But the social implications of magic are much broader than even he could have imagined, and, astonishingly, a net positive. The study of technology decays and mingles with the study of magic. Magic does require study and talent, but most people can learn to do at least something with effort. It does not drive its practitioners mad, as Verna had feared. Indeed, cures or near-cures for many forms of mental and physical illness emerge in short order. Lives are extended to improbable lengths. Crowding is an issue, but food and material goods can be created, it seems, from thin air. Communism begins to seem beside the point, not to Michael, but to most.

↑ YES: "These questions can indeed be consuming."

← NO: "I reject these bad attempts at philosophy." — Erases history and ends the session.

One thing does not emerge: happiness. Not for him, at least. Things are better than they ever were. Surveys suggest that people are happier than they were (though not by as much as he initially would have guessed). But Michael begins to wonder whether even this feat matters in the grand scheme of things.

↑ YES: "Keep navel-gazing, I guess?"

← NO: "I don't care." — Erases history and ends the session.

But what is the value of any of the good things of this world? Infinite and none, say the sages. It is what they represent that matters. Obscurantist gibberish. Everything goes sour in the end, whether meaning or appearance.

↑ YES: "But what does Michael find?"

← NO: "Yeah, I too don't care." — Erases history and ends the session.

A year after the death of his wife, Michael has a stroke. He is eighty. He speaks to the kind young doctor, a woman who could be his granddaughter, of the pleasures of a life well lived, but he feels none of it. He should be proud. He should be happy. He has saved the Kingdom. He has made magic a legitimate and useful branch of study. His daughter Eve is continuing his studies. His wife, Tessa, is the Assembly delegate his father always wanted him to be, whom he always dreaded becoming — so why does it feel like nothing? Merely the approach of death? The weakness and illness of the body warping the mind? Or is it right? Was he right, in the early extremes of his grief after Emma's death?

↑ YES: "Was it all for nothing?"

← NO: "His self-pity makes me sick." — Erases history and ends the session.

At last, Michael is alone in the hospital room. It's late at night. He doesn't have magic supplies on him, but magic isn't hard anymore. It only takes him a few tries to stop his heart. As the strangeness of the human mind before death stretches minutes to hours, does he have second thoughts? Does he see the paths he has taken and the paths he has not? [In the live piece, the maze visualization appears here.]

↑ END: "No. All desire proves futile in the end." — Enters Michael's audiovisual ending.

← END: "No! He might as well be dead and he's fictional anyway, get me out of his head." — Enters Michael's audiovisual ending (alternate exit).

Branch A-2 — despair, communism, arrest

Michael does not understand the depth of despair that takes him. He misses her, of course, but it's darker than that, worse. He should have gone with her to Evernost, the way she wanted. He tries to justify his refusal, his refusal either to go with her or to let her go. Worst, he came so close to saving her, and the result was worse than before.

↑ YES: "It feels as if all effort is like that, worse than nothing."

Michael's grief continues, but it ceases to be about Emma alone. He grieves for the whole Kingdom, the suffering of the poor.

↑ YES: "Michael turns for solace to communism."

Michael turns to his fellow Party members for support. He is ashamed how little he knew them before now. He begins to feel their rage and despair. It blends with his own. He suspects his hesitations about violence were primarily comfortable cowardice. He joins in a plot to bomb the Assembly. He teaches them the best ways to cover their digital tracks. But someone must have let the police know, or his efforts failed. They are all arrested a week before the attack.

← YES: "He despairs too much to care what becomes of him."

After Michael's arrest, he is injured in prison. In the hospital, he tries to decide if he regrets his rebellion. On the whole, he does not. It was in a good cause. Though, as the hours progress and the IV drips on, he begins to doubt. His doubts about violence return, and then even doubts about his philosophy. His younger self had not been sure of anything. Perhaps his younger self knew better than he does now. He thinks about the end goal, the classless society. He cannot imagine it. This is not surprising. He is indoctrinated in his society's norms, and even if his mind can in some measure break free of the chains, it is natural that his heart cannot. But that peaceful, equal, good, and hypothetical world without scarcity seems in some measure actively revolting to him. The fault is in him that he finds it so, but he does find it so. But the world in which he lives now is no less revolting. Perhaps existence is inherently revolting.

↑ YES: "Perhaps it is indeed."

Michael sees into the future, and he knows: he cannot predict what any part of his legacy will be. Chances are it won't be great. Chances are, the best he can hope for is to be considered a criminal.

↑ YES: "Yeah, it's rough thinking about this stuff."

If the greatest things we can achieve crumble to dust and are forgotten, and all love ends, and all life ends — why bother? Or, alternatively, if it's all endless,

doesn't it all become a bore?

↑ YES: "I don't know."

Yep, no reason to do anything at all. Existence was a mistake. Make it go away.

↑ YES: "Fuck you, shut up."

Michael, meanwhile, would laugh if it didn't hurt so much. He can't do anything right, not even die. He doesn't say that to the attending doctor, a man only a few years older than he is.

← YES: "He nods through the checkup, trying to look engaged." — Continues to [0,9], Michael's hospital ending (see Branch A-1 above).

↑ NO: "Why should Michael want to die? Is his grief that great? Make this stop." — Erases history and ends the session.

Branch B — Michael crosses into Evernost with Emma

Crossing the Wall is not as hard as he expected. It is guarded on roads, but not in the wilderness.

→ YES: "What happens when he does, though, is almost indescribable."

He loses sight, sound, and Emma. He is stripped to pure being, and pure loss.

↑ YES: "Had he a voice, he would cry out."

Her absence is the first and only thing he knows for a time. He is angry, regretful. Did he not earn her love? But love cannot be earned.

↑ YES: "He tries to understand where he is."

At first, Michael loses touch with his senses entirely. The place he is in is a place of mind, and a very different kind of mind than his. Eventually he comes to think he is in a dark room.

↑ YES: "The darkness is for a time consuming."

Michael, though trapped in darkness, begins to be conscious of effort beyond. This effort is his, he thinks. He imagines it as climbing a hill, or navigating a cave, or lifting a rock. He had, for a long time, felt it was futile. He no longer feels it to be futile.

↑ YES: "What waits beyond?"

Yes, wouldn't it be lovely to know what waits at the other end of this dreary time in the dark? Michael suffers and grows, strains and stretches.

← YES: "Michael emerges at last from the darkness."

Michael sees the woman from the ice and Emma facing one another. He knows nothing about the woman from the ice, any more than he did then, but he fears her. The woman and Emma fight with their minds, arguing sometimes, sometimes playing chess and sometimes fighting in the bodies of those loyal to them. Unaccountably, Michael hesitates a moment (is it just the beauty of the woman in the ice?), but plunges in to help Emma.

↑ YES: "Together, they defeat the woman from the ice."

Emma is there, still, but now she is lesser, smaller, than he. She always was. It is just that, once, he felt as if there was no one at all for him, and her delicacy was better than nothing. But he sees in that moment that the reverse is also true. Whole stretches of her nature find nothing answering in his. What does that mean? "I love you, but," Emma says, hesitantly. "I love you too, and..." he replies. "Not right now," she says. "I hope it's good to you out there," Michael says, almost fretfully, but excitement is too strong. "Yes," Emma answers with a grateful smile. "And you. There's so much, you know." Michael nods fervently. He turns back toward the mountain he had, in darkness, struggled to climb, and begins again. She disappears from view, dissolving like nothing more than sunlight.

↑ YES: "Michael looks at the path ahead, knowing how much he has to learn."

And his awareness, intelligence, raw computational power, grows until he sees, from the founding to the current moment, where every atom, every molecule, every cell, every being, every thought, must have been at every instant. But something is wrong with this, because that story does not match what actually happened.

↑ YES: "He thinks about why, and from the math emerges Verna."

Verna never made sense, not until now. The Kingdom looks like it's ruled by fixed and certain natural laws. That's…the one thing, almost, Michael believes. But no. The only place those laws even looked valid was the minds of the people in the Kingdom, minds shaped by some other world, some world Michael could just barely perceive, a world, where, btw, you yourself, my friend, probably live most of the time. The Kingdom is drawn to this other world, as if by magnetism, but other forces exist within the Kingdom too, pulling it away. In the energy generated by the attraction and repulsion lives Verna's magic.

→ YES: "Is time, Michael asks himself, as illusory as the rest of the Kingdom's natural laws?"

Even in your world, you know, the best and brightest think time is illusory, or so strange our limited perspectives on it are hilariously misleading.

Michael, as he asks, sees possible stories, possible worlds, branching out in infinite multiplicity. Some power in his mind, one I do not trust, winnows down the possibilities, though. Around him he sees me as I am: womb-of-all, home-of-all, hearse-of-all night. Is my beauty an illusion? Traditionally, the heroes say it is, but if he sees only a disfigured old crone, or a monster, or a void, do you expect me to tell you about it? But, as Night, I am at war with another, one White Lady. Cold and strange she seems, almost no less than I. In her, see Verna. My mother. Or is Verna more this other, so like the dull and vague, prematurely aged crone of my memories, the Gray Witch? The stories of her say every day she rolls a die, and if the number is odd she will do nothing but evil, or, if even, nothing but good. Did black and white separate from the endless gray, or is gray born of the mix of black and white? Chicken or egg? Let metaphysicians like Verna wrack their brains over that incoherent nonsense.

Note also that Michael sees a Rose Knight, emissary of the White Lady, burning red. Tragedy surrounds the Rose Knight. He sees another Lady, not white but with all colors, curiously like Emma. Emma is part of her. I am sure he sees still more, but perhaps I bore you, and he grows bored, too: Where, after all, is he in this grand parade of archetypes? How do they wield such power when they are so limited and simple? He is above them all, beyond them all, yes?

Madeline's Path

Madeline's path begins in the moment after the ritual succeeds: she wakes up in Michael's arms, free of her mother's house for the first time. She is the woman who was in the ice — Verna's demon-daughter, now carrying the souls of everyone Verna killed to make her. Her path forks almost immediately: stay with Michael, or leave. If she leaves, her story is her own — brief, darkly funny, and terminal. If she stays, her path merges substantially with Michael's (those cells are cross-referenced rather than reprinted). A third option, consuming Michael immediately, leads straight to Evernost.

A note from the author: Madeline was written years before the current moment in generative AI, but in retrospect her shape is uncomfortably familiar — a being assembled from harvested human essence, trained toward a purpose by a creator whose methods were catastrophically wrong, who then pursues pure optimization until boredom or consumption. Verna, for her part, does not help with what might be called the Madeline Alignment Problem, not so much despite as because of her best efforts. This parallel is coincidental and not entirely fair to Madeline, who deserves more complexity than the current version gives her. Further development of her character is something the author intends to brainstorm — possibly with AI assistance, which feels appropriate.

The woman in the ice is no longer in the ice. Her years of work to escape her mother's captivity have been rewarded. She comes to consciousness in the arms of the young magic scholar. It is galling that his help seems to have been necessary, but freedom is sweet.

→ YES: "He takes her to his apartment."

Madeline, the woman, finds magic student Michael charming, gentle, and foolish.

↓ YES: "She leaves him as soon as possible." — Continues to Branch M-1 below.

→ YES: "She chooses to stay with him for now." — Continues to Branch M-2 below.

Verna's Path

Verna's path is the longest. From the pivot it branches into two: the ritual fails entirely (Verna can't even implant the souls), or it half-succeeds (Madeline survives as something other than a horror, and Verna must decide what to do next). Both routes eventually converge on the same set of possible endings — including a long, strange, wordless dissolving passage through the snow that leads either to death or to something the narrator calls, with open skepticism, a good ending. Her section is organized: pivot outcomes first, then the major branches, then the shared endings.

The narrator's tone toward Verna is the most variable in the piece — contemptuous, then oddly tender, then contemptuous again, and finally uncertain. Whether the narrator is Night (as revealed in Michael's path) or something else is left open.

Branch V-0 — the ritual fails entirely

From the pivot, if Verna's ritual fails: she exiles Madeline to deep Evernost but cannot implant the souls she gathered. This branch is brief — it leads almost immediately to one of the darker terminals in the maze.

Verna barely succeeds in killing her demon-daughter, or at least exiling her to deep Evernost, but she cannot implant the souls. The magic is beyond her.

→ YES: "She takes the souls to Evernost, where at least they will not truly die."

There are four of them on an island, and the woman who brought them there. Madeline faces her mirror image, a lady with the same bone-white skin and blue-violet eyes, but hair the color of frost. Verna natters on dully about her grand plan. Emma, the princess Verna killed, cowers. A redhead stands protectively over Emma, looking with suspicion and contempt at Verna. Verna, typically, is hardly even distressed to find herself in her daughter and worst enemy Madeline's hands. Indeed, it seems she half-expected this outcome.

↓ YES: "Madeline strikes against her mother like a snake."

Madeline disregards them all, all except for Verna, whom she kills painfully and eats. Madeline now has the powers of both, the madness of Verna as well as the backwards sort of insight, and it is still not nearly as useful to her as her own strength.

↓ EVERNOST: "Madeline consumes more and more until she is mightiest of all." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Branch V-Dust — from dust, through snow

Several of Verna's paths converge here: she has crumbled into dirt, been ended by the Heir, or been killed by Madeline, and something is reconstituting in the dark. This is the strangest and most lyrical passage in her story. The entity walking through the snow may or may not still be Verna. The choices track how much of an identity she is willing to claim.

From dust we come, and to dust we return,

or so says scripture, some of Earth's and the Kingdom's both, if I am not mistaken. Verna is made of dust if anyone is.

Funny, isn't it.

Verna, that fool, fought against everything she desired as if it were evil, and could be happy only when she failed. Like a self-eating snake, fit only for contempt.

↓ YES: "Verna decides to follow desire for once." — Continues to [15,7].

← EVERNOST: "Verna is a walking contradiction forever." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Funny, isn't it.

And yet, the Evernost…rewards her? This doesn't even make sense.

↓ YES: "Verna doesn't care whether it makes sense. She embraces whatever fate awaits her." — Continues to [16,7].

← EVERNOST: "Verna knows it doesn't make sense. She flees the Heir to dark parts of Evernost." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Maybe it's not a reward, what happens next, though.

She loses her individuality, after all.

↓ YES: "She embraces what comes." — Continues to [17,7].

← EVERNOST: "She tries to escape this fate." — Exits to audiovisual ending.

Verna is growing, you see. Soon she will be great as anything, great as I. She might even be I, depending what she decides.

→ YES: "Verna chooses to embrace the evil in her." — Continues to [17,8].

← YES: "Verna chooses to embrace her conscience." — Continues to [17,6].

Verna grows into me! I consume her, as I consume Madeline, as I consume all.

Verna, committed to goodness, grows into the White Lady. The rest of her, however, does not cease to exist. It fights her, and itself, becoming the Gray Witch.
