“I am telling you, Michael,” said Madeline, “I want all of this to happen very much. And when I want things to happen, they do. Sometimes I work with my body to make them happen. Sometimes I work with my magic. But they happen.”

Michael stared at her, finally beginning to think. He didn’t know whether to be hopeful or horrified. This couldn’t be right. Magic couldn’t be that powerful — could it? And if she were really this powerful, why did she want him around? What possible appeal could he hold for her? Wasn’t he just holding her back?
“I love you,” Madeline said, as if reading his thoughts (perhaps she was). “You saved me, and I’ll never forget that. Kiss me,” she said. “I need you too.”
Michael did. But he also said, “Do you suppose — do you suppose some of Verna’s awfulness was fear?”

“Yes,” said Madeline. “She was insane, remember. She thought she was the most powerful person in the world until I came along. How could she not fear that? But she still wished to make use of it. I merely didn’t prove sufficiently pliant and docile...”
“No, you’re not,” Michael agreed. “So she wanted someone perfect by her standards to wield your powers?”
Madeline smiled. “Yes. She did not think any mere mortal equal to them.”
“Do you?” Michael asked.

“I want to make our dreams come true, Michael. What could be wrong with that?”
Michael could think of any number of things, but he thought that was probably being a poor sport. Madeline started seducing him, and he could not resist her. He didn’t want to.

Victory

The day after next, Michael, embarrassed about the glut of noodles even though Madeline seemed at least as no-nonsense in her tastes as he was, cooked fresh vegetables and laid the table nicely. He even scrounged up some candles from his last roommate, and though they were mismatched, one powder blue and unused and the other burnt low and made of unrefined beeswax, he liked the effect.
Madeline, who had been in and out all day, announced that she had a job.
“I thought you didn’t—”

“You’re going to like this,” she said, her small and mysterious smile remaining on her face longer than it usually did.
“What are you doing?” Michael asked obligingly.
“Greg from the meeting,” Madeline explained, “connected me to the Party. They need writing and web development. I figure we’d be perfect. At least, I know you’re brilliant with the web, and they liked the sample I wrote them so much they accepted me on the spot. I met with the chairman in person.”

“You what?”
Madeline shrugged. “I scheduled an appointment. He’s free all day. Doesn’t even have a job—lives off donations and works for the Party. Smokes weed for the Party, more like.”
Michael nodded. He’d imagined Madeline might try to bring about a communist government by magic, but clearly that wasn’t what she had in mind. He asked why not.
“I do things the easiest way to do them. Sometimes that’s magic, sometimes it’s not.”

“You make the magic seem awfully easy.”
She laughed. “Sometimes it is. Getting out of that cell was the nearest thing to impossible I’ve ever done. But I did it.”
“How can I help?” Michael finally asked humbly.
She smiled. “Well, I volunteered your software skills. I figured you’d appreciate it. If you don’t, you can tell me to go to hell.”

“I —” She had anticipated his anger as well as everything else. He thought it through briefly, and he said, “I would be honored to help.”
“Thought so,” Madeline said. “I am honored to work with you.”

The Party’s databases and website were, it turned out, a shambles.
Michael fixed security problems right and left, and, at Madeline’s request, modernized its look too. It was good, fun work, steadier than his usual work, and he was given a freer hand than usual (the Party’s last IT person had been a reluctant undergraduate volunteer who majored in cultural studies, and he was more than happy to give up his post to Michael.) Somehow, Madeline made them pay him more than usual, too, though he’d expected to work for free.

The police had more questions, too. He did not mention Madeline and felt glad Madeline had not told him where Verna was. If she had, he would have found it harder to deny any knowledge.

Meanwhile, Madeline announced their promotional materials were rubbish. “Too academic,” she said, “and not even competent at it.” She locked herself into Michael’s room and produced, as he worked in the living room, pages and pages of articles, pamphlets, and FAQs. Their quality impressed even Michael, who fancied himself a good enough writer, and remembered Emma’s being a better one still (though he couldn’t imagine Emma descending to write advertising).

Madeline’s writing was smooth, sharp, quotable, and simplified. But it was good simplification, he thought; she honed in on the essentials and stated them, bold and bare and absolute. The Party’s usual publicity person, a professor but, in Madeline’s view, “dumb as a rock,” resigned in anger, but most were pleased.

As their website improved over the following months, so did their visibility. Madeline aggressively pushed for interviews with newspapers and bloggers and publically donated a large amount of their suddenly-expanding coffers to victims of a tornado that had devastated the northeast of the Kingdom. This controversial move worked brilliantly. Within three weeks, the donations replaced themselves entirely.

At Madeline’s suggestion (Michael was happy in his dump), they moved into a nicer apartment. She wanted to take out a loan for a house, but Michael refused to go that far — luckily, it turned out, because a month later she started running for public office using the name Alison Montgomery.
“Mother isn’t gone,” Madeline explained. “I have to change my appearance or she’ll suspect.”

Michael said something tentative about wigs, but she just laughed. Of course, as he so often seemed to do (and even she did sometimes), he’d forgotten about her magic. Within the next few minutes he found himself facing a green-eyed blonde with very different facial structure.
Michael jumped and uttered a small scream.
Madeline just laughed. “You’re still mine, Michael. And when we’re alone together, I’ll be myself. Promise.”

He kissed her shyly and she responded with a caress down the back of his shirt and a giggle.
“You make a nice blonde,” he said.
“I know,” said Madeline. “And they’ll like me better, too. Take me less seriously, but that’s a tradeoff I’m willing to risk.”
Within three months, “Alison Montgomery” was elected mayor of the small town Helsom, one of the places ravaged by the tornado, after a campaign conducted entirely long-distance.

He offered to come with her, but she refused. “You’re needed in Prima,” she said, “and we’ll be in touch.” He suppressed an embarrassing amount of self-pitying anger as he saw off the still-unnerving blonde.
Madeline fired many city employees—keeping, however, a few sentimental favorites (she explained her policy in detail to Michael, who wondered at both her acumen and her ruthlessness)—and instituted a series of exams she wrote herself based on something she’d read to determine who served in what capacity.

At the same time she poured Party funds into the library and had it and the City Hall renovated. “It’s a shame Helsom is so tiny,” Madeline said. “I would love to establish public transportation, but who needs that when you can walk everywhere? Free vaccines are my next goal.”
“Tax increases?” Michael asked.
“Magic. The Party. Streamlining the bureaucracy. Who knows?” said Madeline.

She looked into education next and, after reading text upon text from Prima University and declaring all of them marginally useful bullshit, instituted educational reforms she’d cobbled together from all of them. She said she was having a deliciously good time, “though,” she added, “it’s still dead boring compared to running a country. Education is the only important thing I control, and honestly, who gives a fuck.”
“You do,” Michael said. “I mean, you’ve put a lot of work—”

“Oh, goddammit. Putting work into shit like that is like playing your goddamn video games all day. It’s boring. Michael, I love you. I almost believe you would enjoy that—could do a good job at it. I’m not going to let you rot like that. You’re going to be great.” Before he could essay a response, she said, “I know, dear. I love you. Goodnight.”

In the meantime, apart from fixing the occasional bug or hack on the Party website, he was at loose ends. Madeline wouldn’t tell him where she’d gotten the money she’d filled his wallet—and, it turned out, his bank account—with. Probably from thin air, possibly from the coffers of the rich and privileged.
So he tried to do magic on his own. He nearly asked Madeline if she could teach him, but she was busy, and he had no desire to bore her in the way schools did.

But he was bored. He took long, aimless walks. He reflected on his first impression of Madeline as a regal, otherworldly beauty. This was almost laughable. Almost. There was still something about her, despite her practicality, that set her apart from every other person he’d ever known. The practicality was a cloak over inner otherness, a fierce — not drive, because that implied the merely active, whereas the energy of her existed in stillness as well as in motion. If most people were, as scripture said, clay, she was something else—flame, perhaps.

At the local communist meetings, he was treated with a new deference—and spoke with a new deference, careful to avoid contradicting Madeline’s beliefs as written in her pamphlets except in ways that were deeply considered and circumspectly stated. This was not a conscious decision. He actually did agree with her much more than he had two months ago, and his objections to her arguments now felt frivolous, weak, and inconsequential—though he knew uneasily that he would not have felt like that before he met her.

He felt, as he had not before, what a motley crew of misfits the discussion group was — powerless, impractical, passionate but ultimately clueless. He was beginning to see with Madeline’s eyes — or at least with the eyes of someone who had seen power in action.
He finally turned to Verna’s journals, except for three Madeline had taken with her. He fancied, vaguely, turning them into a publishable textbook — a notion that Verna would have found both ludicrous and unwise, but surely magic should be a force for social change.

He read about how particular spells could be performed, but only, say, one in every five tries did he achieve a visible result, and then rarely the result he wanted. Verna had tried to explain how she adjusted her own spells to include him as an agent within them, and how they changed from circumstance to circumstance, but he had grasped only the occasional morsel. He’d suspected she was deliberately mystifying him, that it could not be that complex. And yet — and yet — here he was, poring through her journals and still failing.

It could be, he admitted, that Verna was simply a poor communicator and a fuzzy thinker. She had something that worked, but perhaps even she did not understand it. Or perhaps she really was as far beyond him as he seemed to feel — but no one had ever been that far beyond him, not in his entire life, not even his physics professors, not even his classmates. Not even, he thought, Madeline, who was indubitably (he thought with only the slightest chagrin) more intelligent than he was.

Verna had explained once that she was teaching him her own magic system and he would eventually need to invent his own. How he had created the stealth spell was still beyond him; his panic had spurred him, perhaps, to unconscious insights. But he was not like Madeline; he could not wish the simplest thing true. So he persevered.

The fourth week of Madeline’s absence, and the third week of his magical experiments, he began to realize he did not miss her. She had thrilled him, but she was, as their conversations happened less and less frequently, fading like a dream, and he loved the solitary immersion in efforts at magic, even though they were mostly fruitless so far.

On one of his long walks, surrounded by autumn leaves, he felt for the first time in weeks a strong, unselfconscious longing for Emma and everything that surrounded her. Gentle, laughing, uninterested in eighty percent of his interests and in violent disagreement with him on the remaining twenty percent. He longed for the castle, for the light on the Harmon greenhouse as he went to study with Verna—he longed for a Verna who hadn’t frozen her own daughter into a wall of ice, though everything Madeline said fit with his more Outlandish ideas about Verna even from back then.

And he longed to have nothing to do with Madeline again. He could still summon everything he found wonderful in her, but it moved him little. He began to cry as he entered the house. With the profound illogic of grief, he felt that if he had not encountered Madeline, he might have found Emma again.

Perhaps, though, he ought to explore that symbolism more deeply. Was Madeline the new order and Emma the old? Both beautiful, both powerful, but one brilliant and efficient and aggressive, the other effete and useless and essentially decorative. Oh, how Emma would have hated being called decorative — she worked so hard to be competent and good. He hated even the idea of it for her sake, but at the same time that it seemed obviously true.

And Madeline loved him, he reminded himself. She agreed with him. She was making his dreams come true. Still, he felt an unutterable sense of loss. He lay awake in bed, crying intermittently, most of the night.
The next morning, he heard Madeline (“Alison Montgomery”) was running for a seat in the Assembly. “Out with the old and in with the new,” Michael whispered, expecting to feel as sad as he had yesterday — or, alternatively, excited with his first excitement. Instead, he felt dull and empty, though the emptiness was quickly filled as he pursued several new theories about how to make fire.

He called Madeline, and she apologized for not having told him, explaining that she was scheduled back-to-back, and saying that unless he wanted a public role as her boyfriend, he would have to stay out of the limelight. He said, half-heartedly, that he would like to do that, but she said, “Liar. You hate events, you hate publicity, you like to think. And I’ll make sure you have leave to do that.”

Madeline was behind in the polls, of course. Neither the left nor the right, however, in Helsom’s county, offered an appealing choice. One was even more of an outsider than Madeline, and the other was inarticulate and lacked charisma. And, three weeks into the campaign, Madeline’s investigations turned up embezzlement on the part of the first and three affairs on the part of the second. Both protested their innocence, of course, but the proof was irrefutable and impossible to fake.

At least, so it appeared. Michael wondered. But he supposed that in the service of a goal so great planting evidence would not be so terrible a crime — especially since both men (and Madeline quietly made much of the fact that both were men, and that Helsom had not yet ever elected a woman) were part of a corrupt system and meant to do nothing to bring it down.

When another tornado hit, this time in a neighborhood close to Michael, though it did not touch his apartment (to his surprise, given the predictions), Madeline stepped up again, giving her own money (Alison Montgomery was supposed to be independently wealthy, making her position all the more touching and self-sacrificing, as well as explaining the money; Madeline reassured Michael repeatedly that Alison’s paperwork was all in order and that she’d even found someone to claim to be her grandmother).

No communist had won a seat on the Assembly since the beginning of the Party in 686. It was all the stranger because the Kingdom was growing conservative. Michael was dumbstruck when the news came out in November. He called her and left a message. He resisted the impulse to intrude by calling again and spent that day feeling more unreal than ever. He went to the communist discussion group to celebrate with cheap wine and beer and came home at nine.
That night, at midnight, dressed in a black cloak that covered her face, Madeline appeared at his door.

“My love,” she said simply, stepping in and shrugging off the cloak to reveal the black-haired beauty she once had been the moment the door was shut.
He almost thought she was a hallucination. She was so much more beautiful than he’d remembered—though not different in any way he could pinpoint. He could tell he was staring, but he couldn’t tell what he felt. His heart pounded.

“I am going to win the world—and it’s all for you,” she said, leading him unresisting but unhelping to the bedroom, slipping her hand again under his shirt, and taking it off as they neared the creaky old bed. “Really, it’s all for you. I’ve brought wine.”
And, just like her beauty, it was better than it ever had been, as far as he could tell, as they lay naked together, snacking on wine and cheese. She refused to mention politics, except to say, “This is the goal. This—the rest—the rest is just a means to an end. And that end is a life for everyone as free — as beautiful — as happy — as we have.”

Michael floated on the taste of wine, on fear and on ecstasy. Part of him remained cold, doubting, cynical, untouched by everything. He left it behind. He grew and grew, past his own mind into

white nights

White Night

Though only the dim overhead light (its bulb’s life prolonged by magic) lit the small bedroom, sunlight blazed in Madeline’s mind as she croaked, "I’m done!"

She crouched in a huddle against the foot of her bed, crying, moaning, and slamming her fist into the floor.
None of these were strange activities for Madeline.
She was, perhaps, young, not even an adult — seventeen, eighteen, nineteen — but unlike most teenagers, she had been that age for — she would have been surprised how long, although she knew it had been longer than the usual stretch.

The bedroom where she lived was five yards in both directions and painted white, abutting a ceramic-tiled bathroom. Areas of the wall were scraped down to wood paneling, others scribbled with arcane symbols. Dirty white blankets covered her bed; once, there had been an afghan Mother had made, but Madeline had torn it to pieces long ago.
Otherwise, the room contained a battered red armchair and a bookshelf, this containing several shelves of tomes on history and political theory. Madeline had read all of them several times, even though she hated books, and even though Mother had brought them herself and clearly hoped she would read them.

She had held off from the first fifteen until boredom and an itch for anything from outside, even books, overcame her. Then she’d ripped into them like a starving man through a banquet. When Mother had brought the next books, she’d read them immediately, then torn some to bits, burned others, and written all over yet others.
But Madeline was not in the mood for books. She was trying to remember.
She remembered almost nothing before this room. Her feelings of the outside world contained little more than “freedom,” “sunlight,” and “green.” Senseless, she knew — but as alluring as it was simplistic.

Even before anything had happened (Madeline did not know what had happened; she thought she might have repressed the memories, a phenomenon she'd read about in passing, but found it likelier magic was involved), she had loved the world despite Mother, not because of her. Mother was the constant from before until now. "If you cannot be good," Mother had non-explained when she locked the bedroom door for the last time (Madeline remembered that), "then good must come of you." And even now, she occasionally appeared, gray and wrong, though she never deigned to speak with Madeline.

(That was how Madeline could tell the hallucinations of Mother from the real thing: the hallucinations spoke. In fact, they talked for hours and hours, sometimes pure drivel or about "mad" things like alternate universes and space flight and conspiracies, but more often about Madeline. The hallucinations of Mother could explain how every one of Madeline’s thoughts, instincts, and reactions indicated her innate evil. Usually these rants ended with Madeline’s screaming and trying to kill the hallucination, which would turn out to be nothing but thin air. Madeline had laughed with relief when she’d read prisoners in isolation hallucinated; she had assumed until then that she was mad, or that Mother was sending images of herself to make Madeline mad).

Madeline’s memories were rudimentary, but she had, of course, read. This reading, the sum of it, told her that Mother’s “evil” was a lie. Humans had to work together to survive. You were good if you were with the herd, and evil if you were against — except for the illogical intricacies that resulted when people tried to make morality make real sense.
She dreamed of explaining this to Mother, in clipped and condescending detail, before killing her.
Killing Mother was one thing Madeline knew she wanted to do.

Madeline wondered how she could be “done” with her imprisonment, with Mother, with all of it. She believed, though, or rather, she knew: knew that when she felt that certainty about anything—that she would finish a book, write a story, set a fire, or carve a chunk of bookshelf into a shape—that thing would happen.
The obvious possibility was suicide, but Madeline was not easy to kill. She had tried various methods, and she always healed at once. Mother had stopped feeding her long ago with no ill effects.

But Madeline did not feel suicidal. She felt hopeful and certain.
She had, of course, bashed holes in all of the walls, but they had opened on something she could not name, define, or describe — something she could not touch or enter. This had been, somehow, obvious just from looking at it, but "obvious" did not stop Madeline from trying to climb through each of the holes into the nothingness, if that was what it was. But, however often she tried, she merely found herself back where she started, feeling nauseous. The way her eyes slid away from the gaps annoyed her, so she boarded them up.

Even Mother only entered through the door, so that was her best choice. She had, though, made every kind of attempt she could imagine on the door. She had slammed her bed into it, she had set it on fire, and she had tied a long string of torn up blankets to the handle, secured the string to the bed, and jumped on it.
She had used magic too, of course.

Madeline doubted Mother had meant to supply her with a book of magic, inscribed with Mother's own notes, but the slim volume had come between a tome on socialism and a history of the Open Border Movement's beginnings in the time of Dorian the Defender, who had enacted the Ban on crossing the wall into Outland.
This book Madeline treated with reverence. But Madeline did not need symbols and herbs and strange materials to do magic, though they made it easier and were responsible for her greatest triumph: glimpses outside world in the bathroom mirror, thanks to more than three hundred symbols carved into the light blue wallpaper beside the glass.

No, Madeline had found long before Mother had dumped this present set of books on her that if she wished hard enough, long enough, for something, that thing was likely to happen. She could, with her will alone, cause objects to fly across the room. She could crack the walls. She could set fires and extinguish them.
She did not have the runes and skills and materials to undo whatever Mother had done to the door to keep it shut, though its whole surface was covered in carvings and blood and ashes from her previous efforts. So this time, she did it her way: she tried.

This too, she had done many times before, but she had always given up at some point. When you had infinite time on your hands, though, it didn't matter how long something took. Madeline had written whole books between the lines of her mother's; she had unraveled mathematical dilemmas; all while she still spent hours lying in bed in a dazed and miserable trance, or hallucinating, or crying ,or shouting, or slamming her body into the door.
So Madeline stared at the door, willing it open, for hours and hours and hours. She half-slept sometimes but always awoke to find that her will had been working away. She did not know it, but she sat there, willing, for three months.

When the door began to move, she leapt up and ripped it open.
Madeline squeezed through the door and started screaming in seconds.
The door slammed shut behind her. The flames were too bright to look at; against her will, she squeezed her eyes shut and could not bring herself to open them for more than a few seconds at a time in her efforts to understand the space she was trapped in. These efforts were in vain: the fire hurt her eyes more than the rest of her put together, and she could see nothing but raging orange in every direction.

The pain was not just in her skin but throughout her body; it felt as if everything from her bones and muscles to her eyes to her brain were aflame and screaming. Nonetheless, this was nothing next to the effect it had on Madeline's mind.
Something hated her: hated everything about her, from the her posture to her misery to her rage to her most trivial reactions. Or not hated, exactly. Rejected. Absolutely. And was going to destroy. The fire wanted her to fear and repent and pity, and she felt as if it were trying to burn away everything that was not those things. More than anything, she realized, it was like her hallucinations of Mother's lectures.

Madeline had never seen a use for fear: in general, if you were smart enough to know what would kill you, that was good enough — and, in her case, she couldn’t die even if she wanted to. Madeline saw nothing to repent, only bad thoughts, and her plan to kill Mother. Surely no one would blame her for that. And if someone wanted her to pity someone else when she was trapped in the middle of something that felt like this they were out of their minds.
So, the fire might not hate her precisely, but she hated it with everything in her, and for a time that was enough to protect her.

In the meantime, her efforts to find out the size and shape of the area of flames were going better. She was in a narrow, arcing band of space. A foot behind and a foot ahead of her was nothingness, or whatever intractable substance had hidden beyond the walls of her bedroom. The fire increased in intensity as she neared its edges, so that she could not even bear to make efforts on them more than once an hour or so. On the left and right ends of the fire, however, she found concrete.

At first, she hurled herself at it, because, resisting the fire both physically and mentally, she did not have the energy for planning and active effort. Only after a while (a week, but she did not know that) did she begin attacking the left-hand joint between the concrete and the nothingness with her will instead of her body. And it was several more weeks of exhaustion, overwhelm, and even the occasional spike of terror (of losing herself, not her body), that she broke through.

Coming straight from the fire, she had in no way prepared for the ice. And so it caught her and immobilized her utterly before three seconds had passed. The ice, like the fire, touched her mind as well as her body. And it killed her rage almost as quickly as it quelled her movements. She was not bored or restless or even angry ten years later, any more than she could move freely.

Then she thought she’d fallen back into the fire. But the heat grew to something like an embrace, a comfort rich and welcoming and far too much, almost disgusting. Shortly after that (she was in motion for a time during this phase) it was pleasant warmth, and she realized someone was carrying her.

“Someone” lay her down on a hard surface. “You’re beautiful,” a voice whispered breathily. “Someone should paint your portrait. Pity I’m no artist.”
Oh, boy. She was beautiful, or at least enough so to impress whomever this might be. Maybe this was why Mother had been so — so — In any event, steps creaked and crunched away. Cautiously, she opened her eyes.

For an instant, she feared Mother had taken her for new torments. But the place had nothing to do with Mother — there was nothing of Mother’s strange ostentation about it, nothing of her haphazard ambition. Books, papers, and dirty dishes were everywhere. There was a television, at least, but it was wired to a bunch of plastic buttons, on a desk opposite her, with a dusty black swivel chair in front of it. The room smelled a little of sweat. The only sign of any aesthetic impulse was the immensely detailed and ugly-as-sin dragon poster on the wall. Nor did Madeline sense the deathly gray of Mother’s presence.

Madeline liked messy and ugly, she decided, but, more important, she was almost positive she had really, truly escaped, and by this Madeline knew that she could do anything.
Not only that, but there was a young man who was besotted with her in the other room. She stretched luxuriously. Everything would be easy from here. She thought back on her brief, hard-won glimpses of the real world in her bathroom mirror and scoffed. She could have done so much better than that. She could even have escaped long ago — But how long ago? She had to learn the date.

A tall, doughy, pale man came in, dropped the tea to the ground, and yelled. He sucked on one finger as he picked up his black mug with his other hand. “Oh!” he said. “Argh, I’m sorry! I —”
Madeline suppressed a laugh as he ran fussing around to the kitchen. He reacted like that — to a burned finger? What a fragile little thing — he didn’t know the first thing about pain.
“What’s your name?” she asked, invitingly.
“Michael?” he said. “And you — what’s your — also, are you okay? Can I get you anything? What happened to you?”

This genuinely horrified pity felt good and right and delicious, a vindication. She looked into Michael’s earnest face and tried a melancholy half-smile. “I am Madeline, and I’m well, thanks to you. You saved me.”

Michael

He blushed bright pink and visibly tried not to look too pleased or honored. “I thought I saw you in a wall of ice,” he said, though, curiosity taking over from delight, slightly to her disappointment. “It disappeared off and on. I know that doesn’t make sense. I’m not at all sure that’s what really happened.” He looked sweetly, foolishly humble again.
“Oh, it happened,” said Madeline.
“Where do you come from?” he asked eagerly. “What was Verna doing with you? Do you know? Or do you not want to talk about it? I’m sorry, I know — I know it has to be stressful.”

Madeline did not want to admit that she knew about as little about her past as he did. So, feigning sadness, she said, “I — I would prefer not to talk about it.” After a moment’s thought, she added venomously, “Verna is my mother.”
“Your mother,” Michael repeated, precisely the right mix of disgust and horror and confusion in his voice.
“Indeed,” said Madeline.

“Oh,” Michael said, clearly unsure what to say next. He began tidying up, self-conscious. “Is there anything I can get for you?” he repeated. “I have pop and some leftover pizza… We could go out for dinner! There’s a good chicken place just a couple of blocks away... I mean, what do you like?”
“Whatever you would like,” Madeline said. She knew nothing about food beyond what she’d read, and it seemed best to let him have his way anyway. “But yes, I’d like to eat. I’m ravenous.” This was true, she realized now that food suddenly seemed a real possibility.

“Give me a second to get ready. I have just the idea,” Michael said with a grin and a look like he was about to do something new and bold. He disappeared into his room and emerged with a mighty, incongruous laugh.
Apparently “new and bold” meant wearing a worn suit instead of shorts and a tee shirt and proposing a restaurant that — Madeline gathered from his tone of voice — was expensive.
Well, as he liked. She followed him out —

— into open air. The neighborhood was nothing next to air and sun. She was like the sun, she decided, and not like the apartment buildings or Michael or Mother, small and gray and scruffy. She suppressed her own leonine laugh but saw Michael looking at her strangely. Oh, yes, you weren’t supposed to look at the sun. It could hurt your eyes.
Her eyes weren’t hurt. Michael clearly noticed this — maybe he wasn’t so stupid after all.
In the rusted, battered little white car, Michael clearly wanted to ask questions and repressed the desire. Two birds with one stone: she didn’t have to try to piece together her past and she had an appealing aura of mystery.

At the restaurant, whose sleek black leather and dim red light suited her much better than anywhere else she’d been, she played as sweet and dumb as she thought she could get away with. Let Michael be horrified at her ignorance and isolation, moved by her admiration. He ordered wine but looked at his own glass as if it were a poisonous animal; amused, she wished that he would drink more — and he did, until he shoved the glass away.
It proved a night of long and self-conscious explanations — of menus and waiters, of the Kingdom’s government and history, of his amateur heroics.

On this last point, he barked first, “I’m going to be arrested.” He digressed then on the injustice of the legal system, and only after he’d solved the world’s problems in one speech, parts of which had clearly come from the very books Mother had given her, did he let on he studied magic with Mother. She smiled to hide her gasp of envy. She realized she had once asked Mother to teach her magic, and Mother had not only refused, she’d — Madeline couldn’t remember what she had done.

Michael thought Mother had murdered the princess. Now, that was interesting. Also interesting was the way he blushed and stumbled when she came up. Clearly, Madeline had competition in his heart, albeit dead competition.
But no — he thought Princess Emma was not actually dead, that Mother had preserved her soul or something. Madeline wanted to dismiss this as wishful thinking, but there was very little she’d put past Mother.

She resolved to keep Michael from dwelling on that possibility, which would be a challenge, since hope of saving Emma had inspired him to break into Mother’s house. Madeline was not sure she would have dared to do that for any reason, which raised him in Madeline’s estimation.
Nor did she have any desire to see her only friendly contact in prison. Madeline concentrated and found she could see the window Michael had broken, the rifled-through stacks books and boxes and papers. She looked at Michael with what she hoped was a sufficiently enigmatic smile and said, “That shouldn’t be a problem now.”

“What do you mean?” he said, all predictable confusion and astonishment.
She entertained herself with that astonishment for a few minutes, claiming (or perhaps surmising — it didn’t seem an unreasonable explanation, after all) that magic was why Mother had trapped her. “She was afraid of my power. Afraid I would grow stronger than she was,” Madeline said. “I don’t know much, but I know this about the world — most people will feel that way about me. I will be a threat — or a tool. I know you’re different.” Michael was distracted, then, looking around — perhaps she was laying it on too thick.

But no, he was merely worried about eavesdroppers. As she should have been as well. Belatedly, she made a wish that their conversation not be remembered. Especially the part about her powers. “No one will make anything of our conversation,” she said. “I could feel you had secrets to impart.”
After a few more questions, Madeline asked him about his childhood, feigning great concern. Powerful yet vulnerable — enigmatic yet caring. That was her goal.

Pretty soon he was discoursing on loneliness and science fiction novels and “computer” games, whatever those were. Apparently science fiction novels were considered lowbrow literature and they were about Outland, only based on what scientists were discovering instead of folklore. When he began to drift into how he’d done better in school than everyone else, however, embarrassment took him at last.

“Think about what your power could do,” he said, more loudly, mid-thought. “How much good it could do. Just imagine. You could — can you make food appear? Or I suppose rearrange molecules of air or whatever into food? Because if you could we’d never have to hunger again. You’d be able to — oh, I don’t know, build buildings with a thought. You’d put us all out of work, with just a little effort — but that’s just the impetus we need to finally — you’d be everyone’s hero. Do you think that other people could exist with your powers? If you had children, maybe?”

Madeline had not credited Michael with much imagination, but his mind had just leapt ahead of hers — and in a peculiarly unpalatable direction. “That would be to be a slave again,” she said, before he could get further.
He was clearly ashamed, but, disturbingly, he did not give up. Madeline realized further protests would make her look heartless. “Oh, I’ve had such thoughts too,” she said, “but it has to be on my terms.”

Michael set off driving home, badly — wine, she supposed — but, when she reminded him, swung by Verna’s house. She felt almost as gratified as he to see that the window was intact. He wondered about the stuff he’d left lying around, and Madeline reassured him.
Once they got home, she directed Michael to the journals. Even a few minutes’ perusal scratched a deep itch — but clearly Michael couldn’t concentrate (too drunk, too infatuated), so she said, “Show me what you were using them for.”

He grabbed a journal opened to a page with something about the essence of nothingness and explained something about blood from Outland — Madeline didn’t bother following his words; the moment she got alone with these journals, she would read them all and work it out for herself. “I don’t know how I thought of it,” he said, though, visibly wondering at his own ingenuity. “It just came to me in a flash — I still don’t even know if that’s what did it.”
He was proud of himself? Well, she would be even prouder of him. “I love you for it,” Madeline declared, making her voice low and throbbing. “You’re so clever, Michael...”

Of course, he blushed and, stumbling, demurred.
This gave Madeline a new idea, out of one of Mother’s history books. “I have a difficult question to ask,” Madeline said.
“Yes?” Michael said. This unnerved him. Good.
“I think you are wonderful,” she said, “brave and kind and honest and clever.”
“Thanks, I guess...”

Madeline thought how to proceed. In these matters, tone and appearance were everything. “I want — oh, a thousand different things. Always, I want more. But I would love it if you gave me a kiss. Would you do that for me, Michael? You don’t have to love me. I know you love the princess — ”
“But she doesn’t love me!” Michael burst out. “I’d love to —”
“She is a fool,” Madeline said. She didn’t love him! How much more delicious could this get? “You are so brave. You’re a true hero.”

She’d meant to have some fun, but when she kissed him, something else woke in her. In that instant she knew — decided — she couldn’t care less about this human (human, she thought, relishing the word, and realizing that she was, decidedly, emphatically, indelibly, something else).

She knew this because of, not despite, the way his touch made her hunger for his flesh, any flesh, to mark herself on, in. Michael responded, though she could tell it frightened him. So she gave herself to hunger all night long, and he gave himself to her, sweaty, wild, and in a state of remarkable exultation—except that at one point he felt suddenly outrageously guilty that he wasn’t using contraceptives. She didn’t even have to think to know that no life would take root in her without her intending it; she laughed him to silence.

Around four a.m., he lapsed into boring insecurities, so she ordered him to sleep, putting enough of her will into it that he did and pretended to sleep herself as he got up and made breakfast. After an hour or so, she came into the living room nude. She found Michael working away at the television thing — she’d gathered it was the “computer” he played games on — and embraced him.

He trembled in her arms and flushed and began to have trouble with whatever he was trying to do on the screen. She started him on a quite informative monologue about computers and cellphones, and when he finished she showered and asked him about clothing — she would clearly need something other than her single dress, which was almost as worn and dirty as his furniture, or the furniture of the room she had come from. Typically, she was beginning to think, he took her somewhere quite expensive and looked aghast at the price tags of what she chose. She made it quick, though, and emerged satisfied with a rudimentary wardrobe and thanked him with a kiss.

He then protested that he had to go back and work, which she supposed was necessary, so she said she’d explore and let him go. She wandered for a while, stopped into a cafe where she paid for coffee and a donut with a five-dollar bill she willed into existence, got directions to the library. She pulled out travel books and sketched out a map of Prima on her receipt. She read about different kinds of animals and plants. She read about particle physics and relativity, and though she found it difficult to understand, she felt both some pride at having made the effort and a strong feeling that she knew enough catch phrases to impress Michael. She even found some erotica to inspire her with other ways to impress Michael.

After that, she visited sights the travel guide listed and basked in the sun. She ordered a cone of strawberry ice cream, so delicious she could hardly concentrate for a moment. When she got bored, she went home.
She spoke of the city and then changed the subject to Michael’s project. Alas, this inspired him to say she needed a job.
Madeline hadn’t thought about this. She had no intention of doing any of the kinds of work she’d read about, but before she had to come up with an excuse, she remembered her magic. She wished thirty hundred-dollar bills and a bunch of Michael’s packaged noodles (they amused her) into existence, and Michael was duly impressed.

Then, because why not, she had him teach her programming, and she was happy to discover that, even though he seemed to think it intimidating, everything he taught her was straightforward. It was enough of a slog she decided she’d do no more of it, though, and as soon as she thought she could, she proposed they make love instead. But, though she impressed him still further, the frenzy of the night before did not return.
The next day, she reminded Michael of magic journals.

She read ahead by night. Apparently, she didn’t need sleep either; it seemed she’d only done it for lack of better things to do. She found three journals that were interesting; one was very interesting indeed. She showed it to Michael only once he’d bored himself looking at Mother’s endless goddamn tables. And somehow he found maybe the one page she’d skipped in that journal, one describing Madeline’s own magic. She seized it from him and read it — Mother’s usual obscurantist gobbledegook, but she memorized it anyway. Then she tore it up, in case Michael would study it and learn something about her magic from it that she didn’t understand.

He protested, but she explained that Mother was cold and terrible (true) and that she was fairly sure her magic was nothing but a strong will, which, however true it felt, seemed unlikely to her. He looked reasonably convinced, fortunately.

She handed the journal back and watched his face as he looked at the list at the end of it:
Katherine: Heart
E. creature: Spirit
Emma: Mind
Madeline: Body

“Body, eh?” Michael said with a bit of a laugh. “I — I feel awful saying this, and Emma’s — sweet, and good, and kind — but you have more of a mind than she does — just from watching you, I’m positive of it.”
Madeline was startled to hear such objectivity from him, but she didn’t let it show. Instead, she said,“I don’t like it. I don’t know what it means, but I don’t like it.”
Michael asked, in a perfectly everyday way, “Can you wish to know and then know? Or does your magic only work with physical objects?”

“Oh!” said Madeline. A laugh even escaped her. She would have to be careful around him. The moment he said it, she knew that she did have the power to know, and the moment she used it, the entire plot fell into place in her mind. Some of it, she knew, she must, must, must not tell Michael.
Michael, of course wanted to know. Madeline decided she could let her anger show. She said, “What? Oh, only that she was going to kill me and use my body to house all these other people.”

“Oh!” said Michael, duly horrified, but he went on to add, “that doesn’t even make sense, though. Why would she want to do that? Are you sure?”
“Positive,” said Madeline. “Excuse me.” She walked to the bathroom and pretended to throw up, because this was, she was fairly sure, the sort of revelation that would distress most people as well as infuriating them.

“Are you all right?” he called in.
“What do you think?” she asked and added, putting a tremble into her voice, “I’m honestly scared sick of what will happen if she finds me. Will you hide me, Michael?”
“Of course,” he said. She could almost see his chest puff out. He was far, far, far too easy to manipulate. She would have to find — oh, lots of people. Interesting people. Strange people. She was wasting herself.

“I wasn’t good enough for her,” Madeline said haphazardly, plaintively. Mother had done something to her mind that made it refuse to hold what she’d just found out about her childhood—she would have to undo that. She made one effort to do so, and she almost threw up for real. Later, then.
“How could you not be —”

“Oh, you don’t know Verna,” Madeline said. “I mean, you know her, but you don’t actually know her. Nothing short of perfection is good enough for Verna. She wanted me to rule the country. She thought I would be the perfect ruler. Only I’m a mere mortal. So she had to choose better people, perfect people to replace me. The only thing that suited her about me was my appearance.” She looked over herself.

“Ruler?” Michael said. “Of the Kingdom?”
“Where else of?” Madeline said. “Evernost?”
Michael flinched.
Madeline thought she saw something of Emma in his face. “I’m sorry,” Madeline risked saying. “I’m not a romantic like Emma was.”

“No, I wasn’t...” Michael began, but he trailed off. This had started him on the wrong train of thought. He asked fitfully, “What do you know about her? Is she still alive? Is there any hope of finding her soul? Since Verna meant to —”
Madeline wished to know, and — was confused. Disturbed. But yes, Emma was almost certainly alive, just in a different time and place and manner. “I hate Mother,” Madeline said. “Hate her more than anyone else in the world. I don’t think anyone but me could understand how much I hate her. I don’t even understand.”

He was not to be distracted. “What —” he said.
“Emma,” she said, “is dead. Mother meant to capture her soul but failed. Now she’ll have to find a different mind for my body.”
“Not once we’ve arrested her,” Michael said.
Of course he wanted the law involved. Why? Didn’t he want to overthrow the government? “On what evidence? I’m not revealing my powers.”

“If we find something in the journals — we already have, really. Then I just have to admit to breaking in —”
“Don’t be an idiot,” said Madeline. “This isn’t worth sacrificing yourself for. Trust me, we’ll get our revenge somehow.”
Michael put his arm around her, so she kissed him. “This will all be for the best,” she barked out, physical tears, to her pride, on her face. “We’ll make it for the best.”

Stars

She asked to be alone then — there was only so much sex she wanted to have, now the hunger had passed, and she figured the shock of learning Mother’s plans would make it seem natural, not like she was cooling toward Michael. Instead of sleeping, she thought, sitting by the window and gazing out into the hazy sky. There were stars, Michael had explained, in the country, and as a boy he’d enjoyed looking up at them and wondering what was out there.

Madeline could remember stars too, in dizzying dark expanses—maybe from an astronomy book, there had been one or two of those, but she didn’t think so. The night sky felt too immediate, cold air on her face and wild ecstasy like looking into the sun, or her first night with Michael, or flying.

Flying. The more time she spent here, the clearer it was to her that she had the brains and power to make something vast of herself. It made her hate Mother more, and also understand her better — of course Mother would have feared what Madeline would do. What she’d told Michael seemed likelier than it had as she’d said it.
She just didn’t know what to do with her power.

She hated the idea of becoming the slave of the world, making food and curing illness and so forth. People would love her, for sure — but Michael was already teaching her that gratitude got dull, and puppyish approval, and all the things she might expect if she were to do that — and, her reading suggested, even more people than loved her would take her for granted and get angry with her the moment she ceased to perform perfectly, as if she had to do anything at all. A supremely unrewarding effort.
She thought of wiping out the Kingdom entirely — fun, but a waste.

She wanted to rule it, probably. That had to be manageable. In fact, preliminarily, pressing her face into the screen, feeling the cold of the glass outside it on her cheek, she wished to rule, and felt — that feeling, it came more and more frequently — that she would.
She thought of a few scenarios that would bring her to the head of the Kingdom, and, when she got bored of those daydreams, she thought about how she would find Mother and have her tortured and killed. But she realized, after a half hour, that the Kingdom was far too small for her.

She thought then of the Sun and the stars and the mysteries beyond the Wall. She would explore Outland. There would be none of this mindless fear if she ruled. The outside would be hers.
She thought of Michael. She’d been assuming she’d leave him behind, but now she visualized his face if he found himself in a place of power, if she could make him great. And the thought filled her with laughing delight.
In the meantime, he might give her a start. He was a communist, a member of a growing, if still small, political movement. Suppose she were to take it up. Suppose she were to make that dream of his come true — he only thought he admired her now.

Resolved, she asked him at breakfast that morning, with her most ingenuous expression, to tell her about communism.
Predictably, he rambled on chaotically, and when even he realized what he was saying was useless, he thrust three books at her. These, of course, Mother had given her long ago and she had them all but memorized, but she skimmed through them as Michael worked. When Michael mentioned his communist discussion group that night, she agreed, thrilled.

The meeting was populated by a species of idiot inferior to Michael’s, but she charmed them. Michael spoke up in favor of gradualism — couldn’t he see that was the last thing to say here? — so she cut his argument down as succinctly as she dared. “We need to move soon,” she said. When they revealed they had no funding, she saw the moment to move. One quick effort of will, and she had a checkbook — Mother’s checkbook, to be precise — and was writing them a check for ten thousand dollars. She spent a moment or two covering her tracks then left, placating an awed, protesting, insecure Michael with parodies of the other members.

The next day Michael was supposed to have a magic lesson with Mother. Madeline was excited to see what Michael thought of her now and what happened—although there was some danger Michael would reveal their secrets. Madeline wished he wouldn’t in every way she could think of.

Bored and surfing the internet, she learned that Mother had disappeared — leaving a note that they would all be grateful to her someday. She asked the universe and learned that Mother was living in Helsom, back under her birth name, Veronica, and being the most inoffensive spinster/almost-madwoman imaginable, though there was something odd about the information she couldn’t pinpoint. Madeline turned on the news to listen more, then read some, then wandered the city (Michael had given her a copy of his key), then came back and read more, until at 9:30 a panting, pale Michael arrived at the door.

She cut short his nervous gabbling by indicating the radio, which was still discussing Verna’s confession to Emma’s murder and total disappearance. She’d done it quite dramatically enough to suit anyone’s ideas of a sorceress. They talked a little as Michael made himself dinner (Madeline claimed to have eaten already), and finally, Michael asked, “Did you do it — make her disappear?”
Madeline said, truthfully, “I don’t know. I wished very much that she wouldn’t find out our secrets at the meeting, and I think perhaps this is how the universe obliged me. There are many ways to make a wish come true. I must be cautious.”

“You think?” said Michael, clearly much more impressed by this than she was. “If it can really be like that — have you read the Monkey’s Paw?”
“No,” said Madeline.

“It’s a kids’ story,” Michael said. “Just — this Outlandish artifact — the paw of this demon that was called a monkey — is sold to someone because it supposedly grants wishes, and it does, only in the most awful possible way. So someone wishes for money, and he gets it from insurance for his son’s death. That kind of thing. You have to think hard about the steps as well as the outcome, I guess. I mean, I don’t think your magic is cursed or anything like that, just—”

Madeline tried not to roll her eyes — she should have known better than to think she would get anything practical out of him. “I know my magic,” Madeline said. “I’ve been using it all my life and nothing like that has happened. This with Verna is good.”
And, of course, he immediately and irritatingly agreed. “Yes — I suppose you’re right. All you have to do is find her, and then we can call down the law. This is important. Really important. You have the answer — revenge on her and everything.”
“I suppose,” Madeline said. Hadn’t they been through this before? “But I don’t want to do it that way.”

“What do you mean? It seems just about perfect.”
“I told you — it’s as much society as Mother I hate. I think that’s true of you too,” Madeline said.
“But Verna killed — she killed my friend! She hurt you! I think —”
He thought he was angry? “You’re letting your heart get in the way, Michael. Of course I want to crush her into the dirt. It’s the only thing that’s kept me alive all these years. But we could do so much more with it. Verna will wait. I know where she’s hiding and what she’s doing. Wouldn’t her arrest be an astonishing coup for a communist government?”

She had hoped for astonished delight and excitement. Instead? Anxious carping. “What communist government?” Michael asked. “No one — and I repeat, no one, votes for communists. We’re a tiny minority. A hated minority. You’re trying to convince me —”
“I am telling you, Michael,” said Madeline, “I want all of this to happen very much. And when I want things to happen, they do. Sometimes I have to work with my body to make them happen. Sometimes I work with my magic. But they happen.”
And, since that wasn’t enough to stop the carping, she distracted him with sex.

Over the next few months, Madeline began working and wishing to rule the Kingdom in earnest. First she got a job doing publicity for the communist party — it was a sort of fun, and clearly no one competent was there to do it. She got Michael to do computer things, and Michael seemed to enjoy himself even more than she did. Then she came up with a green-eyed blonde to be and ran for mayor of Helsom. She wanted to be the mayor of Helsom very much indeed — she loved the idea of stealing Mother’s dream right from under her nose.

Something niggled in the back of her head: what if Mother figured out who she was? Madeline wished that Mother wouldn’t, and that Mother would come to a bad end. But even though she felt the wishes taking effect, she didn’t trust Mother.

Michael didn’t like Alison Montgomery—he wanted Madeline. And Madeline didn’t blame him. Montgomery, as Madeline had designed her alternate identity, was rather too round, didn’t have great skin, and smiled at the drop of a hat. In addition, Montgomery was, to the best of Madeline’s ability, kind and diplomatic as well as efficient—something Madeline had decidedly not tried to be until this point. So Madeline, just to tease him, always had internet dates with him as Montgomery and not as herself. She enjoyed watching his irritation.

Madeline enjoyed running the city too. She made changes, ranging from drastic and brutal to simple and pleasant for all concerned, all with her silky-sweet smile. She fired the incompetent and the people who doubted her (it was easy enough to make them look incompetent), expressing the greatest of sympathy for them. Within a few months, Helsom was richer, better-groomed, had an art community, and had had substantial improvements in its schools.

As the weeks progressed, Michael grew more distant—and more himself at the same time. Likelier to go off about magic and less likely to drool over her. (She avoided talking magic with him, mostly because she was a little afraid of what would happen if he got too powerful. He didn’t seem to be making much progress as it was.) Was his memory that short?

In the meantime, Madeline began to make inquiries into the Assembly as well as continuing her work to build the repute and popularity of the Party. After wishing for some catastrophe she could rescue the Kingdom from, there were tornadoes, she donated a bunch of money, about half of which she had simply created, to disaster relief.
Tornadoes aside, it was a strange year. Droughts and floods of rain took place in adjacent counties, and the weather forecasters could make nothing of it. Madeline suspected her weather-working was to blame, but she couldn’t work out how and didn’t care enough to use magic to find out.

That was the year Madeline began her magical experiments in earnest, though. Magic was draining, she learned, but not draining enough that she couldn’t do quite a lot of it if she cared to. She found that doing things was, on the whole, easier than knowing things, though neither took too much effort. She could read minds, bend minds to her will, create matter out of thin air (Michael had once harped on the impossibility of creating something from nothing and insisted that she probably was rearranging the air at a molecular level to do this rather than simply making things; she didn’t know and didn’t care). She could bring about quite complicated chains of events.

Most exciting, there seemed to be no limits to her powers, or, rather, the limits of her powers were her imagination and her will. The reason, she assured herself, that she couldn’t, say, control every single Kingdom citizen’s thoughts at once (her limit so far seemed to be five people for about three hours — she’d gotten the same annoying piece of music stuck in each one’s head) was that she couldn’t care that much about what was happening with and to that many people at once or fit them all into her head. She did, however, do broader but also simpler mind-control in which she wished passionately that everyone who read about her work would love it — and from that day onward, money poured into the group.

The hardest thing she had ever done was open the door out of Mother’s basement room and escape into the ice, and the difficulty in the ice was that it had sapped her will.
The one thing in the Kingdom she had so far failed to do, apart from total mind control en masse, was get useful information about Outland. But she suspected that this was simply because she hadn’t cared enough to do so yet. She had it on her list, but right now it seemed more important to gain public support for her run for the Assembly.

She decided to aim straight for the Assembly, skipping regional governorship. She’d forged a thoroughly plausible diploma from Prima University (supposedly, she’d graduated with high honors in political science and history — she figured that was about the “curriculum” Mother had given her anyway) and a past involving an older woman whom, she hadn’t the least doubt, she could magic into telling the world she was her grandmother. Madeline had talked to her once already and given her a great many simpering-sweet childhood memories of “Alison” (she’d read quite a bit beforehand to figure out what childhoods were supposed to be like and then made everything about ten times more pleasant and happier).

By the time elections rolled around, her campaign was well funded. Once again, she seriously considered forgetting about Michael, but she decided not to. She was annoyed that she apparently hadn’t made a deep enough impression on him and wanted to change it. So she decided to. (This in itself, she was fairly sure, meant that she would succeed. Her magic worked deep in reality, and whatever her intentions were, she realized, tended to come to fruition even when she wasn’t consciously trying to bend reality to make them do so. And magic only ever seemed to get easier.)

She was not surprised—there was not even the thrill of victory—when she was elected. She didn’t take Michael’s congratulatory phone calls, but, at midnight, she slipped past the guards, stepped into a restroom, put on her Madeline body instead of her Alison body, and went to Michael’s apartment. She knocked on the door at midnight. When a peevish, though clearly still wakeful, Michael came to the door, she seduced him and made him as happy as it was in her to do.

Assembly was a bore. She couldn’t stand the procedural rules, she hated the speeches, she especially detested the bright, arrogant young Alec. Alec was clearly used to being the smartest, handsomest, most popular person in the room, and clearly thought she was an ill-bred upstart idiot. She loved making a fool of him with smiling, innocent questions and threatening noises about his love life (he seemed, from her discreet inquiries, magical and otherwise, to have dated a man once, which decidedly did not fit in with his public image. Madeline intended to use that knowledge to its full effect when the time came.)

She told Michael about Alec on the phone one night (she tried to visit him in person once a month, neither more nor less, and always in the dead of night, in her real body), and Michael, it turned out, hated Alec almost as much as she did. Alec had used to bully Emma and still bullied him. She spearheaded a few reforms but knew that she would not even want to be Prime Minister under the current regime even if she could. Something drastic was needed, and soon.

Conspiracies

The current Prime Minister, one Tom Reeves, was a bright man, though neither as bright nor as arrogant as Alec, and also inclined to be on her side.
Magic told Madeline certain folks in the Assembly had supported Mother because of the open-border movement — they Mother’s her magic had been strengthening the border — so Madeline finagled an invitation to a meeting with the most adamant of closed-border advocates, including Reeve, who invited her to a late-night meeting in his own study, swearing her to secrecy.

She sipped at the whiskey he offered her, neat and polite in her skirt and jacket. Alcohol did not make her drunk.
Reeve spent the first half hour probing her feelings about the Border, and censorship of Evernostian literature (he seemed to be in favor, something that she knew by now would raise the hackles of pretty much all the public). She took a hardline closed-border position herself, relieved that before this she hadn’t seen fit to weigh in strongly on the issue before.
“We’ve seen the dangers of Outland, after all,” she said.

“Here,” Reeves said, “is what people don’t realize. The rates of contaminants everywhere are rising, and the border is moving slowly — very, very slowly, at a rate of about an inch a decade — inward. Not the wall, of course — you know, I’m sure, that it doesn’t follow the Wall exactly, that there are safe zones outside the Wall and the occasional unsafe zone inside. Scientists have found this. It’s been happening for the past fifty years, at least.”
“Why?” said Madeline. This was news to her. She’d seen the occasional reference to contaminants in the newspapers and so forth and took them for some kind of pseudoscientific garbage — seemed like the best scientists did.

“No one knows,” Reeve said. “There are many theories, the likeliest of which is probably that the contaminants simply move naturally off and on across the ages — or that the Kingdom is slowly being worn away by natural processes. But personally, I sometimes think it may have been happening since censorship was lifted — you’ll think I’m paranoid, but Verna believed that even thinking about Outland brought it closer.”
Madeline made magical inquiries about this and received — annoyingly — no information. She couldn’t even get her head around what the contaminants were. She would make more sustained efforts once she got home.

“What can we do?” she asked, saving her other question, why do we care, for later, because clearly these men cared a great deal.
Reeve laughed, short and wild. “Not much, according to pretty much any scientist worth his salt. The magicians, on the other hand...”
“The magicians?” Madeline concealed the avidity of her attention under an air of mild skepticism—which was the view she wanted to propagate about magic, for now at least.

“Have you heard of Verna De la Mare?”
“She’s some sort of royal aide,” Madeline said, assuming a puzzled air, “who confessed to the murder of the princess and is still wanted. I assume she’s a magician, then? But how —”
“The business with the princess,” Reeve said grimly, “was nowhere in our intentions. We do not know what she is doing now, and the longer her silence, the likelier it seems she is a madwoman and nothing more.”
“But she seemed something more,” Madeline prompted.

“Oh, yes,” Reeve said. “She had a hundred magic tricks she showed us, and we couldn’t find a single expert who saw the mechanism. She certainly seemed like the real thing. And she said she could keep Outland out for good. That she’d been dreaming about doing it for decades. That she knew just what we knew and felt its inevitability, unchecked, and had every desire to check it. And, what’s more, she had a plan to do so.”
“Which was?”

“She says that this world works only partially according to the physical laws the scientists have observed in it. Indeed, the scientists themselves admit it — you know, I’m sure, that they haven’t yet found their Theory of Everything.”
“No, indeed,” Madeline said.
“She says that the magicians work in the gaps in the rules,” Reeves explained. “I frankly don’t understand it myself, but in the magical half of the world, as it were, the land is in some real sense married to its ruler, or one with its ruler.”
“Oh?” Madeline permitted herself to sound more skeptical still.

“She said if we could topple the monarchy, she would supply a king or a queen, and, somehow, because of the nature of this person and because of the power of the connection between land and monarch, this would seal out Outland for good. Or at least for much longer than would otherwise be possible.”
“Oh,” Madeline said, allowing herself sarcasm. “This is what you put your faith in?” She felt almost as skeptical as she sounded. She was positive Mother was mad, for one thing, between all the things she almost remembered and everything Michael said. This was just the fuzz-brained ass-backwards ways that Mother went about doing magic.

Madeline thought about it and felt she could likely block out Outland if she wanted to, which she didn't.
“You don’t understand,” Reeve said. “Where the contaminants go — it becomes like Outland. There’s a forest in the northwest that someone once got lost in and never came out of — and the Wall in that area is intact. And what is even less widely known is that this year the rate of creep of contaminated ground and the rise in contaminants generally has skyrocketed. So it’s more urgent than ever that Verna do her magic — and she has, instead, committed or believed that she committed murder and disappeared.”

“How do you know that killing the princess wasn’t in her plan?” said Madeline.
“We don’t. That’s...kind of the problem. That is further than almost anyone would have been willing to go on such flimsy evidence. But some of us think that was exactly what she intended to do all along. That it will somehow break the bond between the country and the current ruler, enabling the next ruler to sit more firmly, or — I don’t even know.”

“Almost anyone?” Madeline prompted. “That suggests that someone would have been willing to go that far.”
Reeve was silent for a long time, and then he said, “Some of us have lost relatives over the Wall. I can understand those who would say it is better to destroy one life than the many lives that would be so lost if — if Outland consumes us.”
Madeline vowed that she would not run in blind panic from Outland when she ruled. She would learn what it was before deciding any policy whatsoever regarding it. But, in the earnest, haggard look of Reeve’s brown eyes, she saw an opportunity.

She quirked a smile. Some papers she judged to be relatively unimportant went up in flames. As soon as they were consumed, the fire ceased.
“What in the —”
“Verna,” Madeline said, looking him straight in the eye, “is not the only person you know with magic.” At this more a book burst into flames.
“Stop!” he cried. “Stop!”

“Nothing important,” she said calmly, “and it won’t spread. And before you call the police, if I were you, I’d hear me out. I might be able to help you.”
The awe and hope in his expression almost equalled the terror. He pinched himself, presumably to make sure he wasn’t dreaming. Thoughtfully, she let her body return to the shape of Madeline.
Reeve gaped. “Who are you? What in the?”

“You won’t tell anyone,” Madeline ordered. He flinched as she let him feel the force of her order lock down in his mind. “Of course, if you get around that somehow, I can just combust some papers you care about more. My name is Madeline. Verna is my mother.”
“Your — how did you impersonate —”
“Alison Montgomery never existed. I merely convinced a nice lady to be my grandmother. I can be convincing, you see.”

“Oh,” said Reeve weakly.
“I’m on your side, though,” she said. “Mother hated Outland, and so do I. I have every respect for Mother — although, as you can see, I’m stronger, and our methods differ.”
“What do you want?” Reeve asked, sitting up straighter.
“Your help,” said Madeline. “I, you see, am the one Mother wanted to rule. I am the fruition of her plan.”
“I thought you were a communist. Isn’t the ultimate goal no government at all?”

“I will, ideally, eventually become as much of a figurehead as Alexander V is in actuality now,” she said. “I am flexible about the specifics, though. Outland is a more immediate threat than the bourgeoisie.” She allowed the slightest wryness to slip into her voice. “I will rule as well as I can, and I will secure your borders. For good.”
“For good? I suppose, your heir?”
Madeline smiled very slowly. She glanced around and said, “You wouldn’t happen to have a gun, would you?”
“What?”

“Oh, well,” Madeline said. She handed him a gun she’d created herself. “Kill me,” she ordered him.
“Are you trying to get me arrested?”
“I am trying to answer your question: will an heir be necessary?” He continued to hesitate, so she willed him to point and pull the trigger. He did. The gunshot echoed. Madeline gasped in pain as the bullet hit her throat and even let out a little shout. Then, without her even having to make the effort, the bullet rolled onto the ground and she stepped backward. She smiled at him again. “You see?” she said.

“People will have heard —” he protested.
“No,” Madeline said. “No one will have heard a thing.”
“You’re a nightmare!”
“I am a nightmare who wants to do everything for you,” said Madeline. “I want to do what my mother sent me to do. You say she is mad, and she is. I do not even believe that she really killed the princess. She said that for reasons of her own, or perhaps she convinced herself of it.”

She tried a few more demonstrations and mental manipulations, and shortly Reeve was shaking and begging for her help. She didn’t indulge her sudden urge to make him kneel to her, but she smiled yet again, listening in her mind to his fear and weaving it into loyalty. She would have this man as an ally. “You won’t,” she said idly, “believe that any of this happened, you understand. I merely identified myself and explained myself, and that is all. And you said yes. I don’t want everyone to know about my power yet.”

He nodded, realizing that even what he remembered was in her power, and her smile widened.
“We must rule. You must vote — as soon as you can — to remove the monarchy, and I will do everything I can to fill the void. There may be bloodshed,” she said. “But I will do my best to avoid it.”
He did not think this possible. He spoke of incrementally convincing people to remove even the monarch’s last powers without officially removing the monarchy. He spoke of all sorts of things. Madeline did not pay any attention.

When he tried to pull out his phone to snap a shot of her, clandestinely, when her back was turned, she pulled it out of his hand. She’d known he would try something like that — felt it from his fear at her mind control. “If you don’t want me to hack into this and read everything on it,” she said, “I would recommend you do nothing of the sort again. I don’t want to be documented — until I do. I’ll know.” Though she’d never tried to make a wish to know if something happened indefinitely in the future before, she made the effort, and thought she felt it taking.

Before she left, she became simpering Alison Montgomery again, wished Reeve a good evening, and went home.

Victory

Reeve was, thanks to Madeline’s wishes, wrong about voting to end the monarchy. He found allies in people he’d never imagined, and the opposition (especially Alec) found themselves spouting their most offensive privately held views on television, and a well placed rumor about Alec's love life began to circulate.

Madeline crept into the castle one night to watch the king sleep, gaunt with grief for his daughter. Even now, after Mother confessed to murdering his child, he did not hate Mother. He thought her tragically mentally ill and possibly not even guilty. Despite being the impression Madeline had given Reeve, this was so offensively wrong she could not stomach seeing someone come to it spontaneously.

He hated even the small amount of power his position required him to exercise and avoided taking any stances whatsoever. She nearly killed him as he slept. Instead, she made a wish, and the next day, he came out with a sincere and passionate defense of the aristocracy, filled, however, with a contempt foreign to him — a contempt even his unwise daughter would have shrunk from. (Emma had gotten away with such statements, at least sort of, two or three years ago, but she’d been little more than a child, and never taken seriously; besides, thanks to Madeline, the times were changing.)

Madeline was Prime Minister by the end of the year. At the same time, the monarchy was officially abolished. When she received news of her double victory, she called Michael and then bodily transported him to her house. It was the first time she’d done that, and she found it exhausting — almost as exhausting as her now-continuing efforts to understand Outland. People were complex.

She found him a spot in the government’s internet security, over his protests that he wasn’t good enough. This, Madeline saw, after speaking with a few current experts, was not quite true. He was not the genius that the best of them were, but he was considerably better than the worst, some of whom were in positions of authority. She appointed him head over his protests, and soon he was doing admirable work and learning quickly, though deferring embarrassingly to his predecessors. She then persuaded him magically after considerably more intense protest to amp up surveillance (she sometimes thought Michael was made entirely of scruples).

Next, she created a large, gelatinous black monster out of thought and rage and sent it to terrorize Helsom. It ate a woman who’d run against her for mayor before the army blew it up with a bomb. Then she created a swarm of strange insects to come “over” the Wall. These were eliminated with insecticide. She blamed the weather patterns on the encroachment of the contaminants, which she made as public as she could. It helped that the encroachment continued to increase at exponential rates.

Shortly, she succeeded in declaring martial law. She accused certain groups of open-border radicals of deliberately breaching the Wall. They denied this, until she’d worked on their minds. She considered sealing off Outland, but she still relished the challenge of trying to understand the contaminants, which she imagined she was beginning to do, so she did not.
She increased the number of people she controlled at once to the whole population of the Kingdom, but she could not focus on them all the time. For a while she merely wished them compliant, but two weeks of brainwashed zombies annoyed her, so she loosened her hold again, slowly. Conventional ways of ruling were more entertaining.

Michael, meanwhile, could surveil all electronic communication. She ordered reports made of what was done. She had wished, long before, to know at once if anyone was plotting to betray her. Now, one morning, she felt that Michael was. A thrill of anger and curiosity in her, she looked into his mind and found not admiration or even fear but rather violent rage. Nothing but violent rage, a rage that could almost equal hers — and grief.

She demanded the universe tell her why, and she learned that Michael had found out about one — one insignificant arrest — she had made based on the information he’d given her. One. And that was enough to destroy everything she’d done for him in his mind? He was an idiot among idiots, and she was too enraged to be prudent and kill him at once without confronting him first. As a result, he created an internet virus that revealed every horrible thing he could think of about Madeline, down to the fact that Alison Montgomery was a lie.

And the number of horrible things astounded her — she hadn’t realized how many lies he’d caught her in — or how much he cared about that. She hadn’t realized that in the few seconds she’d waited, he’d found out about more arrests. She hadn’t realized how well he’d known she could manipulate minds. Nor had she realized that there was anyone in the country left, after the arrests, who would believe him.

He died of a curiously painful “heart attack” the moment the virus spread. She arrested and killed his entire department.
