Then she told, on the radio, the affecting story of how she had fallen in love with Michael — and he with her — and how she had made his communist dreams come true — and how she had helped him to one of the highest positions in the nation — and how he had betrayed her. She said, “He’s an ingrate and a psychopath.”

Well, people’s reactions to the virus and to the magic he’d been doing too (somehow, he’d figured out a little more magic, and he’d used magic as well as programming to send the copies of his story from computer to computer) at least made things a little more challenging. She was, by the time she finished mopping up those difficulties, rather proud of herself and almost grateful for his defection.

The next obstacle, though, was not so easily accepted. One afternoon, as she paced up and down, muttering plans to herself, she realized that all this time, there had been no sign of Mother, not even when the monster came into Helsom. She asked where Mother was — and the answer, which she received only barely, was Outland.
“Outland!” she said aloud, and ripped the strap of her overalls entirely off.

The very next day she declared it necessary to research the Outlandish threat and, if possible, act offensively. Perhaps, she hinted, it would be possible to expand past the Wall safely. Create room for the children and grandchildren of the Kingdom. That night, she was reminded that she wanted, really wanted, to go Outland herself.
This was a strange thing to want. The Kingdom was still a challenge. But, as she had known would happen, even the fun of watching people tremble before her had grown dull, though she had hardly begun to exhaust the possibilities of her new position. But with Verna gone, the interest was gone too.

Did she want to want Outland, though? Oddly, she found that she did not actually mind the idea at all, even as she thought of it with all of her mind. She had forgotten how likely things she wanted very much were to happen.

Doom

As she thought this, she felt at once that she was about to be attacked. She opened her eyes, sat up slowly, and saw two young women — about her apparent age, a good bit younger than Alison’s — staring at her, guns pointed at her. She had only just realized that one of them was, against all possibility, Princess Emma, when both shot.
“This is my bedroom, my friends, and my country you ruined.” Emma cried as Madeline fell back, blood spraying around. “I’ve never wanted to kill anyone in my life. You’re the first. And you deserve it. You are the only person I’ve ever known who deserved to die!”

Then both began to shoot at her. They shot and shot and screamed and screamed, and Madeline only laughed.
She took hold of Emma and began to strangle her. Madeline taunted, “So I’m not the only one after all. I always heard there was something strange about royal blood, but I never imagined it was deathlessness. She certainly looks like she might die, but I know better. She’s done that once before —”
The other lunged at her, suddenly, biting and pinching and scratching and punching until Madeline was enraged to find herself tied up in her own blankets. She sent the blankets up in flames.

“This isn’t possible!” Emma told the tall one, the one who could fight, urgently. “This is a nightmare, the wrong universe, we need to get out — wake up — before she calls the guards —”

Madeline looked at the protective way the redhead was looking at Emma, as well as at Emma’s astonishment at the ash surrounding Madeline, and said, “Maybe the blood of the royals isn’t as special as mine. You can’t do this?” She set the hem of the princess’s uniform on fire. Emma was an amusing creature, and the other a great brutish lunk of a girl. This would be fun. So would figuring out how the guards had let them pass.

Unless... Doubt took root in her and grew quickly.
“Tell me who you are and what you’re doing and it will stop,” Madeline improvised.
“I’m Princess Emma—” Emma screamed. “And — and you can’t make me —”
“I’m Katherine,” said the loutish redhead.
“I should have been more specific,” Madeline said. “You must tell me why you’re here. Who sent you. Was it Mother?”

“Who’s your mother?” Katherine yelled.
“You don’t know? She didn’t send you to kill me?” said Madeline. “Oh, do tell me another one.”
The princess started to cry. Madeline made the flame climb and smiled.

“Just stop,” Emma said. “You mean Verna, don’t you?”
“Who do you think I meant, Saint Teresa?” Madeline said and raised the flame again.
The tall one — Katherine evidently — said, “Verna killed both of us.”

“Yes, and took us to Evernost, and then she died!” Emma screamed. “She turned into a statue of mud right in front of my eyes and crumbled away! You think we’re here because of her — you’re madder than she was. You think the princess — the Queen — of the Kingdom has no better reason to kill you than Verna — you’re madder than the Gray Witch and the White Lady together, and if you’re going to kill me you’re welcome to because you killed my father and my friends, and —”

Madeline confirmed with a wish that Emma was telling the truth. She let the flame go out for a moment — and at that, Katherine leapt on her. She nearly lit Katherine on fire when something else happened.
She was in darkness. Katherine pushed her away, and the darkness grew deeper and thicker. Katherine was gone.

gray dawn

Gray Dawn

Verna knew the wrongness before she could walk or speak. Nothing fit, nothing worked, nothing made sense. “Everything has holes,” she told her mother at age four, pale and urgent. She needed an explanation, or at least comfort.

Verna rarely needed comfort, and was not good at asking for it when she did. Her father engulfed her limp body in bear hugs whenever something normal girls would find upsetting happened, or when he found her cute. In those early years, she often seemed cute, in a pale, waifish way. But Verna could not remember once getting a hug when she really needed one.

Her mother could make nothing of these holes, so Verna did not bring them up again and concluded no one saw the holes but her. Other people thought they lived in a trustworthy world.

As a teen, she would explain her “holes” in more sophisticated ways. She would refer to philosophers who considered the principle of cause and effect an unprovable theory or who saw no way to know the most elementary precepts of logic held true. But the uncertainty that was a stern mental discipline for the philosophers was for her confusion like a rock in her shoe or a sickness in her gut.

At five, though, she started clinging to the belief that the world was trustworthy, and she was the one who had holes — holes in her thought where certainty and understanding ought to be, holes in her feelings where love and humor and all the other human virtues belonged.
There was evidence for this. Verna could not find anything she could call love in her feelings for her parents, let alone her classmates. They ignored her, and she them.

School was excruciating. She could not concentrate on the arbitrary things teachers asked her to learn, nor could she find words for the hundreds of questions other students didn't notice. It took her several years to learn to read — though when she did learn, she read constantly in hopes she would understand something better.

But almost as soon as the feeling of holes arrived, well before her fifth birthday, there was the feeling of Something Else. Something whole and golden and true and strong that was to her the sight of water in a desert and (she grew to believe) just as likely a mirage. She thought it might be the sun. She thought it might be God.

When she was seven, she began to pray, sometimes to the God she was taught about, and sometimes to the Something Else — she could not tell if they were the same. Her prayers were about holes, that they would heal shut, that the great golden thing would break through and fill them, that she herself would get through the day without harming anyone or breaking any rule, or at least that she would act more correctly than the day before, or, failing even that, that the devil in her would not make her act worse.

The prayers were in vain, though she felt the healing burning of the golden thing always just out of reach. So Verna continued, in her own eyes, to grow steadily more evil.
She did almost nothing outwardly evil. Sometimes she broke rules in ways no one noticed — or failed, from perversity rather than laziness, to perform the duties she identified. She did, however, do much that was peculiar. She could not bring herself to care about her clothing or her hair, and her parents grew angry when she almost went to school in her pajamas or once again got the comment on her report card that she was unsociable.

She began to read history — not what you learned in school, but the history you found in the back of the library, the history that wasn't censored, not anymore, but that your parents had better not catch you reading. And, reading, she concluded the Great, Golden Something Else was beyond the Wall.

That is, she did what every conservative had feared when Alexander III freed the press: she believed what the Erased Poets wrote about Evernost, and, what's more, she resolved to bring Evernost in.

Well, when she felt (as she still sometimes did) that reality was as full of holes as she was, the Kingdom seemed a great stretch of blood-starved tissue that needed to be reconnected to the rest of the body. When she did not feel that, though, she did not really want to free the whole country. She wanted the goodness of Evernost for herself. But, although she knew others were not as full of holes as she was, she suspected Beyond would be good for anyone. And she knew that, however she felt, one way she could be of worth was to bring down the Wall.

Once she had discovered Evernost, she could interest herself in little else. By asking the right librarians the right questions, she even secured a battered copy of Of Evernost itself, the infamous seventh-century anthology of folktales and poems, which she read again and again, though most of the stories were pretty and slippery, hard to find meaning in at all. It was the theoretical texts she could not resist, and these taught her that heaven, far from being a heresy or a dream or a future, lay beyond the Wall. She sighed over the texts and scribbled notebooks of ideas about them.

The only topic that could occupy her mind other than Evernost was magic. Some girls gnawed their fingernails; Verna made wishes.

Verna did not believe in magic. In fact, she could feel that her spells and wishes and rituals did not work. It seemed, sometimes, a substitute for the Golden Thing that might or might not be God.
More than that, it seemed a substitute for doing the right thing. If she could not make herself admit to something she had done wrong, dropping a vase, say, she could wish the vase back together, and even though it never worked, some part of her thought wishing might be worth something.

Her parents never found out she was interested in Evernost. She did not confide in them and saw no reason to upset them with something beautiful they would see as evil.
They knew about the magic because she felt compelled to do it in obvious ways. They were not religious enough to fear for her soul, but they did fear for her sanity. At first, they punished her or gave her long, serious lectures when they caught her. Then she would stop for a while. But, inevitably, against her will, she would start again.

She found plenty to read on magic. Even blatantly fraudulent accounts (and most of them were) held her interest. They tended to depend on the associations between things that were not naturally associated. Many of the associations were arbitrary. In that, magic was like language that worked backward: instead of the things referred to determining (more or less) the things that referred to them, the things that did the referring changed what was referred to.

She began to track associations in table after table, connecting (for example) phases of the moon in one column to jewels in the next, flowers in a third, and stages of human life in a fourth.
The more she learned about how magic was supposed to work and didn’t, the more it seemed to mirror the way her mind and the world were supposed to work and didn’t.

Regarding how the world worked, she began to study physics — the simpler Jurian physics of force and motion, but also the physics of supposed subatomic particles and of light and of time. She read physics texts a whole three years before she began to understand the math behind it viscerally (in this period, she excelled in school when she bothered to try, which was not often).

By her thirteenth year, she knew, correctly, unemphatically, that she was a genius, and was depressed that it never helped her be good or make friends or believe in the laws of nature or do anything that struck her as worthwhile.
But Verna was not a mathematician or a physicist, because, however well she understood what she learned, she did not believe it. She found it harder to believe in science than in God, because God she could at least imagine.

She was relieved to discover that all too often scientific experiments did not yield results that coincided with the math at all, but this merely strengthened her doubt, whose root was the simple fact that reality didn’t feel like that. Perhaps reality somewhere was like that, but it was not here.
Where did the physics and mathematics come from, if they were so wrong? It would be natural to suppose that they came from the Golden Thing, but she did not think so, because the rules required death, and she did not believe that It did.

Her studies of Evernost told her little beyond emotion. She did not think the emotion was strictly her imagination. It was full of joy and love, and Verna had never experienced either elsewhere.

Her fourteenth year, Verna discovered real magic. She thought about physics and math and holes. She tried some of her rituals and changed them, twisted them, did things wrong, where they felt too good to be true. This is a very inexact description, but I do not think I can give a better one. Sometimes she chose where and how to twist the rituals with counting rhymes, sometimes with equations, sometimes by disobeying complex intuitions. Real magic, she soon found, made her feel dizzy and unreal and ill, and it felt, even more deeply than fake magic had, like cheating. But it worked.

If You Cannot Be Good

Verna suspected magic was evil. She knew what she did with it often was (speeding through chores and eavesdropping on others’ complaints about her). Magic twisted Verna’s mind into strange shapes. It made holes worse, the world grayer and dustier and twistier. She thought she ought to give it up entirely. Yet a glimmer of rebellion in her said she was not good either. She could not be good — she had tried and tried. So good must come of her, as much good as she could make, whether evil was required to get there or not.

She began to think about how to get to Evernost — not by crossing the Wall, it was too well guarded, but from Helsom. The Kingdom felt too fragile and thin to hold anyone in it who really wanted out. She longed and longed to go beyond. But she knew she must not think of herself only.
The conviction grew in her that she could only cross over — at least, in this place, at this time, without a great many preparations — if she were to leave her body behind and become pure soul, that is, pure idea.

Most people could not kill another human being, but she was cold and strange and felt sure that she could, and should, and must. There was the good that would come of evil: she would send others Beyond before she went herself.
She thought of sending her parents over, first, because she owed them a large debt of gratitude for feeding her and clothing her and trying to help her even though they did not know how. But she did not want to. They were small, simple, dull people. They did not go deep enough to get the most out of that other place.

Who, then, did deserve to go? She thought over her teachers and her classmates. Many of them she almost hated, but toward Katherine — she felt strong obligation. Katherine was beautiful and worthy and good, strong and glorious. And Katherine had done so much for her — even for her! Better still, Katherine did these things while loving others properly — she was not like the ones who came to Verna out of proud pity or because they had no one else.

Verna began to lay her plans. First, she turned to alchemy: she needed a gold net that could catch the soul.
It had to be gold because souls, that was, the guiding ideas that made people themselves, were like gold too, and because the place she wanted to send them was more like gold still.
Such thinking, it was true, was not clear and lovely like mathematics. It was, instead, real like the mind.

She worked and worked until her net of old yellow yarn was pure gold, so fine it would go through everything other than an essence — that is, a thing strong and pure as it was.
Then she began to plan murder. She imagined, unwillingly, every night, sliding a knife into someone — there was a relish or rightness in it she dreaded. A few months before Katherine's graduation, she was ready.

She hid in a tree a ways off the path Katherine took (she had taken to watching Katherine, looking for her most vulnerable moments).
She screamed, knowing how Katherine ran to the rescue instead of prudently hanging back, expecting someone else to help. When Katherine came out, Verna did it. A good life awaits Katherine here, some part of her protested at that last moment — but a better life awaited Katherine there.

In Katherine’s last moments, Verna fought a quiver in her hands. She had feared she would be able to kill without emotion, and she was relieved to find this was not true. It remained to be seen whether she would suffer lasting effects, but for now the forest seemed at once too vivid, too vast, and too distant, and her blood pounded in her ears (whether with fear or excitement she could not tell, though).

She pulled her crumpled gold net from the pocket that had held her penknife, tied it around Katherine's head, and pulled. The cloth passed through, bringing with it a ball of light, which Verna cradled in her hands.

Verna wanted the light written across the sky, wrought into the rocks, made an inextricable part of the world.

As she wished this — with all her heart, or, at least, with most of her mind (the wish was surely nothing but cold, meaningless, and twisted sentimentality) — it flared and then shrank. Smiling bitterly, she looked up to the Sun, and then toward the Wall, from which the breeze blew, and — well, she’d taught herself half-consciously to reach since she first felt the Golden Thing, but now, instead, she pushed.

When Katherine's light vanished, she decided her push had succeeded — but she felt a small misgiving. She shrugged it aside as the inevitable cowardice, her fear of being caught. But it returned. It began to hover around her as she washed herself in the stream and lay Katherine’s body in it, where the fingerprints would perhaps wash off. It grew as she donned the unbloodied clothes she had stashed under a juniper bush. It became torment as she dumped the old clothes in a dumpster in a warehouse on the opposite side of town. When she got home, no one was there but Dan, who would believe she’d been at library.

The way the light had disappeared, at the end — that had been wrong. Katherine had not gone to the right place. For all she knew, Katherine really had died. The evil within her had tainted even this deed, which she had planned and struggled toward for months so that it would not go wrong.

Then she wanted to confess. She nearly did when her parents got home. She knew the horror they would feel. They would not believe she had sent Katherine to a better place. They would not even believe she had wanted to. She would confirm their worst fears about her.

She half-welcomed that horror. She deserved it. But the other half of her was stronger. She did not confess. She remained stony-faced and apparently oblivious when Katherine was found in the river, through the funeral — so oblivious that her mother shook her head and her classmates, she felt almost sure, whispered about her coldness and ingratitude after Katherine's protection.

The injustice that they might call her cold stung, though it should not — hers was not a morally pure act, she was at best trying to gain the approval of her conscience. She did not care about Katherine deeply. She wanted to wail, though, that at least she was trying. She was trying to be grateful to Katherine, to be good to her, as Katherine had been to her, perhaps even more so, whether from good motives or not, and even though she almost certainly had failed.

Had Verna had a more impulsive nature she would have killed herself, and she would not have reached. She did not deserve to. But she had power, and a clever mind, and she could not shake the belief that someday, somehow, even if she never became good, good would come from her, and she would trick the devil within her.
It was in this state of mind that Verna finished her sophomore year of high school.

The Love of the Sun

To understand the next part of the story, you must know that as a girl Verna had always liked to escape to a certain meadow in the woods, aglow with sunlight. Sunlight felt like the Golden Thing.

Two years and a great deal of magic later, she lay in the sun, drowsing. She thought, but languidly, of the curdled mass of everything wrong with her. She thought of the job as a nurse’s aide she was supposed to want, since she’d shown no interest in boys and vice versa. That job would be a waking nightmare — the devil in her would drive her to small cruelties. As for large cruelties, she suspected even now she ought to be killing more people and sending them to Evernost, but, irrationally, she could not bear to think of doing it again.

She wanted to escape to Evernost so much that she nearly tried to die at that moment and do it, but she knew she must not. It occurred to her — she was nearly asleep — that the Wall was not the only boundary of the Kingdom. There must be an upper and lower boundary as well. The Sun, surely, was not of the Kingdom but of Outland. That was, perhaps, why she loved it.

She squinted up at it through her hair. I don’t care if I go blind, she thought, but she did. She would find it harder to do good if she could not see, and she was a coward besides. She looked down, then squinted up again, back and forth. She began to feel words in the heat and feel its touch on her legs and arms and chest. She wriggled deeper into the light.

“Dear child,” came the words, sizzling around her face and through her brain, articulated in the interruption of heat by breezes and wisps of cloud. “I’ll do my best for you if you want. What do you want?”

“I want to be good,” Verna murmured.
Deeper in her, she said, “I want you.” The sun and the Golden Thing, if they were not the same, were closer than ever before.

“Hm,” said the voice. “I have watched you all these years. I don’t know if this is worth it, for the sorrow it will cause. I can’t not though, seeing you — do you understand at all?”
Verna shook her head, barely (she did not want to move at all).

Verna did not know what she should do. For once, she did not care.

“Come!” she cried. The cry echoed through the trees, past the trees, past the world, high, feral, miserable. All of her was in the cry, and it was not even human. No one could love such a sound as that. She would, of course, be refused.

“Perhaps — close your eyes — I do not want to hurt you or frighten you — ” said the voice softly, closer still, gentler, not in the least dismayed.
“You won’t,” Verna whispered, but she obeyed, and the heat grew around her. She felt herself lifted off the ground. Heat came from all sides, pressing with a pressure, almost pain, through her dress and her hair, and then —

“Relax, little one. Let me.”
Tears ran down Verna’s face as she tried to understand what he meant. Then it happened, she gave up some control, some fear she had felt always, and there was something in her now as well as outside, touching gently the wounds she had called holes with caressing warmth.
“Yes,” came the whisper. “Yes, like that. Let me — a little more — ”

And the light was filling every part of her, eyes and mind and feelings and thoughts and ideas and muscles and tendons and nerves, in rolling waves of gold, and it was what her mind had thought of and imagined and asked for for years only better.
“Yes. Be a little — softly — be — ”

And she was outside the world, and none of its troubles, of her lifelong troubles, even made sense. She understood everything from an angle she never had before. Her holes were real but they were and meant something entirely different than she had felt. They were failures in a shoddy imitation of something else, another world, a world that did not know Evernost even as the Kingdom knew it. She wondered who had created the imitation but she didn’t care much.

She laughed. “You may remain,” the Sun said. “Leave that behind. I love it too, but you seem to hate it so much. I want you here, with me — I always lose you humans. I don’t want to lose you.”
Verna almost said yes. She could do everything better here, even do good, even be good. From here she could see what she did and was.

An instant’s delight, though, in the labyrinthine strangeness that had been her human mind — and she fell, and fell, and the sun could not stop her fall. She somersaulted down, straining up, trying to seize any trace of what had been.

There was a weight of something in her. She surrounded it with what she could remember of joy and hope — now she felt in the sun only the sweet intensity of physical warmth, her mind cold and dead.
She lay shuddering for the next hour, two hours, three hours, until the sun began to set, and she had to go home.

Her parents saw her covered in dirt and pine needles, late for dinner, not a single chore done (it was eight, and dinner had been at six), and they merely sighed. She apologized, feeling that much of the strange freedom still within her. (Usually she did not dare to apologize, because she knew it would be taken as false, or sound false, or in some sense be false, because she could not care about what her parents cared about enough to feel guilty for the things they thought she should feel guilty about.)

That night, it all echoed inside her, again and again, both before she fell asleep and in her dreams. This, she supposed, was what the other girls giggled about, the source of so many of their excitements. Sex? Love? Verna couldn’t tell and didn’t need to. Something real had happened, real as her magic and utterly different. Perhaps, if she thought about it, goodness would be given her. Perhaps she would create something right from her magic. Perhaps she would learn to give or pass on to others what she had received, or felt she had received.

Doubt remained, but she did not accept doubt in that night's hope-spun dreams of trapezing through the sky, of kissing the sun, of growing bolder and greater in the place beyond, in Outland, in Evernost. She would go there someday, she felt herself deciding. Knowing. Others would too.

She speculated over the following months that the means, or part of the means, might be growing within her. She skipped periods, and though her belly never expanded, something was inside her. She could learn nothing about it, though she tried to warm it with what warmth she had left (over the months that warmth went on fading, leaving behind, though, vague and dusty hope.)

First Birth

During a geometry class whose material she had mastered years before, she knew. She asked to go to the bathroom and fled the school for the woods. She would say she had been sick. She did not want to give birth alone; hope or no hope, she did not think she could be a mother to a child. She wanted (oh, joy) the father to be present. So she found the clearing where she had lain.
Though the sun shone, pale with early spring, she felt nothing in it.
She sat beneath a tree, and at once nausea came over her, and from her mouth came a black something.

At once, she lay flat on the ground, a liquid shadow or puddle of ink before her. She stood, leaned against the tree. She looked at the creature, but doom had made a hole in the bottom of her, and everything was falling into it. What she had created — it was not human, it was not beautiful. Still, she spoke with a ghost of hope: “What are you, creature, and whence do you come? I’ve always been strange, but not, I think, strange enough to give birth to such as you. Even the Sun, it seems, is not a trustworthy source of goodness.”

She realized that with that last sentence, she had cursed this creature. And, sure enough, the creature formed a face, red eyes pointing in opposite directions, skin chalk-white, a maniac grin, a tongue pointing out. It disappeared.
Verna did not cry, because Verna almost never cried. She had loved the Sun, and hoped that he could make good come of her, and instead — a demon —
“I hate you,” Verna said, small and quiet, to the sun above her, and imagined she felt the smallest sadness in response. Then the blackness flew at her, murder in its eyes, and she scampered up the tree.

With a cold, hard laugh, the being, now a humanoid silhouette of opaque black, appeared on the branch opposite Verna. The branch cracked. Both fell.
Verna lifted the fallen branch and limped off, half-hoping the demon would kill her, cursing herself. Everything she did made things worse. She had not once helped anyone. And could she trust the Golden Thing to be anything other than the deceit of demons from across the Wall, the Sun probably the worst of them?

“I’m sorry,” came a clear, cool voice from behind her. She turned around and found a girl, with red pigtails, blue eyes, and a round pink face. Hope stabbed Verna again. This must be the demon's ploy. Still — if she hoped to be good, was it possible a demon could do the same?
“What did you do?” she asked the girl, or the apparent girl. “Are you my child?”
“Yes,” the child said.

“Ah. Then I am responsible for you,” Verna said, looking her in the eyes, trying to communicate some kind of friendship and knowing with almost complete certainty that she was failing. “I am sorry too. I want to do my best for you, but I don’t understand you.”

The child was not put off. Matter-of-fact, she said, “I wasn’t here and then I was.”
“I am your mother,” Verna said, trying not to let herself sound dubious. “I do not think I can be a good mother, but I will do what I can.” She thought of inviting the child home to dinner. She would say — Verna didn’t know what she’d say. She could not help feeling everyone know where the child had come from, which made no sense. She studied the sturdy, clear, healthy-looking little girl before her. “Would you like something to eat?”

“Yes,” the child said after a moment’s pause, “please.”
“If you wait here I’ll bring you something,” Verna said, cowardice winning. “It might take a while. Will you be okay alone?”
“Yes,” Madeline said. Verna stared at her a long moment. She did not quite believe the child. She felt too guilty, for one thing.

Her parents were predictably upset. The teachers had called. She said she’d been sick, thrown up. Her parents did not accept this, as she seemed well and had not come home. But it was as true a thing as she could tell them. Secrets were, of course, nothing new for her, but she could almost see this one widening the gulf between her and them.
Verna waited drearily through the lecture, knowing that there were people who could — even from this place — either fake or feel contrition that would bring them closer together and not further apart, and that she was not one of these people.

They sent her to bed without dinner. She did not want to defy them again, but the child waited. She dug some candy she'd gotten last fall out from under the bed — she didn’t like candy, and would rather have given her daughter something wholesome, but she didn't feel equal to stealing leftovers.
Then she hid herself: she thought, she dredged up some dust bunnies to scatter over her head, she whispered scraps of incantations. It took her two whole minutes to feel the sickening click of magic done.

She crept out of the house, quiet in spite of the magic, and went into the forest. She found her daughter pushing a swing — a wooden plank hanging from ropes on a tree. “I made it for you, Mother,” she said.

“Oh!” Verna loved to swing. She had, maybe, detected a note of cunning in the girl's voice, but she still felt almost ready to cry — wanted, strangely, to hug the girl. “I brought you candy,” Verna said. “I didn’t think I’d be able to make Mom not notice that I was taking leftovers. It’s not good for you, but a lot of people think it tastes really good.”
The child bit the candy bar, squinted for a moment, and then said, “That’s good.”
Verna smiled hopefully.

Over the next few days, Verna grew in courage. She stole all sorts of food and was delighted by the girl’s delight in it. But sometimes the devil in her, or maybe just her homework, made her forget to go. She looked hopefully to the sun — possibly not a demon? Possibly a demon capable of goodness, or capable of giving birth to a demon capable of goodness?

Next — the question of names. The child was powerfully magical, very much her own person. Perhaps she already had a name. Perhaps she feared to share it — demons were supposed to. But what if Verna was merely demonstrating her own uncaring nature? All parents wanted to name their children, but Verna hadn’t thought of it until a week into her daughter's life. Finally, after much deliberation, she went to the library for baby name books, not magical texts. She wrote long lists, complete with magical associations, and chose a neutral name, a name she liked, and the name of a grandmother: Madeline. It was a name of good omen, not the name of a demon.

Verna, the day after, arrived with a batch of badly burnt cookies she had made herself and some chicken salad. She said, “I realized I need to give you a name. Do you know if you have a name already?”
“No,” said the child.
“I would like to call you Madeline,” Verna offered. “I’ve been thinking about it for a week, and I’m very ashamed because I haven’t done it before this, but I like the name. Do you? Do you think it’s a good name?”

“Yes,” said the girl simply.
That was so much easier than Verna had been expecting. Verna wondered how much else would have been that easy — perhaps introducing the child — Madeline — to her family, giving her a warm place to stay — at least it wasn’t winter. Verna asked, “Is there something I can do for you? I feel very stupid, because you must get bored. Do you know how to read?”
“No,” said Madeline.

“I could try to teach you how,” Verna said nervously. “I’m not a very good teacher, though. I try to tell other kids how I do my homework sometimes, and they don’t understand.”
“I’d like you to try,” Madeline said firmly, and timid warmth sprang in Verna.
Verna dug up the books they had used when they tried to teach her to read, the strange, dull ones that had mystified her for years, and then went to the library to find more.

“I don’t suppose you know the alphabet,” Verna said, “or even what an alphabet is.”
“No,” Madeline said.
So Verna explained that the sounds in words were broken up into different visual symbols, approximately one for each sound. She started to go through the sounds.

Madeline learned quickly, and Verna began to look forward to their meetings. She could still feel the holes where she ought to be warmer or more understanding, but at least she was doing something useful.

One Saturday morning, Verna went on a forest walk. She was not looking for Madeline, whom she usually met in the afternoon. She doubted Madeline would want to see her. The child was kind, but Verna was not good company, and Madeline seemed to enjoy her free time. Verna wanted to ask what she did, but she had not yet worked up the courage.

She heard a screech. There was a bird, and over it a shape like black ink lifting up the tiny worm of an intestine from the screaming body. Then both disappeared into the sunny afternoon.

Verna left, as nonchalantly as she could.
For three days, she was too afraid to visit. She was afraid of what Madeline would do to her, but she was also afraid of what she would find out. Perhaps the demon had not been Madeline. In that event, Verna would have to do something too. Perhaps she could be a hero.
If it was Madeline —

She should have asked about Madeline's free time, obviously. The girl, if girl she was in any meaningful sense, had to do something. Reading was not enough to entertain her, though Verna left her new books every time she visited. If she had sneaked up on the child more often, if she’d found magical ways of watching her — “No hope,” she mumbled often through those days. “No hope but is a lie.” She wrote this in a poem as an exercise for class, and also, “Why is no one cursed but me?”

Second Birth

Verna came on the third day. She had devised several spells that she thought could subdue a demon. At least one of them ought to work, though with her luck they would not. She did not know whether she wanted to use them.
She looked at the little redhead and saw something in her expression, the clear coldness of it, that brought to mind both the demon that had tortured the bird and death.

Thinking aloud, as was her habit when she dared, she said “I musn’t let you kill me. You could do so much harm. And maybe I could stop you. If you can’t be good, good must come from you. Maybe that’s the good that will come from me — saving people from you. I don’t want to hurt you, though. If you kill me, do you promise not to kill anyone else? I guess I couldn’t believe you even if you did. I don’t know you well enough to know if you keep promises.”
Verna looked at the child, willing both of them to act.
The child did, in several ways at once.

Verna hoped Madeline would succeed, and the situation would no longer be Verna’s problem. She would not reach for Evernost — that place was cruel as Madeline — though part of her still wanted to. Instead, she’d probably go to hell. She did not know if she believed in hell, but knowing the way things went with her, it would be real. She could not, though she knew it was foolish, believe in a hell worse than this. And in hell, at least, perhaps, she would be harmless.

And, not despite but because of her hope, the devil, or the guilt, or both, forced life on her. All three spells Verna had prepared wove themselves together into something greater, and Verna, dying, in an act she would have thought was impossible, murmured and twiddled and wove everything into life for Verna herself and a solution for Madeline.
Verna stood, got a handful of dust, and called, “Yarrow, full-moon yarrow in the barrow,” and threw the dust at Madeline, who was still a redhead holding a knife. The dust made miniscule holes in both Madeline and knife.

“There we go. I knew something would work after that moment. I musn’t kill you, though. Killing is wrong. I really am a murderer, I see that now, and I don’t want more blood on my hands.”
Madeline began to fight dust, to fight as a black liquid fights with gray death. This was right. Verna broke a piece of bark from the tree and collected the pitch that flowed underneath and around it. She caught the dust and the black non-substance of Madeline with the pitch and began to knead, singing a lullaby laced with magic.

Verna began to make a human shape. When she had something roughly like a baby in her arms (Verna had never been good at sculpting), she bit her tongue and spat. She worked the line, “blood of my blood, flesh of my flesh,” into the lullaby, continuing to pat and push, and then, reluctantly but hopefully (she thought it might be the devil in her commanding her to do so), “sun’s child, full of light.” With luck the light would not prove more evil than the darkness.
One more click of magic done, and there was a baby in Verna’s arms, a real human baby.

“It’s okay,” Verna whispered miserably. “You’re my baby now for real. It’s gonna be okay.”
Hope always came to Verna, and always it was wrong, but she still could not refuse hope. Perhaps, human, the child would be safe and good. And she would treat this child as she should have from the start: she would bring the child into Helsom, into what family she could find. The strength of will that allowed her to do this allowed her also to believe that no one would think the child was hers — not even if she were to claim it was.

When she arrived at the house, sleeping baby in her arms, she faced horrified parents who took her for a kidnapper.
And so, indeed, in some sense, she was, she supposed. At least, she’d stolen the demon from itself.

Madeline in Helsom

Verna assured her parents, blankly and weakly, she had not kidnapped Madeline. They did not believe her. They posted notices all over, they bought formula, and Verna’s mother found some warmth for the infant she had not, in Verna’s memory, ever felt for Verna.
Verna felt a wince of jealousy and sadness, but it was because of Verna’s own awkward coldness this was so. And what if Mother's affection meant that the child was something different than Verna and better?
When no missing infants were found, Mother told Verna she had done the right thing.

Then Mother gave Madeline to the orphanage. Verna, duty-bound, asked her not to. The child was Verna’s curse, and Verna had to take care of her. But Mother explained they could in no way afford a child, and a great many other practical things that almost assuaged Verna’s conscience for wanting the child gone, followed by a passionate promise that Madeline would get a good home, with people who really wanted her.
Reluctantly Verna took to visiting Madeline after school, when she would usually wander the woods or study magic. Even more reluctantly, she began to look into the child’s mind.

She had not known magic could read minds. She supposed it would be wrong to try it on others, though she wanted to. She wanted to find the seed of goodness the others had and she lacked. Perhaps, if she found it, she could emulate it in herself. In the meantime, she must understand if and how this child was a danger to others.

There was much in Madeline she could not understand — perhaps where Madeline was “sun’s child” and not “flesh of her flesh.” She saw no memories of cruelty; this mind could not quite hold memories from the sort of creature Madeline had been. But she saw patterns of cruelty that the child would want to reenact — the desire to hear how something would scream if she pulled it apart. Every time Verna felt such a thing in the child, she tried to make a spell to send it away.

But mind magic was difficult, sensitive, and subtle, and only sometimes did she have the feeling of success. It was almost as a spell itself, though not the kind that worked — more the kind she had done before she had learned to do real magic — that Verna whispered things like, “You’re innocent.” Or, “You will be good. And safe. God will love you as He hates me.”

No one wanted Verna to visit — or, rather, a teacher and a neighbor or two were touched, but when they gossipped about it (and Verna sometimes heard them, eavesdropping magically) they were told that Verna was strange and not right in the head and should not be trusted with a child. Verna knew this was probably true. But she was not sure that the child who could happily pry apart screaming birds should be trusted with ordinary people, either, and so she went.

Madeline the baby had piercing blue eyes and quickly grew wisps of black hair. The ladies in the orphanage would always tell Verna how glad they were that Verna had found the child and what a dear she was. They always wanted to supervise Verna with the child, and Verna encouraged them to. She wanted them to know her intentions were good.

Verna occasionally forgot, but never for more than a week. And every week she prepared more and different spells. Madeline manifestly did not like her, cringed away from her, cried when she came near. Verna took this in stride. It wasn’t like anyone else liked her. She missed the days when Madeline had appeared to like her with high, burning sadness, but that had almost certainly been a ruse.

The next year, Verna finished high school and started work at a local gas station. She was not good at it. She did not like it. She forgot things, grew confused quickly, only sometimes noticed customers, never knew what to do about shoplifters, and did not please anyone. But the owners were friends with her parents, and everyone knew she was “off” anyway, so she did not get fired, and she did like the sensation of earning money and saving it (she still lived with her parents and spent almost nothing apart from her share of the food; she let her clothes get holes and never learned to drive).

Verna’s interest in Madeline increased as the child grew older. What would she be able to do? Madeline turned out to be bright and charming, and the ladies at the orphanage would brag about her — even to Verna. Madeline grew friendly again, though still warily, but Verna knew better than to trust her. She did the small magic that let her see some of Madeline’s thoughts and found that Madeline still despised and feared her. She considered doing magic to change that, but it would be an abominably selfish use of her powers, and she knew little enough of love herself that she did not think she could inspire it in another, not even magically.

Verna’s fears began to center on love. Pastor Jacobs assured his congregation at their Saturday worships that love mattered more than everything. Her heart, like a bell of doom, told her nothing induced by magic would count in that regard. It would not be real. Love had to be given freely.

Verna watched Madeline from a distance as well as visiting her, and Madeline showed no signs of knowing. What Verna saw disturbed her. Madeline did not, indeed, remember her demonic pre-childhood, but she did bully the other girls, and sneakily, and lie whenever it was convenient. Verna did not trust the way everyone — even the surliest matrons, even the least sociable children — seemed to follow Madeline, do what she said, light up when she spoke to them, listen to her every word (and there were many words, even before Madeline was two years old.)

Madeline was growing charming, lovely, glowing — but she was not growing good. Not even, Verna found herself thinking with a cruel spike of pride, as good as Verna could be, which was not very good at all, though perhaps she loved in a way that Verna did not for all her viciousness. The matrons did not see her cruelty. And if her magic were responsible for their love — what would Madeline do as she grew older?

Verna would adopt Madeline.
It was her duty, she reasoned, to protect the world from the demon. Or kill it, something in her whispered, always, at this point in her musings (they ran in circles through her head). She felt sure whatever so whispered was the devil in her, something no good mother could ever have thought, let alone thought with the cold, dead relief she felt at the idea.

She concluded from her fear of murder that it was better to exist and be evil than not to exist. And since the world contained her and Madeline and people like them, was not that itself a sign that God (if He existed, which Verna tended to think He did) felt the same? Well, if she could mold Madeline into the kind of goodness she knew and set her up to experience the kinds of goodness she did not know — Madeline would be good and would exist. The question would be moot.

Verna did not tell her parents. She found an abandoned cabin in the woods and began to fix it up. She knew nothing about carpentry, but with magic and the occasional log was able to fix it so the floors held and the doors shut and locked. She learned through trial and error to fire up the wood stove (fire frightened her, but necessity proved stronger than fear). She cleaned the chimney that took the smoke outside. She drank water from the well for two weeks with no ill effect before deciding it was probably safe, though she’d boil it anyway (Verna was not sure she was human enough that what made other people sick would make her sick as well. She never got sick.)

At the library, she made notes about edible plants. She bought packets of seeds and began to garden. In a grim way, it was pleasant. She rarely had the experience, except with magic and school, neither of which seemed to count, of setting out to do something and then doing it.
She watered the apple tree, and it produced apples. When some of the seeds would not grow, she put magic on them so they would, though the act frightened her, because it required calling on the power of the Sun. She put spells on the house, so that it would not be found by those who wandered past.

She set up two bedrooms, the smaller for her, the larger for Madeline. She repaired the beds in them as best as she could and made Madeline’s into a makeshift sort of crib.

The Afghan

She then pleased her parents by using her crochet needles to crochet and not to make spells — at least, not obviously. She thought of everything a child ought to have that she could not give and tried to work it into the blanket. She summoned every memory in her that even somewhat resembled love (except for those about Evernost, which she tried to keep out), occasionally spitting or burning photographs and using their ashes, but mostly whispering stories and saying words backward or using their antonyms at the magically appropriate places.

She thought of her parents. She thought of Katherine’s splendor and reluctantly included her guilt over Katherine’s fate. She did not want Madeline to be miserable, but she did want Madeline to be moral, and perhaps a taste of Verna’s own morality would give Madeline the same?
The peach yarn belonged to Katherine. She still had not gotten the Sun out of her head — she so wanted to make the yellow yarn his, his beauty, his seeming kindness. But instead she made it about her own father, distant, busy, anxious and a bit afraid of her.

Blue belonged to her brother, blue of gentleness and of the mind. Dan was a bright boy, good with math. Though he was afraid of Verna too, and she rarely saw him, he was kind to her. She murmured fierce words that would weave his kindness and her wish to answer kindness with kindness into the blanket.
Green was Mother’s. Her mother loved her, even though she showed it through worry and disgust. Verna did not feel answering warmth, but she did feel a debt. Even though she half-wanted to hate her mother, she held herself back, and she tried to weave the holding-back in.

Purple became Madeline herself. Verna was afraid of Madeline and for her. Madeline must know some of this, but only some. So she crocheted in her desire that Madeline be good, good as Verna could not be, but also everything positive she could summon up about the child. There was so much. Beauty and forthrightness, cleverness and cheer and charm, she incorporated, and Verna’s pleasure in the reading lessons, though not the lessons themsleves. If Madeline remembered she was a demon, she would become one again.

She wanted God’s love for Madeline and worked that in too, shaving pieces off the wooden Holy Eye her mother had given her for graduation and inserting them in the yarn. But that frightened her, because God brought to mind Evernost and the Golden Thing. So she kept her thoughts there to the doctrine she had learned. God seemed the poorer for it.

She finished the blanket one November night. She had even told her parents it was for the child, though not why, and they were bemusedly accepting. She heard her father joke that night that if he believed in Verna’s magic, he’d burn the thing.
Verna remembered in a flash everything the Bible said about witchcraft.

She did not know if she believed the Bible. Her parents supposedly did, but they did not think about it. She did not see why one should, but, then, believing it was like believing in goodness. One simply did, because it was simply true, and to doubt was itself evil. She found it easier to believe in God, but she realized how little, of late, she’d been taking that belief into account. She began to pray that night, and lay awake all night, praying.

“Let Madeline be good. Let Madeline love. Let Madeline love. Let me love Madeline. Let there be love,” she whispered over and over, as a refrain to her pleas and exclamations, but it was like doing the kind of magic that didn’t work, the kind that was just making wishes. Could it be that if God were making the changes and not she, she would not feel them?

She drifted into more usual prayers, prayers from childhood, for herself, that she would be free from holes and evil.
When dawn broke, she sat up and began to write out spells. Magic was probably evil. But spells came to her without her will’s assent. Determination rose; and faith fell. Within an hour, her fears seemed absurd, and she resolved to use every means within her power to fix herself: it was of new importance that she be less wicked.

She doubted, even in the daylight, that magic could do it. She had tried before, on occasion, but now she felt ashamed of how rarely she had tried, and how weakly. For all her pain over her wickedness, she had not been able to take the practical — magical — steps that would remedy it.
“If you cannot be good,” she told magic itself, “good must come from you.”
She began to work.

She would the tactics she had been trying to use on Madeline use on herself. That would teach her how to teach her daughter. Besides, she shouldn’t ask the girl to suffer something that she herself was unwilling to suffer.
She did her hardest work at the renovated shack. Often she sat very quietly and tried to understand her mind. She tried to listen to her thoughts, to pick out the wicked ones — the cold ones, the lazy ones, the thoughts that would make her an abomination as a mother. The thoughts that would make her warm-blooded classmates shudder or sneer or hate.

She could not keep it up for very long at first, but in time the practice began to clarify her mind even independent of magic. She could catch cowardice and perversity at their roots — on occasion.
Then she began to think of powerful, subtle spells that might shape the person she was. She found spells to neutralize thoughts she did not like, and she used them when she caught wicked thoughts beginning. For the first time, almost, in her life, she felt happy.

Why hadn’t she tried this before?
Still, though her mind was a quieter place and less painful, she did not love. She did not feel the gushes of warmth, the spontaneous outbursts of sympathy, the agile humor of her classmates.

She knew how to change things in minds and eliminate them, but she did not know how to create them — or, she realized, several weeks into thinking about the problem, she did not know what to create. There were so many different ways of loving, so many different shapes of goodness. Any, she felt, would be untrue to what goodness might remain in her — she entertained with a thrill the idea that there might be goodness left in her that she had not ruined, or potential goodness that she had not made actual. She did not know where to look for the roots or seeds of goodness in herself, so she went to Pastor Jacobs.

Respite

Pastor Jacobs had a round, bald head with tufts of gray hair above the ears, and round glasses. He looked at her with interest. He knew who she was, of course — in Helsom, everyone knew everyone and she had eavesdropped magically on her mother’s conversations with him before. They were dull, everyday things and all too often about Verna’s inadequacies — as a companion, as a student, as a help around the house, as a future wife.

Mother felt sure now that no one decent would marry Verna. This bothered her acutely (and she agreed with Verna that the idea of Verna’s having children was...unfortunate). Pastor Jacobs always responded with calm sympathy.

Verna went over what she would say again and again in her head. She decided she must cut to the chase. She wrote out different questions she could ask. She still had not chosen one when she went in.

“Well, hello,” he said, inviting her into a room with a fire burning in the fireplace. Verna shuddered at the fire, having recently nearly burned down her shack with a wrong symbol. “Sit down, sit down,” he said eagerly. “I feel sad I’ve never gotten to know you. Can you tell me what you want to talk about today?”

Verna stared at the carpet, overwhelmed by the warmth in his voice and feeling the inadequacy of her plan.
After a long pause and several false starts, she said, “I am very wicked, and I do not know how to become good, because everyone who is good is good in their own way, and I don’t know what my way is.” The words, though they came almost straight out of her notebook, sounded completely useless: naive, euphemistic.

He looked at her seriously and kindly for a few moments and said, “The easy answer, which is not at all easy, really, of course, is love. But the first part troubles me more. I do not know what a girl of your age could have done that she could call herself wicked.”
“I have, though,” Verna said. “I don’t want to talk about that part. But — I’m sorry — I don’t know what love is. That is what I mean, some of what I mean, that I am wicked.” She felt sick, suddenly, that she was not confessing to murder, a sickness that had slept for years. But she couldn’t help Madeline from prison.

“No one knows what love is,” Pastor Jacobs answered her.
“Maybe? I — I don’t think I can do it, either, though.”
“Everything alive loves — that is what to live, to act, to perceive, is. I know — forgive me — you read a great deal. You should read theology. It is a matter of how, and what, and how much. Nothing deserves all our love but God, who is all, and more.”

“I don’t love, or barely love, people. I try, and I can tell you ways that some are beautiful or kind or brave or good. I can tell you a lot of that. But I do not think I have the courage — to do anything for anyone, almost. I always fail people. My parents I have failed in hundreds and hundreds of ways. Even the best things I try to do for people turn out to be — so horrible I can hardly bear to think of them. If I actually do have the courage to do it, it turns out to have been wrong all along.”

She went on in this vein for some time. At last she began to cry. It felt like the room was shaking.
When at last she began to calm, he said, “What has someone done, to make a girl so young feel so terrible?”

“What have I done, you mean,” Verna said. “It is not my parents’ fault. They are as good to me as they can be. For starters,” she began, unwillingly, wishing it could make up for hiding the truth about Katherine, “I don’t believe in God. Or, at least, sometimes I do and sometimes I don’t, but I don’t understand how anyone could know.”

“My dear, as you surely must know, in this fallen time that does not mean that you are wicked. It means that you think deeply. Those with the deepest faith often question the most. I, for one, do not believe that God hates anyone who is honestly mistaken about Him.”
“Really?” said Verna. Then, feeling she must continue, she went on (though it was treading perilously close to Katherine), “Sometimes — often, even — I think that God is — is —”
“Is what, child?”

“Out there,” Verna whispered. “Outside. You know. Evernost.”
“That,” he said, “is indeed a heresy. But as I said, it is possible to imagine many things earnestly, and I do not think in the end we will be faulted for our best thinking — how did you come to such an idea?”
“I just felt it,” Verna said miserably. “Like —” Here she dropped her voice to the lowest whisper yet. “Like something mighty and golden that would — that could — heal everything, if we’d just let it in.”

“Ah,” said Pastor Jacobs. “And you do not believe this now?”
“No,” said Verna vehemently. “I have seen that it is demons, really.”
“It — please, do not be frightened; I think no one truly faithful has grounds for fear, ever, truly — it seems to me most likely that you are correct,” he said, at last. “Most do not believe in demons, but I do.”
“I do magic,” Verna added. “Perhaps — perhaps I need to stop? I mean, now I say it, I feel that of course I need to stop.”

Pastor Jacobs said, “Can you tell me what you mean? Magic is a crime, but there are many people who do it in small ways, and it is no more unforgivable than any other offense we may commit. And I do not believe it, ah, works.”

Verna began to explain that she could do all sorts of things. She listed a few of the things she had accomplished. She mentioned that she could eavesdrop, and that she knew her mother had told him things she was telling him and also other things, about her housework and her marriageability. She explained to him how it worked, how she could see the connections between things, and how she could use those connections and break them at the right moments to make things change.

“I am not sure I understand everything you’re saying,” Pastor Jacobs said, “and I would be almost certain, in your place, that the magic does not work as well as you think, but I feel fairly safe saying you should remove this from your life. But I feel almost bad saying so, because it seems you’ve put — an enormous amount of thought into it.”
“Oh, I have,” Verna said. “It — it is the main thing I think about. If I didn’t, I don’t know what I’d do.” And, to her alarm, Verna found herself crying again. “Do you think that is what is wrong with me?” she asked.

“I think the first step is to stop this, but you must find things to study in its place. You are clearly, clearly,” he said warmly, “a most intelligent young woman. I have no doubt you could succeed in almost anything you set your hand to.” He held up a hand as she was about to protest. “I say this not only because you are articulate, and can make me half-follow along with what I am — forgive me — almost certain is an imaginary system of learning. I say this because your teachers, and your parents, have both spoken of your talent.”

“I fail at everything,” Verna said simply. “At everything that matters, at least.”
“Do you think the mind worth nothing?”
“Next to the heart,” Verna said, but there was again a scent of hope in the air.

“Your desire to love is much more like love than, say, the simple feelings a baby has toward its mother. But think. We are not the only ones who can be loved. You can love nature. You can love the structures of thought. Those things are of a great of worth — perhaps even of greater worth — than the love of mankind, for that is love of the mind of God Himself. I think you should go to the university.”

“Oh!” said Verna. She thought this over. It had never occurred to her, and she didn’t know why. She could study physics, or mathematics. Those were her strongest areas in school, though she was good at everything when she tried. A happy life, a life of hard, right work — studying the universe as it should be and not as it was, something whispered to her. Because she knew that magic was all too real, and physics and mathematics were, at best, approximate in their relation to the world.

But what of Madeline? Could she care for a child and study at once? Would anyone believe that she could, whether she could or not? She could not afford daycare, and she would not trust Madeline with the other children anyway. Her heart sank.
And something leaped up in her, rich and spontaneous — some approximation, she thought, of the warmth she tried to feel toward other people. She said, “There’s one problem. I have a child.”

“I don’t understand you again. Forgive me, but I have seen you. You are a tiny creature, and you have been at church every Sunday.”
Verna began to explain about the sun, but dread struck her again. The devil in her! It was in her moments of hope that the cruel thing did the most harm. Because the more she explained, despite her hope, the clearer it became that Pastor Jacobs thought she was mad. He looked on her kindly and sadly as she spoke. He listened to everything she said, and she told the whole story, down to the demon’s ripping apart the bird.

“My obligation,” she finished, despairing, “is not to myself. It is to Madeline, and to those she endangers. I have found ways to keep her mind away from her past — with magic. I came to you because I want to learn to shape myself into a mother — and her into a human woman. I would have to use magic to do this,” she added apologetically. “Otherwise I don’t think it is within my power at all.”

Pastor Jacobs sat deep in thought for several minutes. Then he said, “I see several possibilities for what has happened. I find it most likely that you are mistaken. I have seen Madeline with the other orphans, and she is a bright, lovely child, and I have seen also that she has cruelty in her. I commend your powers of observation; many do not see past the bright facade. I remember the day you found her, too. But then you said nothing of demons—”
“Because no one would believe me,” Verna said quietly. “I did not find her. I gave her a body. She tried to kill me, and I tried to catch her.”

He nodded. “Still, the child bears no resemblance to you in either disposition or in appearance— ”
“I gave her a form! With magic!” Verna burst out.
“It is possible. I do not think it likely, but I think it possible. Will that suffice, for now?”

“I don’t know,” Verna said. “What are you going to do?”
“I’m afraid for you,” Pastor Jacobs said. “I’m going to pray for you. I’m going to speak to your parents — forgive me, but I must. You are in danger, whether in danger of madness, or in danger of damnation, or both, is no matter. And I will ask you to come again at this time tomorrow.”

“I can tell you what you’re thinking now,” Verna cried. “You almost believe me, even though you know that no one else will, because you believe in the supernatural. You think the devil and his demons are real, and they are outside the Wall. You've felt demons at the edge of your mind. You’ve seen the shadow of a demon yourself, waking out of a dream, fifteen years ago, a shape of black ink like Madeline. You think there is a demon in the room with us whispering to me. You are about to try an exorcism. I did not know anyone could be so kind to someone like me. You — you —”

“Please, child, stop! I believe more in the demons now and less in the madness, if that helps,” he added bitterly, “but I am not at all sure that it does. Demons or no demons, I think there is madness in you, though, and that the magic worsens it. Obviously you should have refused the being that called itself the sun, but I understand the trickery it employed, and why you would be vulnerable to it, and I do not know what it impregnated you with, but I do not think magic will help you or Madeline, whether she is of your flesh or not. Nor is it right you should bear this burden alone. But you must repudiate witchcraft. That is what I ask of you first.”

He was speaking more loudly now.
Verna said, “I am afraid of what you might do with me. You thought of witch burnings. But I just stopped reading your mind, so you don’t need to be afraid of that anymore.”
“Cruelty was a sin of those times past,” he said calmly, “but I would be lying if I said you do not you frighten me. Why did you choose magic?”

“I don’t know. It — it was a bad habit. I always wanted to wish things real, and I couldn’t, but I kept trying and trying because — because you read about how people can, sometimes, and it felt like it should be possible, like it was always just around the corner and I was too stupid, and then I figured it out. I can do almost anything,” she offered humbly. “It’s just that it all turns out wrong, because I’m wicked. How can we help Madeline, if not by magic?”
“Prayer,” said Pastor Jacobs, firmly.

“But it doesn’t work!” Verna said. “As a child I prayed every night to be good, and it never happened. Changing my thoughts gives me hope. I am happier and I do fewer things I regret. And I can see the magic itself better, the madness of it, and how that madness can be controlled and channeled.”
“You have heard it before, I am sure: God always answers prayers, but sometimes the answer is no. Give up magic, and I promise many things will be better. As for Madeline’s soul — I do not believe for one second that that is something we can do more than influence. True goodness is chosen, not forced. She must make that choice.”

“But what’s to become of her, then? She can torture and kill. She is angry — with all of us, but with me in particular. She is welcome to me. I am the reason all of this happened. But she would not stop there!”
“It remains to be seen,” said Pastor Jacobs. “Whatever she may have been, she is more a child than you, now. Do you think —”
“I am almost certain she has great powers, even like this. Everyone likes her and believes her no matter what she does.”

“Why should it be supernatural? Here. Let us go together to see the child.”
Verna stood, put on her coat, and followed. He commented on the beauty of the frosty, foggy, sunlit day. “Is it beautiful to you?” he asked, clearly genuinely curious.
“I don’t know,” Verna said. “Sometimes it is. The sun —” And she lapsed into silence. She almost cried for a third time, even though she cried so rarely.

“Ah,” he said.
They arrived at the building made of industrial brick and checked in.
“Yes, Verna,” the woman said neutrally. “This is certainly a time Madeline can see you. It is good of you to be so attentive. Pastor Jacobs! How can we help you?” Her tone grew warm and effusive. “We are so grateful for everything you’ve done —”
“And I for all you do,” he said, but with a touch of severity. “Could I see Miss Madeline too?”

“Yes, of course! Why don’t you both come to the parlor, and we’ll bring her there. Madeline is a dear. We all love her so much. We’re sure she’ll find a lovely family.”
Soon a rosy-cheeked, black-haired, smiling, lively child entered the room. “Auntie Verna!” she said, but she sat opposite the two.
“Hello,” Verna said gravely. “This is Pastor Jacobs. He wanted to meet you.”
“Oh!” Madeline said, turning a huge smile on him, then, feigning shyness, hid behind the mistress.

“Good morning, my dear,” said Pastor Jacobs. “I have a gift for you.” He handed her a Holy Eye, which she took happily and looked at with eager curiosity. He met Verna’s eyes.
Clearly he thought this a powerful test of demonic nature. Verna did not. A symbol so simple would only have an effect, even on a demon, under very special circumstances, magically speaking. She pulled her own Holy Eye out of her shirt and raised her eyebrows. He took in necklace and message, nodded.

Pastor Jacobs asked grave questions of Madeline, about God and the church, and about the other girls, and about the mistresses.
“Everyone likes me except Verna,” Madeline said sadly in answer to one of these questions. Verna had never heard her be so frank, not with anyone, and certainly not in her own presence. Pastor Jacobs somehow brought out truth in people. “I don’t know why she doesn’t like me.”

Pastor Jacobs looked at Verna. Verna thought a moment and said, though she did not know if it was a right thing to say, “There are two reasons. The first, the worst, is that I am not good at liking people. I do not like very many people at all, however I try. If you can avoid holding that against me, I will be grateful, but if not I wholly understand. The second is that I have seen you do things that you should not do. I sometimes see you hurt the other children and frighten them. That is wicked. That does not mean you cannot be good, and I want to help you be good.”

“But—” Madeline said, clearly nearing tears (false tears, Verna was almost sure). “She scares me,” she whispered. “She hurts me, when no one else can see.”
“What does she do?” Pastor Jacobs asked.
Madeline looked confused for an instant, then said, “She pinches me.”
“People watch us all the time because they are afraid of that,” Verna said. “I try to help you, and I don’t want it to hurt, and I am sorry if it does sometimes, but I have never pinched you. Not even once. That is a lie.”

“She says things,” Madeline said, in an even lower voice.
“I do,” Verna said.
“They make me go all strange.”
“I know,” Verna said. “If you did not pinch the other children, and hurt them, and take their things, I would not do that. I am not sure it is your fault you do those things, but it is harmful.”

Madeline nodded, then glanced furtively at Pastor Jacobs. Verna, from the long habit of watching her mind, knew she was merely angry and confused and a bit afraid of what Verna would do. But as he drew breath to speak, Madeline threw herself on him, sobbing large, false sobs. Verna could not tell whether he could tell they were false. He patted Madeline uncomfortably and then set her beside him on the couch. “It’s okay,” he said. “It’s okay.” Standing, he picked up Madeline and carried her out to the entryway. There, he handed her to the nurse. She prattled happily about how nice he was as they left.

He walked Verna back to the church and invited her to sit down again. He said very seriously, “I am going to ask you not to visit the child again. Will you do that for me?”
“Yes,” Verna heard herself lie, something clever in her faster than thought.
Pastor Jacobs said, “I do not believe that the child is safe with you.”
“I do not believe that anyone else is safe with her,” Verna replied.
“Let that be in God’s hands,” he said. “Do not hurt the child more.”

“It doesn’t,” Verna said. “She doesn’t like it, but it makes things better.”
“She’s just a child, and very young at that. What you have said she did — it is not good, but it is nothing to dread and hate. Many children are like that. There are things no good can come of, and magic and Outland are two of them. Go to university. Study the universe. Love how it is, not how you can twist it. Forget magic. Forget Madeline.”

“I would so love to do that. It would be —”
He smiled. “There is absolutely no reason you should not.

“Where would I get the money?”,
“There are scholarships. And you have been saving. Keep saving. Start applying. It will be good, and, I think, delightful for you to turn your mind to healthier things. Now. I have an engagement in the next ten minutes, but I want to stress that I’m there for you. Come again. And please — tell me about mathematics and physics, and learn more. When you would be worrying about the child or about your heart — please think of these things instead, and pray.”
“Thank you,” Verna said weakly.

As she left, she was sure she couldn’t follow his advice. But as she walked home — the landscape had something to do with it, and the light of the sun above her (though that disquieted her too, of course) — what he suggested began to seem possible.

When she would have visited Madeline and gone to the woods, she went to the library. When she would usually have asked to see obscure texts, she instead looked for textbooks. She reminded herself of things she had studied over the years. This was not so hard as magic, and the kind of effort it required was different. She remembered a fair bit of calculus, but there was so much she didn’t know.
She found a biography of the mathematician Xavier Aren. She read about his childhood, and wondered what she would have discovered by now if she had devoted to mathematics the time she had spent on magic.

The more she thought of Pastor Jacobs’ horror, the more she felt her treatment of Madeline was horrible. Yet again she had found herself, through her best intentions, doing the worst thing she could. And he thought this path offered freedom from that.

Mother said at dinner that she seemed unusually cheerful. Fearfully, she explained that she had talked to Pastor Jacobs about wickedness and magic (her parents knew she still did it and did their best to ignore it, as they had never succeeded in stopping it) and math.

Both were immediately skeptical.
Verna? Study math?
“You could hardly read as a child,” Mother said decisively.
“I learned,” Verna said.
“But think how long it took! How much effort! I’ve never seen you put that much effort into anything except your make-believe.”

“But I can — I can feel that I can,” Verna said weakly. Then she added, “And I’m going to try.”
And so, against her parents' doubt, for the next few months she did. She learned and learned, she did proof after proof. She wrote (Pastor Jacobs, who continued to see her when she wanted to, suggested it) to a professor at Prima University, asking what she should study and talking about what she knew.
He wrote back, lively with admiration for her work and her evident passion for the subject. He wrote, “I can hardly believe you are as new to this as you say you are.”

Verna did not, of course, explain the similarities of magic to math or the uses of the latter for the former, but she followed his suggestions and began scouting about for research topics.

Not Necessarily

One morning, Verna picked up the newspaper and read that a girl had been found dead at the orphanage. Another child had come to the matrons crying and terrified, and they had called an ambulance.
There was no indication of how the first child had died. Heart attack, the coroner speculated.
Verna wanted to do magic to learn the truth, but when she went to try, she found that kind of thought elusive and almost painful, because, for the first time since she had learned how, Verna had spent a year without magic.

Pastor Jacobs had been brilliant to suggest substituting another passion rather than scolding or frightening her. Her next thought was to go straight to him. But he would say not to worry about it or even investigate.
As a compromise, Verna called the orphanage. She straight-up asked whether Madeline was the child who had found Donna, and said something muddled and, she was sure, insufficient about how she hoped Madeline was all right.

“Well, yes, actually she was,” Sarah, the matron, then began trying to backtrack. Verna thanked her and bid her goodbye before she could say more.
The next day after work, Verna went to the shack instead of the library. Because of her magic, it was still in good condition — the rain had not fallen on the floorboards for all the leaks in the roof, nothing had rotted, dust had not even collected. She took her textbooks out and set a fire and worked on math, as she felt her future crumble around her.

She pictured living there with a child. She looked at the magical blanket of everything she hoped was love that she had crocheted for Madeline.
Her mind again pointed out that Madeline could, quite simply, be killed.
That was unconscionable.
What, her mind, though, kept saying, if she were to kill only as she had killed Katherine? Then she could kill and push, and it would send the child — the demon — back to Outland where it came from.

This thought consumed her for weeks. The only thing that kept her from it was guilt over Katherine. She knew she’d regret it. Perhaps the very act of murder was something dreadful, whether the soul died or not. That was something others seemed to think, though she could not understand it.
