Was that one of the things that made her wicked — her failure to grasp the value of the body?
But, then, perhaps being born in the body of a human was a chance at salvation for a demon. Perhaps it was for Madeline’s own sake that she must not be sent back to Outland.

If you cannot be good, than good must come from you.

Those words had not haunted her dreams for a full few months, and Pastor Jacobs thought them wrong-headed. Yet they came back now and spoke themselves over and over as she stoked the fire, watched the snow falling outside, poked at her latest set of proofs, and read more of the textbook she’d checked out. The idea of sacrifice had not meant much to her before she’d begun to think of a future. Now, though, she hated the idea that she must give that up to live in the woods with this strange child. Even the thought that Madeline might kill her, which once had brought a sense of relief, now brought her sharp fear.

And the idea that she not only could but must care for a child whom no one thought was hers felt absurd, itself like a child’s fear and a child’s idea. Could she have imagined everything that happened in the forest. That she had not woven a body for Madeline, or seen the older Madeline learn from her, or any of it. That all of that had been hallucination, or was a false memory. She wanted to believe she’d imagined it.
She ached to talk to Pastor Jacobs, but she knew well enough what he’d say, and knew also that she would believe him if he actually said it, when she knew with dreary she could and must stop Madeline from killing more.

Instead of going to the pastor, she wrote letters canceling her applications and put together a spell for inconspicuousness — like but not like the one she had used to see Madeline before. Doing so actually hurt her. She felt dizzy and ill the rest of the evening. She also readied the spell for reading minds, and the spells for altering thoughts, and a spell for casting sleep.
She withdrew all her money from the bank account. She watched as the disturbed teller counted out hundreds after hundreds. She put the money in her backpack.

She left a note with her parents: “I am well, but I am leaving. Please do not try to find me. I will be fine.” She did not know if they would obey, but she ought to at least give them the chance to. She knew that a year ago, she would have thought it right and proper that they not search for her. Now she was not sure she agreed, but she felt that everything would be easier, both for her and for them, if they did not.
She considered sending Pastor Jacobs a letter too but did not. He would know what she had done and why, and would try to stop her, but she did not want it to happen any sooner than it had to.

She crept into the orphanage, disguised by the spell. She approached Madeline’s crib, which the girl had almost outgrown. She made Madeline sleep — the sick magic-feeling increased sharply as it took effect, alongside the haze in her mind. She picked the child up, careful to leave no fingerprints on the crib (though she did not intend to be found, so she supposed it didn’t matter if they realized she was the guilty one) and carried Madeline out of the orphanage, out of town, into the woods, and to the shack.

She settled sleeping Madeline into the crib, pulled pajamas out of her own backpack, checked the house and did a quick spell to check the spells (it was not just dizziness and nausea they caused — they also made things seem unreal, hazy, connected in ways other than they should be or seemed to be. Which, she supposed, they were.) She locked Madeline in, set up a spell to make sure that if Madeline grew shocked or frightened Verna would know, and climbed into her own bed. Now that she was actually carrying out her plan, she felt very little about it, only calm determination and the creep of magic-madness. There would be time enough to grieve for her studies.

She awoke at five. She looked into Madeline’s room — the spell, designed to last three hours, had worn off, but the child was still asleep. Verna dreaded her awakening. But she boiled water, collected some roots and leaves that she knew were wholesome, and began to boil them into a stew.
The sun had just risen when Madeline awoke. She began calling at once for help, so Verna came to the door. “Good morning, Madeline,” she said quietly.

“Verna,” Madeline said angrily.
“Mother,” Verna corrected. “Now that you’re here, you should know that that’s who I am, and that’s what I want you to call me, because it is a term of love, and love is something both of us need to learn.”
“No!” Madeline said. “No! Take me back!”
“You killed another girl,” Verna said. “I can’t. I don’t know what else you’d do. I am not as good as you deserve, because I know it is not your fault, but I am all there is right now. I am better able than anyone else to handle you.”

“Did not,” Madeline said, beginning to cry, and Verna began the mind-reading spell. It confirmed her worst suspicions. Madeline was lying, and knew she was lying, and did not care, and the tears were mostly of rage.
“Yes,” said Verna, “you did. I know what is in your mind, and I do not want you to lie to me. It is bad to lie.”
“I didn’t,” Madeline protested.
“But you did,” Verna said patiently. “And if you tell that lie again, I will make you forget.”

“Of course I didn’t,” Madeline said. “How could I? Nothing was wrong with her. It was just — it was just — ”
“You did,” Verna said, and then she murmured the erasing spell.
Madeline grew distant and said, “Stop it! That’s what you used to do to me, and that’s how come I got you kicked out.”
“It is better than that you should kill,” Verna said. She saw that she was being a hypocrite. She had killed, or worse, and not confessed. But all of that was for reasons. The distinction was hazier than Verna wanted it to be. Perhaps what Madeline had done had reasons too? “Why did you kill her?” Verna asked, listening to Madeline’s mind as well as her words.

“She kicked me.”
“You mean,” said Verna, knowing what she said was true, “that she told on you, and you kicked her and told her not to do it again, and she said no, and so you killed her.”
Madeline turned white. “How come you know that?”
“I see into your mind.”

“No such thing, they said so,” Madeline said. And this she really meant. Madeline hoped that the teachers were right.(Clearly she had been telling stories about Verna. Well, let her. That was fair.)
Verna did not say that Madeline could herself do such things with an ease that Verna had never known: let Madeline find that out for herself, or be told when there was some chance she would use her gifts wisely and well. That time had not yet come. Instead she said, “We may experiment, if you’d like. Think of a number.”

But Madeline refused, and Verna did not press the issue. Nor did Verna press the issue of Madeline’s refusal to touch the soup she served for breakfast. There would be treats, but they would be rare and for good reason. If ever she could afford more, she would give more. By dinner, Madeline had grown hungry enough to eat, and so she did, though she made faces and yelled at Verna and talked about running away or finding the police. She did so with an air of nervous defiance. Verna did not use magic, except to monitor her mind, though. Verna wanted to see what she was dealing with before she began, and she dreaded what the constant immersion in magic would do to both of them.

Verna’s resolve not to use magic other than mind-reading was tested over the following week. Madeline cried often, which was mainly a problem because Verna did not know how to calm a child, though she murmured some senseless reassurances and also tried to reason Madeline out of her distress, which increasingly was real. She could not provide real comfort, though she tried. Madeline, probably understandably for a child of not quite five, did not see either the danger she posed to other people or the inherent goodness of goodness, the main reasons Verna had to offer for taking her away.

Harder than that, though, were Madeline’s many tantrums over those first few days. Madeline destroyed a chair, for which Verna calmly scolded her. This only made her angrier, of course, so she bashed out the cabin’s one surviving window (Verna had boarded up the others, whose glass was long since gone). Verna scolded her again, locked her in her bedroom, and spent the rest of the afternoon cleaning up shards of broken glass.
She kicked, scratched, and bit often. Verna bore this calmly enough, though she had to use magic on herself to make sure the wounds didn’t fester.

It was the next day that Madeline all but set the house on fire because Verna left her unattended for five minutes, opening the stove and thrusting in a stick she’d found. Verna cried out, the flaming stick fell to the ground, and it was only with a pan of water multiplied through quick, impromptu spellwork that no damage was done beyond a black scar on the wood floor.

Looking inside Madeline’s mind, she found, to her dismay, that Madeline had known perfectly well she could destroy the house (they’d told the children at the orphanage a story about a bad little girl who played with matches and died when the house burned down) and had hoped to. Verna knew she must not put everything down to Madeline’s ill nature — Pastor Jacobs had taught her that, and that she must not want from Madeline things that no child could do. She supposed any small girl could, desperate, think of burning down the house where she was held prisoner, though running away seemed to Verna like a likelier, more practical, and less destructive way out.

But, with further probing, Verna saw that Madeline knew she could not die and that Verna could. Madeline had consciously used this knowledge in her efforts. Verna did not want Madeline thinking herself invulnerable. That seemed like a sure path to destruction. She resolved to make erasing those memories a major goal. Immortality, like powerful magic, was something Madeline could only be allowed to know when she could bear it well and not before.

The whole project looked impossible. Verna did not know how ordinary parents went about shaping their children’s character — at least, she knew that her parents’ efforts had come to nothing — and she realized that she would have to reinvent the wheel to do even the most ordinary part of her job well.

First, she took a trip into town, disguising herself with magic, to find books: she picked out the books that Pastor Jacobs had recommended to her as expressing something of the goodness of goodness and the nature of love. These were theological treatises too dense for an ordinary child combined with a few parables that he said were quite beautiful, but she knew that Madeline was far from an ordinary child. She also cast about for books she could remember reading that were harmless — books for learning to read or learning about the world that would not allow Madeline to dwell on darkness or destruction but that would educate her. Recalling that Pastor Jacobs had truly believed that the love of knowledge was a worthy love deserving of the name, she found textbooks on all subjects, for a variety of ages.

Loaded down, she returned to Madeline (whom she had left locked in her room; Verna thought that if she ever grew to trust Madeline, gained Madeline’s own willing assent to her efforts that Madeline be good, she might allow the girl excursions into town with her.)
Madeline had, when she returned, torn her room to bits. Verna merely put it in order as Madeline watched, saying, “I will teach you to do this soon.”
Madeline made a fart sound. “It’s more fun than I thought,” Madeline said. “I thought you’d always do things to me.”

“I will,” said Verna, “but not yet. And only if you’re bad.”
At night, once Madeline was asleep, Verna read the books she’d bought. She eliminated about half the children’s books she had remembered as benign because they portrayed cruelty, petty or great, that she did not want Madeline even to think about, at least, not before Verna created a conscience in her.

Verna blacked out the parts in the textbooks about the uncomfortable facts of nature, predation and parasitism, but left the anatomical drawings—uneasily, as she knew Madeline could all too easily ask how they had come about and then fall to pulling apart animals herself again. The theology Verna loved. Sometimes it moved her to tears. She did not believe it, though, and she suspected the bits she loved the most had a taint of Evernost about them.

At least, they felt like the Golden Thing. As for the parables, one of them was an Evernost story, only not under that name, and with a few words changed. That Pastor Jacobs didn’t realize this was startling to her, but perhaps he hadn’t read the Evernost stories, or read as many of them as she had. Perhaps, something in her, something like hope, whispered, that is because there is a grain of truth in the Evernost stories—even just a grain. But she was not willing to risk her daughter for such a slim promise as that.

Reading grounded Verna. She was shocked at the fact that almost nothing she’d read voluntarily as a child was something she would allow Madeline to read. This gave her a sense of grave misgiving, both about herself and about her plans, but she supposed that perhaps if her parents had forced her to read what she would have Madeline read, she might have been a different and better person.

After she bought the books, she began, regretfully, trying to shape Madeline’s mind. She wanted Madeline to retain cheer, energy, intellect, if possible. Those were the closest things she could find to virtue in her daughter. She struggled, as she had from the start, with defining the goodness she wanted the child to display. She wanted no cruelty, no dishonesty (or, at least, she thought, thinking of her own dishonesty, no dishonesty for no reason). Of course, she wanted kindness, love — joy in others’ joy, sorrow in their sorrow. She wanted Madeline to help others, spontaneously — and effectively, as Verna had not been able to do yet. The rest of the wickedness — she supposed she’d know it when she saw it.

Verna’s first practical struggle, before all the rest, was protecting herself. Madeline, she saw, the deeper she delved into the girl’s mind, hated her. The hatred had been passive and quiet when Verna worked on her mind, but now that she had stopped for months without restarting, it was violent and active. Madeline thought alarmingly often about how to kill Verna. She wanted very much to kill her the way she had the other little girl. Luckily, though, Madeline retained the dimmest ghost of a memory of her first attempt to kill Verna, when Verna had trapped her in a human body. It made her fear to try again — though Verna could see that if her mind were left untouched, she would do it soon.

Sometimes Madeline wanted to do it more painfully and viscerally. She wanted to carve Verna up with a knife the way Verna carved and chopped potatoes. She wanted to set Verna on fire. These were all things she’d thought about others, too, but only idly. With Verna it was serious and vicious, because of what she sensed Verna had done to her already and was going to do to her — and also because Verna responded disappointingly little to her less violent naughtiness. She wanted Verna to scream, to rage, to faint in terror, and Verna had yet to do any of those things. Knowing this made Verna feel wan pride and also a certain relief that she had, perhaps, made the right decision.

Verna first tried to erase Madeline’s memories of killing Donna. She watched the girl’s mind constantly and murmured her spell of forgetting whenever Madeline thought of it.
“You’re doing it,” Madeline said. “I didn’t do anything wrong.”
“Wicked thoughts are like wicked deeds,” Verna said.
“But I wasn’t thinking anything bad!”
“You were,” Verna said peacefully. “You don’t remember it, so of course you don’t think you were,” said Verna.

“You could say that whenever you wanted to.”
“Yes, but I won’t,” Verna said. “It doesn’t matter if I do, though. Did I hurt you?”
“Yes!” said Madeline, preparing a long, wailing complaint.

Best to cut that short. “Please don’t lie.” Here, Verna tried a new spell, a spell of conscience. She had, to ready it, imagined every time she herself had lied and the discomfort she’d felt about it, and the importance of the connection between words and reality. She’d tried to embody that feeling in the ash of a burned piece of paper on which she had written I must not tell lies, something a teacher had made her write over and over when another student had (falsely) accused her of stealing and she’d denied it.

She had resented it at first, before she realized that it could just as easily apply to any of the lies she had told her parents about what she read at the library or where she went after school. Then she had written it as a penance, though she found it did not stop her from doing any of the things she was not supposed to do (she did, though, develop a habit of lying by omission rather than commission.)

So Verna reached into her pocket, crumbled a bit of the ash of this paper, and whispered some words over Madeline, and Madeline flinched a bit, her face going momentarily tight with revulsion.
“Sorry,” said Madeline in a mumble.
“It’s okay. Just do that less often. Now we should turn to lessons. I know you are bright, and I want to help you learn as much as you can.”

“Boring!” said Madeline, and she was mentally preparing to throw a fit. Verna found herself in a dilemma. To use magic would be to suppress something Verna was fairly sure was ordinary through extraordinary means, and to deny the girl (Verna had come to use this phrase in her mind a great deal, though she did not yet know what she thought about it) room to be human. Should Madeline have such room? Should anyone? Verna had also read, in the period leading up to the abduction, books on child development. She remembered throwing no tantrums in her own girlhood — her wickedness was of a more cold-blooded variety.

Perhaps the warm kind enabled one to avoid the cold kind? In any event, Verna merely insisted on Madeline’s obedience verbally and did not use magic until Madeline picked up a chair and swung it at her (she did not hit the child; her parents had on occasion hit her and somewhat more often her brother, but it was always tempered with warmth, and limited by it; Verna did not trust herself to limit herself in such punishment well or wisely, and so she abjured it altogether.) The girl fell back, hit with both forgetting and conscience, and (Verna knew with guilt) a shadow of the sick, dull feeling the magic gave Verna herself.

So Verna quizzed her and learned easily enough after magically again forbidding the child to lie (Madeline had been planning to pretend to know nothing and be unable to learn; Verna told her that learning was one of the best things either of the two of them could do with themselves, thinking of Pastor Jacobs) that Madeline knew her letters and could write her name and a few words.

Verna began to reteach Madeline what she had taught her before she had been forced into this body, and Madeline learned — not quite as quickly, but still more quickly than the child development books had led Verna to expect. Within a week, Madeline was reading the benign readers Verna had found. When Madeline completed a task well or showed what Verna judged to be a moment of kindly joy rather than angry resistance, Verna gave her an apple. Madeline loved the apples. They were the sweetest thing near the shack.

She tried to run away twice, and Verna caught her and brought her back. She did not put a spell on Madeline for doing this — she was all too aware that the girl was a prisoner. She tried very carefully to use the spells only when she saw the child doing something actually wicked, and actually wicked beyond the ordinary wickedness of small children, for which Verna would merely send her to her room, or rebuke her. But the line was harder to draw than Verna had expected it to be, and as the months passed, Verna decided that Madeline must not be angry at all, because anger led at once both to her violence and cruelty and to her magic.

Things happened when Madeline was angry. Verna, leaving her “room to be human,” once found that she had killed a bird with a glance. And so Verna began taking her anger away whenever it showed its head.
The third time Madeline ran away, Verna let her, though she filled a bowl with water and watched her in the bowl. She saw Madeline find a bear, and just as she was about to rush to the rescue, she saw that Madeline had tamed the bear.

She could not see into Madeline’s mind from that distance at that moment, but she knew that no bear would let a child ride on it, stroke it, and pinch and tease it, without magic.
When Verna tried to erase all memories of what Madeline had done to the bear, Madeline was truly sick for days afterward. Eventually, through divination, she realized that the child had eaten something poisonous too — perhaps some mushrooms. She suspected that weakening Madeline’s own magic had made her susceptible to the poison in a way she would not ordinarily have been. So Verna did a spell for perfect health that took on Madeline at once — it made her outwardly well and lively.

After a year, Madeline grew taller and realized she could pick apples herself. Verna saw this, felt a moment’s irritation that almost, to her shame, led her to murmur a spell, and then she realized that the child’s discovery (naive she had been not to realize it all along, too!) was perhaps for the best.
“You realize,” she said, “now, that I will not reward you. The apples are worthless, and so is reward. It is a crutch. You must be good for the sake of goodness, if you can.”

As Madeline grew, she grew — Verna believed — better. She was quiet and calm — that was, in its way, a loss, but it was better than the destruction she would otherwise wreak. She did the tasks set to her well, though not quite so well as she would have if magic had not interfered with her mind. Verna refined and refined the spells, trying to make them so they would give Madeline the most they could of intellect, but Madeline always, always seemed to misuse any intellect given her.

After a few years, Verna felt comfortable (or perhaps, she feared, it was mere exhaustion? but Verna did not think it would be acceptable for Madeline to have Verna constantly her keeper, though Verna knew she could extend her lifespan to allow her to follow Madeline forever) letting Madeline read in peace, the mind-reading spell not active but only present enough to let Verna know if anything particularly cruel or manipulative or angry entered Madeline.

She brought the Bible into Madeline’s study, and tried to engage herself in study Pastor Jacobs would approve of, too. She read the Bible cover to cover, searching for bits benign enough to share with Madeline. She found much that moved and interested her, and also many things that echoed, almost as strongly as the parable had, Evernost stories or rewrote them. She wondered about the afterlife, especially. Was the Evernost of the stories, perhaps, the counterfeit of what heaven was supposed to be? Those who believed in heaven thought so.

Those who did not (Verna had been raised as one of these) thought that was itself Outlandish deception, and that if there was life after death it was so different as to be meaningless to most people. God would reward us in this life, if He chose, Verna thought was the safest thing to believe, and, when He did not, it was, as with the apples, a sign that goodness was at once the reward and the means to the reward.

Love

“Love others as you love yourself,” she said, not for the first time, when Madeline was twelve, “is the center of all goodness. I am trying to give you something I wish I could have myself, goodness. I know that my motives are not love but duty. You must know that this is not because you and every living person is not worthy of love but because I am incapable of it. I sometimes think it is bad of me to take you from people who could love you as I cannot, but I believe that you could hurt them as you cannot hurt me, and so I will not.”

I don’t love you and I never will: Verna heard the words with perfect clarity in Madeline’s mind, quite without Madeline’s will. The defiance in them Verna considered dangerous, but the sentiment she fully understood. That feeling was entirely and utterly just, though it might lead to unjust and evil things. It was certainly not important that Madeline love Verna — she just had to love someone. Perhaps, she would grow to love Verna if she had the makings of a saint, but otherwise — Verna fully understood. Still, guiltily, she murmured something to drive off the thought, but it was not the most powerful spell she had for such a purpose — it was, rather, the one that interfered least with thought.

Madeline’s own evident fear of the thought, of what Verna would do because of it, made Verna pause still more. Madeline was an exemplary child, by all outward standards, now. She helped around the house. She excelled in her studies (Verna was teaching her mathematics, and she picked it up more readily than Verna had herself; otherwise, she read as perceptively as Verna dared let her). Perhaps the time had come to see if the alterations were lasting or real. Perhaps she needed to give Madeline more freedom. As she cooked potato stew (her garden now grew excellently, and gave them enough food to last through winter), she lifted most of the spells on her daughter, including the one she had just laid on.

Released from some of the spells on her mind, Madeline grew not only sharper and more intelligent — she grew, visibly, happier. Verna knew that her mind was not going where Verna would choose with its freedom — the more Verna tried to appeal to her reason on the topic of love, the more Madeline resisted, for one thing — but perhaps the greater joy about her, the strength and vigor, were themselves more like love than forced obedience could bring her, and perhaps, just perhaps, the danger to the rest of humanity was over.

Then, though, thoughts of murder came to Madeline, again, not quite through Madeline’s own will. Madeline tried to hide it but failed. Madeline wanted to kill Verna. In earlier, more energetic days, Verna would have erased this from her entirely. Now, Verna decided, uneasily, to see what Madeline did with it.

Within the year, Madeline had resolved to do it. Verna wanted to take the desire from her at once, and could have done so easily, but something kept her from doing so. One night, Madeline stole a knife and would not admit to it. Verna, sadly, began to prepare the spells she would use to foil Madeline’s efforts (Madeline still, at least, did not know her own magical powers).

Verna turned a pillow into a double of herself while Madeline studied. Then she tweaked a spell she sometimes used on Madeline so that Madeline would see apparitions of her where she was not. And she made yet another spell that would make her seem to be something monstrous — it came out, she realized, to a tree with arms and legs and a fierce, gnarled face in its bark. She finally wove a net of sleep — sleep that would last as long as Verna herself did, if she chose that it should, and hitched it to fall from the ceiling if she should choose that it do so.

She did not know what she would do with the sleeping Madeline. Much depended on how Madeline reacted in the moment. Would she be reluctant? Would her externally imposed conscience stop her? Would some fear of causing pain (something Verna had struggled and struggled to instill in the girl)?
Verna stood in the darkness of the kitchen, disguised as the tree, watching Madeline’s mind from afar. No compunction moved in it, not the slightest. Madeline felt nothing but relish and freedom, combined with some fear of her magic.

Verna created the first apparition of herself in Madeline’s mind. “I was afraid of this,” the apparition said, “and I am very disappointed to find out that I was right. I had hoped that if I allowed you more freedom you would use it to be good, but you have not. I had hoped I could take you to town, and begin to introduce you to society, but I can see this is not so.” Verna made up her mind — Madeline would have to go back to the way she had been before Verna decided to let her be relatively free. Verna began to utter the first spells of binding, and Madeline found in herself — rage and loathing and bone-deep hatred, not for Verna but for the spells themselves. That shook Verna. There had to be some other way.

Madeline ran at the apparition with the knife, and it, of course, disappeared. Verna surrounded Madeline with images, and as Madeline paused to decide which was real, Verna fell on her in the guise of a tree and disarmed her. Then, pushing her away, Verna sent the net of sleep down on her.

Verna laid the sleeping girl in her bed, went into the living room, and collapsed. The adrenaline in her veins cleared her thought, and she saw that yet again she had made everything worse. If only she had listened to Pastor Jacobs! Of course, the Madeline could have killed in town. But the dread in Madeline, the hatred, at even the thought of Verna’s spells! Wasn’t the cruelty of that almost as bad as Madeline’s killing people — maybe even worse?

Madeline rose in Verna’s imagination. Madeline’s compliance, all false. Madeline’s wit and cleverness, all cruel. Madeline’s brightness and joy, all selfish. She imagined ghosts of Madeline at every age, and felt in herself a wan mockery of something like love — the love she could have felt and never did — and the weak, faint shadows of dull affection she had had. For they had existed. Verna had been afraid that she would want to kill Madeline. She did not. She decidedly did not. She could imagine doing it, she could imagine that it would not cause her great pain or fear, but she wanted not to. This surely indicated something at least a touch right in herself.

As she thought over Madeline, a much stronger pang of — not quite love, but something like it, perhaps Pastor Jacobs would have thought it was love — struck her. She longed, violently, to see Katherine, all red hair and regal bearing. Katherine did not have the intellect of Madeline, but in everything else, she was almost what Madeline should have been. Joy and warmth and sharpness and certainty. Verna longed for Katherine — and she’d ruined that too. She’d sent Katherine to the same heartless place that had brought Madeline into this world.
Perhaps — perhaps it was possible to make up for this in some measure too?

Verna did not sleep or eat for the next three days as she thought about time. Verna did not understand time. She had, playfully, when she had no other project in mind, tried to before. She had come up with two ideas that she thought made a modicum of sense.

If an event could go different ways, perhaps it went all ways — but a person could only experience it in one. In that case, any hope to go back and change things was foolish — the original way was indelible — but perhaps it was possible to add knowledge and create alternate ways that things could go.

Alternatively — suppose that time travel could not change events. It could only make events go the way they already went; sometimes, perhaps, it was necessary in order that they should. If you did not know the way they already went, though, you could make them go in the right way instead of the wrong way. Verna remembered with delight that she had thought, killing Katherine, that she had failed to send Katherine to Evernost.

Now, she took out her bowl of water and murmured her incantations and, after an hour, came up with an answer: both her theories were true, and time travel was not only possible but necessary to the existence in which she currently lived. The more she thought (and, some cynical part of her thought, the less she ate or slept), the more compelled she felt — gloriously, delightfully compelled—to save Katherine with time travel.

There were, perhaps (she hoped not, but there always might be), worlds in which Katherine did go Outland the way Verna meant her to. There were, perhaps, oh glory, worlds in which she had never killed Katherine in the first place. But this world — she felt surer and surer, the more magic and the more calculations she did — was a world in which Katherine was saved from Evernost, and that time travel was the means of her salvation

She set up a spell to alert her if Madeline woke or if anything happened to her.
This would be her masterwork, and one small sliver of redemption, she decided,
She boiled herbs in her soup pan. She scribbled and burned notes. She chanted and chanted aloud. She worked out all the astrological coordinates of the moment when she had killed Katherine.

At last, prepared, she began to work the spells. They lasted two days straight of fasting and dancing and meditating and reciting. Finally, she looked deep into a mirror and saw not her own face but Katherine’s, at the instant of the murder. She felt memory unite with event, with how she had pushed and then felt uncertain about Katherine’s fate.
Between the push and the uncertainty was her opportunity. She seized Katherine’s spirit and brought it into the ramshackle cabin at that very instant, a glowing light. She had meant to let it dissipate, to go to whatever death it must, for death, while dreadful, was not so dreadful as Outland, and perhaps — just perhaps — heaven waited for Katherine after death.

But she found this totally unacceptable. At the last moment (she had not even been aware it was possible), she ranged all across time, all across the Kingdom, looking for a time and place with the right coordinates, the right significances, to hold a soul in some state, neither life nor death, without its dissipating. Just as she would see later what was to be done with Madeline, she would see what was to be done with Katherine. She sent Katherine to the nearest such location and time, and, just as she did so, the spell dissolved and she collapsed.

She slept on the floor for days on end. Because of her spells, no creature came along and ate her, nor did Madeline escape her sleep. When she woke, hunger gnawing at her, she felt herself a weaker, madder, more broken thing. But she knew she had accomplished her aim. She had saved Katherine from a fate worse than death and then, at the last minute, from death as well, though what would become of her next, Verna couldn’t say.

She began to keep house and did so mindlessly for the next few days, as she recovered her strength, eating first a little and then a lot, more than she would ever have dared eat when she was trying to support two people. She looked in on Madeline occasionally, and lovely Madeline looked — like Sleeping Beauty or Snow White from the Evernost tales. When she ceased to be so exhausted she could not think, she considered what should happen next. She thought of going back to town and talking with Pastor Jacobs. But he would fear her, and he would not understand. She knew this, though her heart thought otherwise.

She realized how long it had been since she had been properly alone — and utter solitude, she decided, was not good. She slept odd hours, ate odd hours. She checked often on her beautiful daughter but sometimes wondered if she were not an apparition herself. She studied — mathematics, for the most part, because it seemed a sort of antidote to magic — but she did not succeed in concentrating the way she had been able to when she’d had school and then a job to ground her. She wandered the forest aimlessly, half-hoping that the bear her daughter had tamed would devour her, or at least guiltily imagining it. She talked to herself often.

Household chores she did some days for hours on end, others not at all. She felt strange. Finally, curiosity drove her to Helsom, to find out what had become of the place she had left behind.
So she went, after, with too much labor for such a simple task, putting on a spell of inconspicuousness, into Helsom and began to ask after Verna. They had, unfortunately, connected her disappearance with Madeline’s.

An idea came to her after a time: she could return, claiming to remember nothing of what had happened in her absence. At first this was merely a fantasy. She thought about it as she lay huddled on the ground or puzzled at some mathematical problem. She thought about it as she read the Bible for the hundredth time and gleaned nothing of use. She thought about it as she paced back and forth, an itch in her, and wondered if she would lose her mind. She finally decided to do it.

She strengthened the protections around Madeline and the spell ensuring that she would sleep and went to town. She went first to the police. She asked for the date and did what she thought was a reasonable, understated job of feigning surprise when they told her that fifteen years had passed. She gave them her name (she did not recognize the young police officer she talked to). She asked after her parents, who were well. Her father was nearing retirement from his job at the lumber mill.

The officer called them. When they arrived, they were both delighted to see her. There was, she felt, a certain dread under the delight, but the delight itself seemed real — even when she tried a quick, subtle version of the mind-reading spell she’d used on Madeline to check. But she swore at once to herself that she would stop doing such things and followed them to the house. They set her up in the guest room.

Her mother questioned her intensely, and she pretended to know nothing about what had happened. She invented a bad man to have “a vague sense of,” on the theory that this was almost true, if you combined Madeline and the Sun into one.
She stayed up long into the night, trying to fail to answer questions, feeling guiltier and guiltier, fuller and fuller of regret that she had decided to do this at all — except that the guilt-laden, off-kilter, uncomfortable, embarrassing world she had grown up in held a clarity that life in the woods did not. But, she found, she missed Madeline.

This was strange to her, even stranger to her than that she should want to be back in her hometown. Here she was at people’s mercy, and still at loose ends. There, Madeline had been at hers, and she had had definite tasks she must complete to get through life.

Her parents were not sure she could or should work at all. She was sure that she must. The gas station had been sold and wasn’t hiring. So she approached the library, and found that they had use for someone to shuttle and wrap books. The quiet of the work soothed Verna, and the presence of the books brought back memories — memories she had never realized were positive because of the guilt that laced them (she had, after all, read mostly forbidden books).

The smells of the place were pleasant to her. She could sometimes crack open a book on the job and read it. All of this because the head librarian knew her and, it turned out (Verna had never imagined it) admired her. “I couldn’t care less about it,” she assured Verna when Verna tried to say the most truthful thing she could (painfully lukewarm) about her skills. “You’re an independent thinker, and you study hard,” the woman said. “Not many children would have sought out the Evernost stories. I rather fancy them myself. You may not be the most organized person, but you’ll do well enough.”

In her spare time, she continued her study of mathematics. She had continued it some while working with Madeline, but now she devoted herself to it wholeheartedly in the hours after she finished work. She did occasionally publish in journals. She eventually bought a small house for herself, with her slight savings, and lived comfortably and eccentrically there, to her parents’ relief.

She wanted to give up magic again, though she knew something had to be done about Madeline first. Nor was she able to check on Madeline as frequently as she wanted to — she had to disguise herself magically to leave town, and she found herself more and more reluctant even to do that.
She considered, also, doing one last great magic on herself — to erase her memory of everything involving Madeline and the years she had spent with the girl. But this would, of course, be shirking her duty to Madeline.

In the meantime, she began to pay attention to politics. She was not, clearly, the only one who had had those poisonous intuitions about the goodness of Outland. The demons of the place clearly knew how to reach out and touch many, many minds: an Open-Border movement was sprouting up. The less extreme parts of it thought that Outland should be scientifically investigated and explored, cautiously. The more extreme parts of it though that Outland was precisely what Verna had imagined it to be — paradise, heaven, of God.

Apparently these people had some power in Parliament.
At the encouragement of the head librarian, Verna began to read history again, focusing on the history of attitudes toward Outland. Verna and the librarian both had many issues with how the country was governed. Verna began to journal about how she would rule, if she could. This was absurd, of course. She could not care about the issues that politicians had to deal with on a day-to-day basis. She could not charm people. Some of this could be remedied by magic, but she did not want to use magic. She would not know when to be merciful and when to be strict. Still she had something the government lacked.

She wanted wealth to be distributed fairly, though work should be rewarded. She did not see why one form of work should be favored over another, mental over physical. She did not see a great many things. She wrote little essays about it, and imagined a ruler, a perfect, benevolent ruler, who could make everything the way she felt it should and must be. This ruler was beautiful, a human ideal, as well as thoughtful, just, and merciful. This person was truly good. She was, Verna decided rebelliously, female.
She did not go back to Pastor Jacobs. She doubted she could pretend with him.

The Test

The third year after her return, on Madeline’s birthday, she decided a permanent solution had to be reached. How, she wondered, had Madeline survived so long without nourishment? She had not asked herself this before, to her acute shame, but the girl was not wasting away. Either the spell itself took care of that in ways Verna had not predicted — something that did occasionally happen — or Madeline could not starve. Madeline did, though, look older.

In any event, Verna felt fairly sure she had confirmed that Madeline could not be good. But was Madeline, of necessity, the demon? Could good come, if not from Madeline’s personality or selfhood, which was demonic, from Madeline’s body and cleverness and beauty? Could Madeline’s strange body be made home to a being as beautiful inwardly as Madeline was outwardly? And what really was Madeline — a demon that had inhabited Verna’s body or a body Verna had sculpted with her mind? Was not the latter more like her daughter than the former? This did not seem true, but part of her hoped it might be.

At this thought, fate aligned. She had Katherine. “Had” was, perhaps, an overstatement. But she could find Katherine, find whatever time and place in the Kingdom she had fallen into, and whatever form, and liberate her into Madeline’s body. For Katherine deserved that body as Madeline did not. It would not, she hoped, even be murder; she only had to send Madeline’s “soul,” the demon that animated her, back to Evernost. She would then implant Katherine into Madeline's body. The selfhood of Madeline would become a demon far away in Evernost, and immortal — and the selfhood of the body (if it had any) would be redeemed by Katherine’s heart.

All of these thoughts spinning through her head, Verna took Madeline, in the middle of one night, feeling mad and strange, from the shack into the guest bedroom of her home. She locked it and made the door inconspicuous and impenetrable to any but she.
If Verna had been speaking to Pastor Jacobs, or, really, to anyone, this would not have looked plausible, let alone acceptable. But the act seemed an increasingly perfect solution to her problems. She would have to kill Madeline in a way that left her body intact — that is, with magic — and she would have to find Katherine in time and space. That was all.

First, Katherine.Verna wrote out everything she remembered of the magic she had used to deflect Katherine into a waiting moment. She looked in a bowl of water first for Katherine (no success) and then for signs. At last, an equation popped into her mind, and she recognized it as one of the keys to the question. She studied the equation, and adjusted it according to what she remembered while keeping its central insight, and applied it to the astrological situations at the time she had killed Katherine and then again at the time she had deflected Katherine’s self from Evernost.

The date she found was December 14, 797, some twenty-three years in the future. The place she found was in the mountains too, forty miles or so away from Helsom. So. The soul could be accessed then, and implanted in Madeline’s body shortly thereafter. Unfortunately, unless she dared to do a great deal more time travel with a skill she was not sure she had, she would have to wait. But perhaps the unfortunate was actually fortunate. Perhaps (this was to be her next great magical work) she would give Madeline a time of life before her death and rebirth and exile to Outland. Madeline was too dangerous to be free, but perhaps imprisoned —

She cut off Madeline’s room from the house and from space itself. She got it (she would only ever be able to explain it thus, though the math and magic involved in so doing was hideously complex) sideways. She rendered the walls and the door impermeable with every spell she had for the purpose and some she only invented then. Finally, Verna walled off the “sideways” place from the rest of reality with walls of ice and fire.

The ice and the fire were the two things that had kept her, Verna, from Madeline’s forms of wickedness, and they were majestic. The fire was hatred for, to be a bit grandiose, sin. It was the fire of Verna’s own self-examination. The ice was the torpor, the passionlessness that kept her from love but also, she suspected, from hate. And so, if Madeline were ever to escape the room, which Verna thought unlikely but far from impossible, she would have to face fire and ice, and even if she had the strength of spirit to persist through those things, perhaps she would not persist unchanged. Perhaps this last-ditch effort at improving her would work.

There was, Verna thought, something more positive, truer, about the fire and ice than there was in most magic. They came from her as well as from holes.
Before finalizing the spells, Verna put the blanket holding what she had of love beneath her daughter — tied the girl to the bed with magical rope, so she would not at once kill Verna — removed what remained of the hateful enchantments from her mind — and woke her. Then she set the spell upon the door, cutting off the room, but she left one chink in the binding on the door itself, so she herself would be able to enter and exit.

“Madeline,” she said. “I am very sorry for this. Again, my best intentions have achieved the opposite of what I wanted. I have hurt you, and for all that you are as wicked as you were before. I should have found some way to teach you the goodness of what is good, even though I do not know that myself, rather than only that it is good. I have failed, and now — if you cannot be good, good must come from you. Believe at least, Madeline, that good will come from you.”
“It won’t,” Madeline muttered, fearlessly, but Verna, just as fearlessly, withdrew and put the finishing touches on the spells.

The next five years passed peacefully. She saw her parents occasionally, and even her brother Dan, who now worked as a reporter in Prima. She picked up some work tutoring. She had been in town long enough, and was seen as reliable enough, that people trusted her with their children, and this pleased her, although it frightened her as well. She was as calm and matter-of-fact with them as could be, and she was better at explaining things than she had thought she was.

A Solution

In 779, approaching the age of forty, she sold her house and moved to where Katherine was supposed to appear — luckily, a lot adjoining the land was on sale. She missed the head librarian, and, when she thought that, she realized she missed Pastor Jacobs, though all he had said now felt small for her. Purpose had made the inside of her spacious and solid.
The move went well. She placed the link to Madeline’s room in a hand mirror, with which she could gaze on her daughter or, with some effort, speak to her, and it was no problem to take the mirror with her.

Living in isolation, Verna again grew less civilized, but she did feed a pair of cats, presumably someone’s lost barn cats. She took up smoking. She drank, too. Both of these things calmed her, and were, perhaps, a reason she did not hate herself so much (she also wondered whether she had put all her self-hatred into the walls surrounding Madeline).
She began to survive on her savings, a magically augmented garden, and freelance writing. She was not the best of writers, pompous and verbose, but she understood what she was trying to write, which was more, she thought, than you could say for most such.

She now published in mathematical journals with some frequency and also began to interest herself in the academic study of magic. The academic magicians were fools, determined to use their narrow idea of the scientific method for everything, and tended to try endless variations on the various rituals they had inherited from older “magicians” to see if they worked; predictably, the vast majority did not. They’d found three that did, and all were useless. Verna realized she could make a splash if she chose. This should have been obvious to her all along, but it had been too shrouded in shame and fear even to contemplate. Now, the concept of something like greatness began to loom before her.

She thought of ways she might break the news of her magic to the world. She was afraid of what would be done with magical spells if they became public knowledge. Many were less scrupulous than she, and others would make the well-intentioned mistakes she had. Independent thought was dangerous. Still, reluctantly, she wrote and sold an anonymous book detailing the theory, though none of the specifics, of magic, and got some interest from the more open-minded scholars and a couple of oddball philosophers.

Greatness. The word had a savor she liked. As a child, she had known her genius. Now that genius could be useful to herself and to the world. She was Someone — already. Through sheer force of mind, she had accomplished things these magicians had never dreamed could be possible. All her (distressingly limited for the vehemence of her opinions) political reading began to return to her. To pursue greatness for its own sake — was a wrong thing to do. She did not trust the part of her that fantasized about Verna, the First Magician. But greatness, like all else, could be used for good. If she achieved it, perhaps a great deal of good indeed could come from her. And if it did —

At that moment, Verna realized that the perfect woman ruler she’d imagined for the Kingdom looked like Madeline.
At first she thought Madeline might be infiltrating her mind, and then she thought it was the Outlandish demons. But perhaps — perhaps, the notion took her — it was her subconscious.
Perhaps her subconscious thought — if you cannot be good, then good must come from you.
The plan that was to rule the next thirty years of Verna’s life came together in one symphonic moment, as if from without, or so deep within it was almost the same as without.

The thoughts of the perfect ruler rose up in her mind. Katherine. Katherine was, however glorious as a person, not perfectly satisfactory, in herself, as a ruler. She was kind and warm and brave — but she did not have the vision, the intellect, to serve as a politician. She had the heart every ruler should have, though. Heart, intellect, vision. The words played through Verna’s mind, and she recalled an old doctrine (suspiciously Evernostian in origin, but that did not bother her in magic, where associations of any sort could be made use of) that divided people thus — body-will-mind, body-heart-mind-soul. And so it was she decided she could take each of these from someone else.

This ruler would bring about justice in the Kingdom, and perhaps magic would allow her to get the ruler seated.
One day she went to Prima. She sent an imperious letter to the palace, demanding to speak with the King, explaining that she could do real magic. She included a demonstration of this fact with the letter: the envelope when burned, would shape the flames into the form of a bear. She asked, insistently, intelligently, and as beguilingly as she knew how, that whoever read it cast the envelope into the flames and see this.
She spent the rest of the afternoon working on the spell and then sent it off.

The blandest of form responses came back, but, undaunted, she sent another such letter, promising that, in flames, the envelope would produce the image of a giant cat. This one too received only a form letter. The third, which was to become a bird in the flames, however, someone must have tried, because she got an anxious reply — not, naturally, from the King, but from the clerk, asking what she had “really” done to achieve this effect.

She wrote back, explaining the steps, but also how spells had to change based on all the variables that went into them, and how it almost certainly would not work for him, but she repeated her desire to speak to the King. (She had considered writing to Parliament, instead, but she had heard that the royal family, while having very few practical duties, was much more responsive to the public than the career politicians.)

This time she received an invitation to Prima. She put a spell on her house to protect it while she was gone, walked some six miles to Corvel, the nearest town, and took a bus from there to Prima. She wandered lost for the rest of the evening, found a cheap hotel, and then, the next day, was nearly fifteen minutes late despite setting out an hour early for the castle. When she arrived, she presented her name and the letter to the guard at the gate of the grounds, and was soon dubiously ushered in. She apologized for her lateness, but not too profusely — she wanted to establish herself as a person of dignity.

She found herself in a parlor opposite a man she recognized from his portrait as one of the foremost scholars of magic and an utter fool, as well as by the King himself. The King was kind, warm, golden-haired, and much younger than she was.
Verna could fall a little in love with him, she realized, listening to his gentle voice explaining this and that and saying something about the poetry he’d just been reading — poetry! Verna had never met a man, or a woman, for that matter, but especially a man, who cared about poetry. He seemed unreal, a character from the sort of novel her mother read.

She was ready, she assured them, to demonstrate her magic. She merely asked that they be sworn to secrecy. She would reveal what she could do according to her discretion, not theirs. A bit reluctantly — the King with good-tempered amusement and the professor with suppressed impatience — did so.
Then she began to take her materials out of her handbag. The professor of magic examined everything very closely (it turned out he also investigated instances of the paranormal with some frequency, and always found them to be frauds.) When he nodded, Verna asked whether she might set a fire in the fireplace. Both nodded.

So she tore up bits of paper, crushed herbs onto the right scraps, turned around once, and whispered two words, and a roaring fire burst out in the grate.
She had not intended it to be so (perhaps, she thought, she had been thinking too much of the other spell — she was, for all the haughtiness she was trying to affect, quite flustered), but the fire took the form of a bird. She saw the professor register the slight look of surprise and dismay on her face as this happened. Then she clapped her hands, took a handful of dirt she’d collected on the palace grounds from her pocket, and cast it on the flaming bird. It disappeared at once.

She replicated the academy’s three magical feats, heating a glass of water (they did not have the requisite vial there) some twenty-five degrees (a thermometer had to be fetched from the kitchen); levitating a coin (it had to be a particular coin, the professor assured her, but she levitated first a penny, then a quarter, and then a nickel, allowing herself the slightest contemptuous glance at him as he explained this); and creating a small wind. She allowed herself then even more: a cutting critique of the philosophy underlying magical studies and their utter lack of theoretical grounding.

He countered, cautiously but condescendingly, that the lack of theoretical grounding was necessary. It was key to focus on what worked, because all theories as to why it worked so far had proven logically and scientifically unsound.
“Logic and science are practically unsound,” said Verna. She was beginning to enjoy herself — something of the showoff she’d never been came out now that she was not only allowed but encouraged to show off this illicit activity of hers. “So, am I tricking you all?” Verna asked.

“It remains to be seen,” he said gravely. “If you would, perhaps, give the ritual you use for these feats over for scientific investigation—”
“Won’t work,” said Verna, smugly and dogmatically. “Most spells have to be adjusted every single time for the time and the place and the person casting. It took me three weeks to understand how Alberecht’s Thermomancy remains consistent, and the others I still don’t understand — it would be a truly startling discovery, if you had any idea what it meant.”
“Can anyone do magic?” the King asked.

“It drives you a bit mad,” Verna said boldly. “But, yes. I believe anyone who performs the correct acts for that moment and for their personhood will get the right results. It is not knowledge I have any great desire to share with those who are not trustworthy. But I can write a ritual that you can perform to effect, once and once only, and only in this room, if you wish. Either of you.”
“I’ll try,” the professor said uncomfortably.
“What would you wish to do?” she asked him.

The question startled him almost out of countenance, Verna was gratified to see. “Perhaps I could flutter about the room like a butterfly,” he said sarcastically. To Verna’s slight frown, he snickered and then said pityingly, “Write me a ritual that will allow me to light the fire the way you did.”
“You can fly,” said Verna, “but it will take me time to think through. Under great stress, I can improvise effective magic, but otherwise....”

The King had another meeting, but he gave the two permission to continue their work.
So Verna worked and thought for forty minutes under the man’s eyes, occasionally asking for information about him — his birthday and any saints who had been born that day, his favorite food, and a few other items. Then she said, “Go pick yarrow. You have quite a garden, there should be yarrow in it. Throw the yarrow up in the air, catch it, and whisper a nursery rhyme, only — I suppose you need something specific, don’t you — oh, okay, whisper Peter Peter Pumpkin Eater, only substitute the word eagle for the first pumpkin. Then come in and write that and numbers I give you on a sheet of paper.”

After a moment’s evident internal struggle, he went outside to obey. He returned in some twenty minutes, looking quite embarrassed, and she had him write the rhyme and a date several months off from his birthday, but ten years ago, on the paper. Then she started her own fire magically in the fireplace, and instructed him to tear off half the sheet of paper and burn it there. He did. The other half she had him tape to his back. Then, at her insistence, he jumped up and down thrice. The third time, he floated up into the air.
The shock on his face was impressive and gratifying.

Verna smiled a rare smile. He had, apparently, followed her instructions exactly.
He laughed as he swooped around, hitting his head on the ceiling. “How long will it last?” he asked, hovering directly above her.
“Until you decide to touch the ground again,” Verna said.
“We must have you up to the University!” he declared. “I do not, of course, rule out the possibility that somehow you have hypnotized me, but it is seeming less and less likely.”

“Indeed,” Verna, suppressing an uncharacteristic giggle.
“It must be a lonely life you’ve led” he said, and he began — quite unmistakably — to flirt. He even complimented her dress. Amused to be experiencing the thing that she’d overheard some of her prettier classmates complaining about, she deflected him politely, until she realized he’d begun to peer through her bag. She took it from him sharply, and declared the meeting over.

Soon thereafter she got a letter from the King asking her to consult with Assembly delegates and from the magic professor asking her to consult with him again. She turned down the magic professor, but the King, she tended to trust. She asked if she might be reimbursed for her travel expenses and was.
At the meeting, she was declared a top-secret weapon, and was asked (rather forcefully) to move into a mansion in town. The King would pretend she was a distant relation.

Verna could not give up access to Katherine. She said she would consider his generous offer, although she did not specify that she was really considering how to go about being in two places at once. One delegate pointed out that there was still a law (considered a quaint oddity) that witches be burned, but she did not worry about this too much.

The First Magician

Finally, she found a way to split herself in two and monitor herself through a bathroom mirror, and so she assented, though she demanded a salary to match the magic professor’s. The King and Parliament, half out of fear, she thought, agreed on a larger sum even than she had asked for. In return, she was to do various kinds of magic routinely.

She made roads better. She made planes safer. She created money, because gold mines were growing depleted. She refused, after careful consideration, either to alter the minds of people — she did not think that tended to end well — or to investigate Outland. She didn’t think that it was inherently bad to learn about what lay beyond the Wall, but she did think that she in particular would be too much tempted by its sweet evil. Uneasily justifying it to herself as merely prudent, she occasionally did look into the minds and eavesdrop on the conversations of the people she worked for, a habit that told her she had, so far, navigated the situation admirably.

Some had been tempted to kill her and donate her to science. Others had been tempted to kill her, period, as they thought her too dangerous to live. Others had not intended to pay her at all.
The King was good to her, not with the politeness of fear but with the kind, pitying yet clear-sighted goodness of Pastor Jacobs. She could tell that he saw shame and loneliness as well as arrogance and competence, and she loved him a little for it. Back home, everything proceeded as usual. She had her counterpart change her name to Veronica; her brother Dan visited “Veronica” occasionally, and it turned out he’d married and had a son.

The King, too, had a child — a girl, called Emma. He asked Verna, like the fairies in the Evernost stories, to lay a good spell on Emma. She was startled, touched, and badly frightened. She begged him, first of all, to forget Evernost, not to invoke it for such purposes of good, but he quietly waved her aside. He said there was no evil in the stories, if also no power. Verna did not dare, quite, explain otherwise. Verna did her best, but she found indefinite spells challenging. Still, she agreed to wish (after long prayers) goodness and happiness on the girl. And she had been practicing so much magic with so many easily visible effects that she almost thought it might work, as her spells on Madeline had not.

Shortly after the birth, the Queen died. Verna was angry with herself for not realizing that the Queen had been in such danger and doing something to stop that — and, more deeply, because she could not shake the fear that somehow the blessing on the child had killed the mother.
She watched the girl grow up, shy, delicate, kind, and clever. Whether or not it was the spell, she certainly seemed good, though weak.

In the meantime, the Open Border movement was making Assembly and King uneasy, though (Verna thought) it did not make the King as uneasy as it ought to. She gathered two of the most vehement members of the opposition one night and proposed ways of strengthening the Wall and discouraging people from approaching it.
Verna was happy and busy in the capital. She did a great deal of good work, and she felt confident that real good was coming of her. She undertook charitable projects; she found a spell that would make nutritious food out of almost anything.

Deeply religious, one Assembly delegate was angry with the anti-censorship stance of both monarchy and parliament over the past many years. He was disgusted and frightened that Emma, the princess, was freely allowed to read Evernost stories and seemed to love them. They were afraid she would prove an Open-Border radical herself. The religious one, she found, did not trust magic, but he had been assured it was more like science than like the witchcraft of the Bible, and Verna, again guiltily, did her best to affirm that this was the case. She even almost believed it.

Still, it was to the non-religious one that she began to talk of the perfect heir, at first only in secrets and hints, and to speculate how this person would, given a strong heart, mind, and spirit along with a beautiful body, take the right position about the borders. (Verna still had the rather naive belief that a good and intelligent person was bound, or almost bound, to do the right thing from an objective standpoint, and it must be confessed that the delegate listened to her at first only to find out if she was dangerously mad.)

As she discussed the plan, she realized the King of a land was considered to be its spouse and also its body. This steeped in her mind. A well-chosen Queen with a rightly-established ritual would be bound to the land, either as spouse or as self, so if that Queen could be herself made separate from, sealed off from, Outland, the land would follow suit, more indelibly than it ever had.
She had to think about this ruler in an entirely different light. She had to think about the properties of the components. And she began to eye Emma. A component with real royal blood would help enormously.

She also thought of the composition of the Kingdom. The Kingdom, though separate from Outland, had to be of Outland as well. She began to develop a theory. The goodness of the Kingdom, as compared to Outland, stemmed from death. Theologians had said this long ago, before, in the year 0, when Dorian the Defender had tried to suppress all information about Outland. Manuscripts Verna had found in University and royal libraries said so. The evil of Outland was, accordingly, married to life. She thought this partly because Madeline did not age and did not starve. She thought this partly based on her own tentative intuitions from the days of the Golden Thing, and partly based on calculations and divinations.

But the Kingdom had life and evil as well as death and goodness. The life and evil of it must have come from Outland, and in order to correctly represent the Kingdom, the heir would have to have a component of Outland as well. Madeline might count for this, she thought, but she did not want the demon present in the perfect heir. The body of Madeline was a thing of dust. The dust, she thought, was very like death itself, for all that the demon seemed to make it incorruptible.

She resolved to catch some lesser spirit from Outland to serve as soul. She studied and studied over the next three years, trying to discover the least harmful sort of spirit. She returned to the theoretical texts about Outland and learned lists of names of supposed Outlandish spirits, some clearly copied from lists of demons, but some clearly sources of those lists. She returned to the stories, and this time found it hard not to love them passionately. Something had changed in her. They spoke to her of the innocence of childhood — and of, unfortunately, the consciousness of the Golden Thing that she had tried to edit out of herself. She wanted to love practically every supposed character instead of none of them.

As she explained a highly expurgated version of all of this, both delegates came around to her point of view, and soon a secret society formed proposing that her plan was the only salvation for the Kingdom. Panic about Outland was, of course, one part of this. Another was that Verna was in some ways more conservative than the current leaders of Parliament (she wisely did not touch on her vague ideas about the redistribution of wealth), and they imagined that an heir created by her might be easier to control and manipulate than the people they had to cope with now.

This treason saddened Verna, because she admired the King and liked Emma too, but she realized at one point that it was not only Pastor Jacobs the King reminded her of — it was the Sun. This was partly golden hair, but partly certain warmth that, though it called itself perfectly trustworthy, had proven itself just the opposite.

The Perfect Heir

At last, she chose an Evernostian being. She had not read of it; she had deduced its existence. It had power enough to make the heir immortal, in the event that Madeline’s body did not. It had little enough power, however, that it would not overwhelm the other souls Verna inserted — particularly if Verna situated them correctly in the body, with the correct links among and protections between them.

It also (and this was important to Verna) had little moral character of any kind that she could discern. She feared both the creatures called evil and those called good, for the latter had to be liars. After all, the Sun was one. This being seemed to be a joyful spirit — that much was hard to avoid; she could not find a being with no qualities whatsoever. Verna, being melancholy herself, tended to think joy a moral virtue. Certainly it would be a blessing to the new heir.

She began preparations. She enlisted her double to bind the being. She wondered at times whether she or the double were more truly she. The mirror linked her not only to the double but also to herself at different ages, or to the aspect of her that had become the double. She had not anticipated this. It felt fated (everything she did involving time travel, and a good deal that didn’t, felt fated).

She learned the reason for things she had done as a child: at ten, for instance, she had stolen a pearl necklace from her mother and buried it, at fifteen snapped photos of a meteor shower with an instant camera and locked them in a box. Her double now shoplifted a light bulb and locked it in her safe. She committed the double’s future self to putting sticky notes with padlocks drawn on them onto every source of light in the mountain house.

All of this helped, but a central spell was needed. She decided the least risky of many risky methods was to mix the life of the Sun, much though she dreaded approaching him again, with the death of the Kingdom. The latter she embodied in a handful of dirt, as she often did. She knotted a net of the two things and realized it was like — indeed, almost the same as — the gold net she had created to catch Katherine.

After a few months’ preparations, Verna performed the requisite spells at midnight — and found herself drowning in the night sky, awed by a thousand knowledges and perspectives (she fell from one to the next, haphazard, and all wove into a whole), foreign and cool and (indeed) joyous and quite unspeakably beautiful. She almost forgot her purpose, but she did not. She loosed the bindings on her target. The being expressed bewilderment, then agony, as it was torn away.

Merciless, Verna did not relax her efforts until the being occupied a single, simple form. She had expected to find herself faced with a choice of times as she had with Katherine’s soul, but this was different — Verna half-shared the being’s perspective and saw every time and place in the Kingdom through something quite other than her usual magic.

She saw, indeed, so much (afterward, she would not remember what) that she nearly gave up entirely. She remembered that Evernost was deceptive, though, and so she chose the time when Katherine had appeared and sent the being there.

She returned, exhausted, to the repurposed greenhouse the King had given her for her magic. The room spun around her, and she could not even see how to move her fingers for the first few seconds — time, in that other place, was entirely different. She slept the next two days.
After that, work continued as usual, and the friends she had made served her well, though they wondered when her plan was to come to fruition. She counseled them to patience and, when pressed, admitted that Emma was to be a part of the perfect heir, and that the right time had to come.

In the meantime, she directed them to prepare for that time by gaining what power in the Assembly they could. One of them proved quite popular, and recruited a third member to the conspiracy. In 796, when the princess was four and a half, Verna remembered that the time had come to collect Katherine’s soul. She went to the mirror on the third floor of her greenhouse and waited for her double to come to it as well.

So it was that the double left her bathroom one winter day after a long conversation with her reflection. She walked into the trees outside her house in her bathrobe, carrying a coffee mug. Quickly enough she discovered a wild rose bush.
As she had been told, one rose hip remained almost fresh. She picked it and put it into the mug. Next she went to the clearing in front of her house, held out her hands, and stared at the chilly blue sky. She had to wait, shivering and cursing occasionally, for an hour before a little seed came tumbling out of the air. She barely caught it.

It was white, spherical, shiny, and about an eighth of an inch in diameter. She put it in the mug. The last was a grass seed, a very fine one from some wild barley. When she got inside, she wrapped them up in five layers of newspaper and put them in the freezer.

The next day, for the first time in seven years, she thought of Dan, but he and his wife Raina were too old to have children. Then she recalled that he had a grown son, Dan, Jr. She accepted his Harvest invitation.
She then delved into her magical library and cut out a picture of the statue of a round figurine (a pre-Founding fertility goddess, some speculated) that had been found buried several miles outside Prima, supposedly from before Dorian the Defender. Earth, water, melons, and the color green were all associated with fertility, so she bought a cantaloupe and a box of multicolored muffin cups.

The day before she left for Dan and Jacqueline's, she made oat muffins, using the recipe her mother had given her. She tore the fertility goddess into tiny pieces and sprinkled them into the batter in the two green muffin cups, along with small squares of cantaloupe. She spat in both and wished very hard for a baby by making up rhymes about it. After fifteen minutes, she took the seeds out of the freezer and added them too, two in one and one in the other, and put the muffins in the oven.

She gave Dan and Jacqueline each one of the muffins in green wrapping, which she had saved out, a little before midnight before the solstice. They ate them, though Jacqueline whispered to Dan and would not bite hers until, blushing, he muttered something sharp. The baby was born next fall, and Ronnie’s reflection was pleased. It was a girl; they named her Jennifer Eve.

She would store the souls until the time came to implant them in Madeline. She had almost chosen to implant them at once, but for astrological reasons, the link between the Madeline-heir and the land would be stronger in ten years. Also, though she did not want to admit it, she was afraid. Afraid to kill Madeline, and, more than that, afraid to kill Emma. And she was unsure; she wanted to give herself a chance to get used to the idea (or back out of it).

Uncertain what of Madeline would remain after the implantation of the others, Verna gave her books: books, this time, of political theory, books she could absorb that would possibly give the Heir a head start. Verna also began to look for tutors for the heir and read about a great many great people of the past, wondering which skills she could instill in the Heir. The Heir would begin life as a young adult and remain, if all went well, a young adult forever, but she would have to be taught the ways of the world.

Verna did not want to fail as she had with Madeline. Her raw material was better, but her training must be too — which meant most of it must be entrusted to other people.
She began to befriend the wise and competent, sounding them about their feelings about the Assembly. She had to find people who both impressed her and might agree to her plan. It seemed a bad sign that she could think of so few. Still, she persevered.

She met her likeliest candidate when the princess was twelve. He was sixteen and extremely intelligent — almost as intelligent, Verna thought, as she herself. He was the son of a senator and had skipped grades. Awkward but well-spoken and thoughtful, he showed signs of moving into physics. Verna heard about him from his father, the senator, mostly, but began to follow his progress. She spoke first to the father and then to the son about the possibility of his learning magic.

Michael was wildly enthusiastic. Verna had him up to her tower at once. She talked — rather more than she should have, perhaps — but once he saw the results she could achieve, he was so eager to learn more that she could not resist expounding upon the bases of magic at some length. He admitted that he only understood some of what she said and was openly skeptical about her deepest point — that math and physics, though they mostly held together internally, did not actually work, and that magic was essentially exploiting the bugs in the system.

(He liked speaking in terms of computers. Verna did not like computers, but she understood enough of how they worked to appreciate his metaphors).

Michael loved to listen to her. This was one of his best features. He found her interesting. He was kind to her, not with the melting-sweet condescension of the King, which Verna loved, condescension though it was, but with a sort of half-embarrassed and half-indignant, “Well, of course.” He memorized everything she asked him to memorize with apparently little effort.
