He followed her instructions and made marvels happen himself. She began teaching him the concepts by which she chose the rituals, but this was much more difficult. Apparently she had instincts he entirely lacked in this area. She tried to help him, but it never went well.

He tried to understand her even at her most abstruse, and she found his continued failure to grasp some points somewhat comforting. He did not understand magic or take to it naturally, but, she reasoned, this was perhaps because the kind of magic she did was based not only on the peculiarities of the world but on her own peculiarities. She tried to interest him in building his own magic from the workings of his own mind, but he did not seem to know where to start — his groping efforts were clearly doomed to failure — as, for a very long time, hers had been.

Yes, he would have to find his own way someday, even though he could, on rare occasion, deduce what her way would be. She emphasized that constant effort and constant attention were key.

Time flowed rapidly now, perhaps just due to age, but she wanted to blame Evernost and the being she had caught. Emma was fifteen — the age Verna had chosen — and Michael nineteen almost before she knew it. Michael had a crush on Emma, and they were good friends. Michael had every intention of becoming Verna’s official apprentice and doing paid work of his own, though he did computer programming on the side — it seemed that he was easily gifted at this as he was not at magic. Verna felt a little jealous — she wanted her area of study to hold his mind and heart.

But magic was not (though Verna thought this less often of late) necessarily a thing to wish on anyone. She warned Michael of this several times, but never, she worried, forcefully enough. He seemed undaunted — and unscathed, but this probably was because he had not delved deeply enough into reality’s interstices.

The time had come to kill Emma. Verna, knowing how Michael felt, had a notion he should marry the Heir — he himself, intelligent and friendly and thoughtful, would be the best of tutors to her, and perhaps better at finding and recruiting others than Verna was. Verna did not work up the nerve until mid-spring, during which time she set Michael to refining the necessary gold net — that task, at least, he did easily and well, once she taught him the way of it, and even seemed, she thought, to have found an innovation or two in the method.

Her coconspirators found, at her request, a revolver and a quart of contaminated blood, the latter to keep Emma’s soul from fading.

She knew how hard it would be to get Emma’s soul into the correct time and place, but she knew also that she would succeed, because her double in the mountains had collected all three seeds, so she began hopefully.
The best way to accomplish the murder was the very slightest bit of a pocket universe — rather like the place she kept Madeline — into which none but the she, Verna, and the princess would come. This spell took several days’ preparation; she brought it off one May evening.

She waited in the now-empty palace for the princess to arrive. Within some ten minutes, Emma did, panting and white.
“Hannah!” she called out as she ran into the parlor. When she saw Verna, she stopped. Verna almost flinched, because the sight of her was clearly an unpleasant surprise. But Emma at once began to chatter at her, asking for comfort and help, begging her to help Emma find everyone else.

Emma was sweet and educated and just and kind — but she could be an idiot. It was good that Verna was pairing her with Katherine, who was not easily cowed. “Your Highness,” Verna said when Emma finished, carefully truthful, “you’re a very lucky girl.”
“Oh — what do you mean?” Emma looked into Verna’s eyes, uncertainty entering her face.

“You see—” Verna said, a bit haphazardly, “There are those who would wish...harm...to a princess like you...who could even kill her... Any fool can see there’s magic about it, of course. You had better come with me. I — that is, as you may have heard, I know a bit of magic myself, and I can keep you safe.”
Emma hesitated a few seconds and then held out her hand. It was warm, and there was a sweet fresh innocent goodness about Emma, even terrified. “Lovely girl,” Verna said. “Hype’s right, for once, my dear. You’ll do.”

“What?” This snapped Emma out of complacency, but it was too late. Verna used a spell she had used on Madeline to force physical compliance and dragged her onward. The worldlet Verna had created kept threatening to crash into the main one, but Verna held it off until they arrived in a courtyard, which was the best compromise between ritual appropriateness and seclusion Verna could find, and then brought the two worlds back together with a word and a gesture.
Emma fell to the ground when Verna released her and looked around wildly. Verna went through her purse and found the gun.

“Am — am I a hostage?” Emma asked. “I mean,” she said, shifting and tensing as if about to leap away — she was much more obvious in her movements and intentions than Madeline had been, even at five — “they’ll never pay ransom.”
Verna said something stupid to distract from the spell she was preparing, and Emma sprinted for the wall. Verna cast the spell, dragging Emma back through the air.

“Yes, run away,” Verna said murmured. “I know I talk a lot. I suppose it’s because I’m lonely. Now, sit still —” Verna ran through the ten-times table in her head and whispered a word, and Emma became paralyzed. Verna pulled the gold net from her purse. She went on, “and we’ll get this done quickly. You may hate me now, but you’ll thank me in the end. Yours will be the glory... Here it is.”

She spread the net under Emma’s face and fastened it with a barrette. At last, she shot Emma in the head. She pulled the a vial of the blood from her purse, lifted on the barrette (the net moved through Emma’s head with little resistance and did no further damage). She crunched the bag of light into the vial, and went calmly, though she felt a little dead and strange inside, to the greenhouse.

Near midnight, just as Verna had finished the first step of sending Emma’s soul to the right place and time, the messenger turned up and announced Emma’s death. “Please go on your way,” she told the messenger. “We are occupied.”
“What about your plans?” Michael asked, pale and sick-looking, clearly thinking his words would be a blow — sometimes Verna thought she was learning to love, and then others reacted to a disaster, or apparent disaster, like this, and she remained cool.

“I have no plans for the princess,” she said. Earlier that day, excitement had driven her to invite Michael up and make noises at him. Clearly she had made too many noises — and the boy, kind though he was to her, had not realized that her plans were good and well meant.
“You were talking about Emma last night,” Michael insisted.
And, with an effort, Verna was able to remember more specifically than that: she had spoken of the princess as someone who could be caught with bait. “Do you not understand what an example is?” she said.

“That was really all you meant?” Michael said.
Verna did not reply. She did not have the energy to focus on anything but her task, and she suspected an unconvincing denial would be worse than none at all.
“Sorry, but I really want to be alone right now,” Michael said.
“Understandable. Entirely understandable, given the tragedy that has befallen us. I will not retire, however, having much to do and an innately ill nature that does not grieve," Verna answered.

Michael left, and Verna carried on. Surely she could trust him to hold his peace, uncertain, until a few days from now, when Verna could produce the Heir. Verna would explain to Michael then, and he would come around. He seemed — reasonable — and had some trust in her. He might even be honored.
So she worked and worked. And then, of course, she was arrested.

Was it Michael? she too desperately wanted to know as they dragged her in to the police. She denied everything, of course, with the haughty air she had been cultivating. And she was in luck — not an hour had passed before the Prime Minister had engineered her release and vouched for her.

She spent the next few nights in the Harmon greenhouse. She told the King she was searching for the killer, and he believed her. He would have to be killed too, to make way for the Heir — ideally not by her, though she had a spell or two that could do the job if the Prime Minister’s assassination attempts failed, and she had already provided the assassin, a hired poor man who’d been promised a position in the cabinet of the future administration, with a spell of concealment. Briefly, too briefly, she felt the gut-knife of betrayal on the King’s behalf.

Dread began to gnaw at her in earnest, now. She did not know if it was from exhaustion or guilt or because she was terrified of Madeline. She thought all of these things likely.

First, she spoke with her double. The double did only the slightest of magics, and only at her command, and so she mailed the makings, half-ready, of a spell. This spell the double cast on Dan, Jr., and Jacqueline. It would confuse them into thinking they must give their blank slate of a daughter back to the one who had really created her. When the spell took effect, Verna instructed her double on how to take the souls from the daughter-thing, name of Jennie.

Souls were a different sort of stuff than spell components. This meant that Verna was all but sure they could travel through a mirror — they were information and not matter. That night the double appeared, albeit an hour late, to pass the soul-wreaths through.

A pounding at the door kept distracting the double, and the door opened. Someone or something too vague to make out came stumbling in and flopped toward the bathroom counter where the double stood ready to pass over the wreath of pearls that was the Evernostian being’s soul. The double did not seem to mind this: she was the worst of Verna — the vagueness, the madness — and she did not even seem to wonder what the thing was.

“For pity’s sake, what is that thing?”
“Nothing. Just Jennie, I mean,” the double replied, pushing the shadowy creature — Jennie indeed, Verna realized when she came into the candlelight — back a bit more forcefully.
“Aren’t you going to do something about it?” Verna asked, with something like the frustration her parents must have felt when they asked her to put on her coat when it was freezing outside.
“What is there to do?”

“What do you think? For now, something like —” She stretched her hand to the mirror, and the double met it. “This.” Verna murmured a spell to obliterate the remains of something that was already dead, because Jennie, as Verna understood the spells she’d used to create the child, was essentially the souls that had occupied her and incapable of living without them.

Her reflection obediently pushed the other two circlet-souls through the mirror, and Verna thrust them into more contaminated blood.
Now came the hard part. She had to kill Madeline, send her demon-soul to Outland where it had come from, and situate in her the three souls Verna had collected.

Verna rushed home to where she kept the other magic mirror, the one that linked to Madeline’s room. She did not like visiting Madeline.

But the circlets in the blood had lost a certain definition, indicating, she was fairly sure, that the souls within were losing strength and reality. So she steeled herself, armed herself with gun and magic, picked up the mirror, and used it to open the secret trapdoor.

Doom

She expected to find Madeline in her usual spot, as she had been when Verna had checked five years ago. But — as she found when she reached an empty stone-walled chamber that did not open on fire, ice, or Madeline’s room — she had been overconfident. She had believed that Madeline was nearly certain not to escape (especially as Madeline had, for all the time she had spent there, not tried a fraction of the things Verna herself would have tried, had she felt it was the correct course of action to try to escape in the first place), even less likely to escape the fire, and utterly sure not to escape the ice, on her own.

For Verna’s plan to be complete, she must find Madeline — doubtless growing more powerful by the second — evict the demon from her body — and replace it with Verna’s perfect ingredients for the perfect person. But that would take time, and she had no time. The souls were fading.

Where could she preserve the souls?
The answer sprang up to her from childhood: Outland! Outland itself.
Evernost, the undying land.

Evernost, poisoned beauty, evil to the root, like her daughter. Was not taking advantage of it as terrible as allowing her plan to fail? Perhaps, she reminded herself, but good was one thing she would never be. Her way was to find her way through evil to events that were good. If she was vigilant — if she was wise as she had not been as a girl, as an eccentric, as any of the things she had been in her life — if she was clever as her cleverest spell — she might prevent the evil of the place from perverting her plans.

Hubris — she knew nothing of Evernost, save that it gave her her child. As often, the decision made itself with the cold blandness of fate. She suspected all the more strongly that it too must be doomed, for when her unconscious mind took control thus it was almost always for evil rather than good.

But hope would not leave. She would contain the souls in Evernost. She would bind them with magic and with their own wills.
If they escaped —
If they escaped —
Verna did not want blood on her hands. No more than she already had. And if they escaped, they would live.
Perhaps, just perhaps, it really was better to live and be evil than die and be good.

When she had taken Katherine’s soul, she had thrust it into Evernost. Then, she had thought death necessary to cross over. Looking back, she could name the exact associations she had drawn on, the exact threads of the fabric of reality she had manipulated — and she could see how to adjust them to cross over living, as the Sun had caused her to do ever so briefly. She felt for the meanings and ideas behind what she saw, whispered a few words, clasped the bottle to her, and reached.

At the last moment, she seized a handful of dust, of death, from the roots of the Kingdom.
When she arrived, the sun nearly melted her — to what she did not know. So she cast the stuff of death over herself, blinding and deafening, rendering her insensitive, and then she cast more of it before her, to make her path safe. Instead of holding a jar with necklaces, she held three entangled — dolls, perhaps — one glowing white, one with the face and mien of the princess, and one with the red hair and (even now) glory of Verna’s defender.

Verna recognized through some dim sense that was not a sense at all a river with an island. She used the last of the stuff of death to cross the river. An island would be the perfect place to keep the souls.
By the time she reached the island, she held not dolls but three live girls, Emma, Katherine, and the Evernostian creature, who immediately grew brighter and larger and shot up into the sky.

Verna did not often cry, and never at her worst moments. She did not cry at the departure of one quarter of her plan, but she felt sicker and heavier. She crumpled in two and sank to the ground as Emma and Katherine looked at each other in wonder.

Verna began to warn them, her words as much incantation as communication. “So much — so much — I have undertaken so we can escape the danger of this place, that if one of you disobeys me, it is not only yourself that should perish but all the grand mission of which you are a part. It is necessary — necessary — that you listen and obey. Do you understand?”
They did not answer in any way, so Verna said, “Hah! You are confused. You do not know the mission? — the place? — me? — yourselves, even?”

Katherine laughed harshly.
“Indeed! Of course you are. To think I imagined otherwise — I am growing mad in my old age, no? But listen to me if you value those half-lives you still have. Do you think this a lovely land you stand in?”
The silence stretched.
“The island?” Katherine asked rudely.

Emma looked to the river and the green land beyond with a shiver of delight and nodded.
Of course. The princess had been seduced by the stories. How could she not be seduced by the thing itself? Even Verna had once been seduced. Once. “Yes, yes,” Verna said. “You love the shore and despise the island. You do not trust me — but you have no one else, and I beg you to trust this.

“Listen well: what you see beyond the boundaries of this isle, beautiful though it may seem, is poison to every right-minded person. Every sort of danger waits there and you will find nothing beautiful at its heart. This river, for instance — it may appear like cool, clear water, but really, I assure you, it is lava — or if it is not, it might as well be, for all the good it will do you.

“The dust that brought us safely to this place you might call the dust of truth. It neutralizes the terrors of this land. But, alas, I have used all of it. If you care for me and for yourselves, your hearts and souls, I mean, you will wait here to save the Kingdom from which you come —”

Verna noticed something strange. What she called holes had always bothered her, but dimly. They were nausea and horror at the edge of consciousness.
Now they would not stay at the edge. First, the earth began to tilt in opposite directions at once. Soon Verna could not make sense of anything her mind told her. Distantly, she realized that her body was — gone? No, it was not gone. It was changing, though how she could not see.

Thought began to lose its seat. There was rage at failure — then profound, pure, unthinking relief: evil defeated.
She knew no more.
